===== [6/99] chapter-06.html =====

xviiPrefaceGOALS OF THIS BOOKWhile attending a workshop or conference on structural bioinformat-ics, you may overhear tidbits of conversations that are interspersed with phrases such as phosphofructokinase regulation, singular value decompo-sition, or class instantiation. e usage of such terminology, the afore-mentioned terms arising from biochemistry, mathematics, and computer science, respectively, would not be surprising in this setting because these three areas of investigation have become the core of expertise required for the study of structural bioinformatics:• Biochemistry provides realistic knowledge about protein structure and functionality.• Mathematics helps us to build models that represent protein struc-ture and functionality.• Computation allows us to apply the mathematical models to detect patterns and make predictions about biological systems. As well, computation is necessary for the creation of visual displays that may be used to demonstrate various aspects of protein structure and function.is book addresses all three areas with an emphasis on computational techniques:• Topics in the book deal primarily with protein structure, and there are many exercises that are grounded in biological problems at the molecular level.
xviii ◾ Preface• e book encourages mathematical analysis because it provides a rm foundation for subsequent computations.• Computational techniques are covered by providing and analyzing several Python scripts that execute within the Chimera environ-ment. is supplemental material (over 140 scripts) is available from the book’s website (http://structuralbioinformatics.com), and it pro-vides solutions for the various exercises at the end of the chapters. Due attention has been given to the modularity of the scripts with the goal of providing the reader with a toolkit of Python classes that can be directly used in the reader’s own applications or used as start-ing scripts that provide base functionality via class inheritance.What are the typical computations that would be done with a script? A script working with Chimera can easily use data downloaded from the Protein Data Bank (PDB). For example, if your algorithm requires protein atomic coordinates, a script can access a PDB le relying on Chimera to parse the content and make these coordinates available as input to the algorithm. If the algorithm is modifying or generating atom coordinates, then Chimera can be used to provide a visualization of the altered mol-ecule. Moreover, Python scripts can be designed to add various graphical constructs that help to describe various aspects of protein structure and function. Visual representation of an abstraction (e.g., a plane that has some relevance to a biological construct) helps us to discover congura-tions that are made clearer with the appropriate visualization. In addition to providing visual output, changing the display can be indispensable dur-ing debugging and for nal validation of the script that implements the algorithm.SCRIPTING WITH CHIMERAis book will help any reader who wishes to use the scripting capabilities of Chimera. ere are over 60 exercises that involve the development of Python scripts. Most of these scripts are available as supplemental mate-rial (downloadable from http://structuralbioinformatics.com). ere are several approaches to the use of these scripts:• To gain experience with Python scripting in the Chimera environ-ment, the reader may choose to work through an exercise and then compare this answer with the provided script. is learning by doing 
Preface ◾ xixhelps to build condence, leading to the solution of more challeng-ing problems. Some of the exercises duplicate the calculations done in research papers. Doing such an exercise is not meant to verify or double-check the result but rather to gain experience in the solving of such problems so that the reader can tackle similar problems in the future.• In many cases, the exercises demonstrate the various applications of linear algebra. For example, eigenvectors and eigenvalues are not just theoretical mathematical abstractions, but they can be used to build visual objects that are then displayed in the Chimera window. Last but not least, scripting is fun to do. ere is a compelling sense of accomplishment when the execution of a script runs a graphical user interface (GUI) or produces an immediate visual feedback in the Chimera window.• e reader may use the script as a starting point for the development of a similar application. ere are many situations, especially dur-ing GUI development, when a working script can show the reader how some code snippet handles a problem that is dicult because it involves some exasperating implementation issue that would other-wise be resolved only aer extensive searches on the Internet.• e reader may use classes from the StructBio toolkit in the devel-opment of an application. In addition, the ability to display some structural entity related to the algorithm can help in the debugging of a complex program that involves several dicult and complicated steps. I have spent several months in the design and development of these classes with the hope that they would be useful for the future scripting eorts of both myself and the readers of this book. ese classes provide several tools that may be used for computations such as structure overlap, data plotting, scenographics, and display of resi-due networks.INTENDED AUDIENCEis book is mainly intended for people who wish to build Python scripts to extend the capabilities of Chimera. It can also be used by students, typi-cally third- or fourth-year undergraduates, who have completed intro-ductory courses in bioinformatics and wish to go further in the study of structural biology. Prerequisites include introductory linear algebra, 
xx ◾ Prefaceelementary calculus, bioinformatics (“biology 101,” sequence analysis, etc.), computer programming, and an introduction to algorithms. We assume that the reader can do elementary Python programming. An introduction to Python is not included in this book because there are sev-eral excellent sources for such information (see the references at the end of Chapter 2).BOOK CONTENTChapter 1 presents some introductory material on protein structure, and subsequent chapters cover various selected algorithms and topics that are typically seen in structural bioinformatics. e algorithms are mainly used as a source of exercises that demonstrate scripting techniques in Chimera. As such, the chosen algorithms are not meant to provide a com-prehensive coverage of the algorithms used in structural bioinformatics.Chapter 1 introduces protein structure. is is essentially a review of the material that may have been covered in the earlier biochemistry courses of a bioinformatics program. e concepts of primary, second-ary, tertiary, and quaternary structures are introduced and explained. e material serves to underscore the most salient aspects of protein structure while providing several gures that illustrate the visualization capabilities of Chimera. Exercises cover the use of Chimera menu invocations to pro-duce various types of displays related to protein structure.Chapter 2 introduces Python scripting for the Chimera environment and rapidly progresses to a description of the Chimera object hierarchy. Various objects and their attributes are covered so that the program-mer can start writing scripts dealing with molecular structure including hydrogen bonds. Some elementary le I/O is discussed so that scripts can deal with multiple PDB les. A nal exercise shows how polygonal sur-faces can be added to a display (an introduction to scenographics).Chapter 3 covers scripts that rely on the calculation of distances (most oen, interatomic distances). Exercises deal with distance shells, contact maps, inertial axes, and dehydrons. e Solids and Ellipsoid classes are introduced to provide more scenographics. Generation of contact maps introduces the reader to scripts that can display plots.Chapter 4 deals with scripts that rely on the calculation of angles (usu-ally dihedral angles). e chapter provides more material for plotting, using Ramachandran plots as an example. Lagrange optimization (intro-duced in Chapter 3 for the generation of inertial axes) is applied again 
Preface ◾ xxifor the creation and display of a least-squares plane. More examples are presented for GUI implementations using Tkinter classes.Chapter 5 covers the supporting theory and implementation of scripts to do structure overlap. is will duplicate the current functionality of Chimera, but the idea is to learn the mathematics behind that functional-ity. is has intrinsic value and, more signicantly, it gives the reader the skills to generate an overlap class that can be used in larger applications. For example, if you need to do several hundred structure alignments, then menu invocations will not be eective; you need a script that can repeat-edly call a function to do overlap.Chapter 6 is a short discussion on energy functions. Its main impor-tance is to provide a simple energy calculation for a script in Chapter 7.Chapter 7 introduces rotamers and the Chimera functions that work with them. Goldstein’s dead-end elimination is covered as a prelude to side chain packing. Scripts provide examples of more extensive GUI develop-ment, including facilities to do strip charts. Scripts to do plotting of three-dimensional (3D) surfaces are introduced.Chapter 8 covers residue networks and provides examples for the use of the Graph classes in the StructBio package. e nal exercise describes a script that provides a visual display of a network for residue contact rearrangements.Appendix A discusses the implementation of GUIs that can be used for Python applications that require a more sophisticated interaction with the user. Because the Chimera window will still be the main window of concern, these GUIs are typically modeless dialogs that set up data input for the application. e appendix gives a quick introduction to Tkinter along with several sample scripts set up as a widget buet. ese can be extracted, modied, and placed into the reader’s applications.Appendix B introduces the scenographics toolkit. is includes several classes that can be used to place various lines, surfaces, and solids into the Chimera display. An extremely versatile set of classes under the heading “Parametric Surfaces” includes classes for Frenet frame surfaces, extrusion surfaces, surfaces of revolution, and ruled surfaces. e LabelGroups class can be used to place labels into the display at specied 3D positions.Appendix C introduces the GraphBase class, which can be used to implement abstract graphs via the Vertex and Arc classes. Methods are included for the computation of shortest paths and for the extraction of various subgraphs. A set of derived classes (Graph, Node, and Edge) are provided for the construction of 3D graphs that will be displayed in


===== [7/99] chapter-07.html =====

xxii ◾ Prefacethe Chimera window. Instantiation of these classes will also generate the underlying Vertex and Arc objects so that various graph algorithms can be applied to the constructed graph.Appendix D covers various scripts to do plotting including bar charts, density plots, scatter plots, plots of parametric curves, and 3D surface plots. Plots can be shown in their own windows or within a modeless dia-log designed by the programmer.Appendix E provides a review of dynamic programming to act as background material for the sequence alignment algorithms presented in Chapter 5.


===== [8/99] chapter-08.html =====

===== [9/99] chapter-09.html =====

xxvAuthorForbes J. Burkowski earned his PhD in applied mathematics at the University of Waterloo, Ontario, in 1970. He taught computer science at the University of Manitoba (1970–1982), and during this time he also pursued studies in organic chemistry and biology. In 1982, Dr.Burkowski returned to the University of Waterloo as an associate professor in the computer science department. Over the past 7 years, he has served as the director of bioinformatics and has participated in the design of the undergraduate program in bioinformatics. He has taught the fourth-year structural bioinformatics course since 2003. Dr. Burkowski’s research interests include side chain packing, drug design, and mathematical modeling of flexibility in protein structure.

===== [10/99] chapter-10.html =====

1Chapter 1IntroductionMacromolecules and Chimera1.1 MOTIVATIONWithin a cell, life processes involve an overwhelming set of complicated interactions, most of them involving macromolecular structures such as DNA, RNA, and protein.* is nal category of macromolecules is argu-ably the most important because it has the ability to contribute the most diverse functionality due to the structural complexity of its individual members. In fact, proteins act as enzymes that facilitate chemical reactions, regulators of cell processes, signal transducers enabling signals to be imported and exported into and out of a cell, transporters of small molecules, antibodies, structural elements that bind cells together, and motor elements to facilitate movement.As such, proteins are the versatile building blocks and active mol-ecules that form the components of all living systems. Proteins always act in concert with other molecules using a wide range of chemical reac-tions and mechanical recongurations. With over a billion years of evo-lution, these complex interactions have become sophisticated enough to accomplish all the coordinated events that constitute the life processes of a living cell. ese evolutionary processes have derived structures, such as proteins, that are much more than simply static macromolecular * In this book, we assume the reader is already familiar with the Molecular Biology 101 topics such as cell structure, the Central Dogma of Molecular Biology, the genetic code, and the roles played by macromolecules such as DNA and RNA.
2 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsassemblies: their composition must ensure proper folding to a stable functioning conformation with an eventual allowance for proteolysis. Proper functioning will mean that the protein interacts with other mol-ecules in a very selective manner demonstrating a high level of molecular recognition.We study protein structure and its dynamic changes so that we can bet-ter understand the complicated functionality of macromolecules. ese studies involve tools for the visualization of macromolecular structure and the subsequent mathematical modeling of structures using geometric analysis, linear algebra formulations, and statistics.In this book, our workbench used for the study of macromolecules (in particular, proteins) will be Chimera: a soware application that has been developed by the Resource for Biocomputing, Visualization, and Informatics (RBVI)* at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF).††1.2 WHY CHIMERA?ere are several molecular modeling systems with very sophisticated visualization capabilities. e decision to go with UCSF Chimera was based on several issues many of which relate to ease of use and suitabil-ity for a student environment. e following points list some of the more important reasons for choosing Chimera: 1. Visualization capabilities As a standalone application for the visualization of macromol-ecules, Chimera has several advantages: a. Changing perspective: Easy mouse movements give rotation and zoom. b. Sophisticated graphics: Atoms can be rendered in wire-frame, stick, ball and stick, or sphere representations. Ribbons for sec-ondary structure can appear as at, edged, or rounded. Molecular surfaces can appear as solid, mesh, or dot.* http://www.cgl.ucsf.edu/chimera/ e RBVI is supported by grant NIGMS 9P41GM103311.† http://www.ucsf.edu/.
Introduction ◾ 3 c. Versatile selection abilities: e Select menu oers a variety of selection mechanisms. Selection by chain, chemistry, residue, structure, sequence, and so forth. d. Visual eects: Users can give a scene a heightened spatial presence by means of perspective and fog. Surface eects include trans-parency, and coloring for electrostatic and hydrophobic values. e. Labeling atoms and residues: A molecular scene can be aug-mented with the provision of various atom and residue labels. f. Other sophisticated features: Chimera provides visualization of density maps, supramolecular assemblies, sequence alignments, docking results, trajectories, and conformational ensembles. 2. Multiplatform Chimera is available for Microso Windows, Linux, Linux 64-bit, Apple Mac OS X, SGI IRIX, and HP Tru64 Unix. 3. Maturity e rst public release of Chimera occurred in March 2000. Since then there have been several production releases that represent a sequence of steady improvements for the soware. 4. Scalability Chimera can be used for the visualization of large molecules.* 5. Pricing Chimera is free to academic and nonprot users. 6. Computational tools More important for our purposes, Chimera is an extensible appli-cation that can be given more capabilities if the user develops Python scripts. Scripting capability allows the user to design, develop, and execute a variety of algorithms that can accept macromolecular data (e.g., atom coordinates), perform any needed calculations, and sub-sequently print results to screen or le. A script can also change the molecular scene being displayed.* Not all visualizers survive the 1FFK test; try fetching the PDB le 1FFK and render it using various display techniques.

While reading these sections, bear in mind that all the molecular visualizations have been prepared using Chimera menu invocations. If you are new to Chimera, it would be a worthwhile exercise to try and replicate some of these gures using Chimera and a protein of your own choice.1.4 OVERVIEW OF PROTEIN STRUCTUREWe review the main aspects of protein structure by considering the various levels of description: amino acid primary sequence, secondary structure, tertiary structure, and quaternary structure. To make these topics a bit 
Introduction ◾ 5 more interesting for the many readers that already understand these con-cepts, we include a bit of history.*1.4.1 Amino Acids and Primary SequenceDuring the 1800s, chemists knew about amino acids and realized that each consisted of a side chain (also called an R group) that is attached to an alpha carbon that is also bonded to amine and carboxyl functional groups, as shown in Figure 1.1.e notion that proteins were linear chains of amino acids was pro-posed in 1902 by Emil Fischer and Franz Hofmeister. Both understood that amino acid residues were joined in a condensation reaction (Figure1.2).By 1906, approximately 15 amino acids were known to be constitu-ents of proteins, but there was considerable debate about the maximum length that a protein could achieve. It was considerably later that proteins were understood to be very long chains of amino acids. In fact, lengths vary from fewer than 20 amino acids to more than 5000 amino acids. It is interesting to note that, despite the earlier discovery of the peptide chain, many scientists, Fischer included, were initially quite skeptical that proteins could have a very lengthy macromolecular structure. Even as late as 1952, Fredrick Sanger regarded the peptide theory as very likely to be * For more historical information, see [D05] for structural molecular biology and [TR01] for proteins.AminogroupSide chainRHHHOHOCCαNCarboxylgroupFIGURE 1.1 A typical amino acid.R1HNNNNHHHHHHHHHOHOHOHOOOO+ H2OHR1R2R2+FIGURE 1.2 Condensation reaction.

6 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticstrue but nonetheless only a hypothesis that had yet to be proven [F79]. e identities of all 20 amino acids were not completely known until 1940.We now understand that proteins are composed of chains of such amino acids and this chain can involve several hundred amino acids. e rst level of structural description of a protein is called the primary sequence and is simply a list of these amino acids mentioned in their order of appearance as one scans the protein starting with the amino end of the protein and proceeding to the carboxyl end.In terms of chemical functionality, each of the 20 amino acids has its own personality. In an eort to simplify descriptions of amino acid behav-ior, it is convenient to place amino acids into various categories. ere are several ways to do this. As an example, Figures 1.3 and 1.4 place resi-dues into four categories: nonpolar hydrophobic, polar uncharged, polar charged positive, and polar charged negative.All of the residues are shown in zwitterion form (an ionic molecule with separate positive and negative charges having a net charge of zero).e given categorization should be regarded as somewhat fuzzy. For example, histidine has a relatively neutral pKa and so fairly small changes in cellular pH will change its charge. So, it could be placed in the polar uncharged group, but in Figure 1.4 it is put into the polar charged group because it can achieve a positive charge under physiological conditions. Similarly, glycine oen appears in the hydrophobic category, but its side chain is essentially nonexistent, and so some biochemists will consider it to be in a category all by itself. It should be noted that hydrophobicity is of particular interest to researchers studying structural bioinformatics because it has been shown that the hydrophobic properties of amino acids contribute to the three-dimensional conformations of proteins.Some amino acids have special features that also contribute to struc-tural conformation. Proline has a peculiar structure due to the cyclic bind-ing of its three-carbon side chain to the nitrogen atom that is normally part of the amine group in the other amino acids. is binding places limits on the exibility of the backbone and so proline will oen cause a bend in structures such as helices and beta strands (to be considered in Section1.4.2). It is more likely to appear at the edges of such secondary structures or in loops that go between them. Another amino acid with important structural contributions is cysteine. It is possible for two cyste-ine residues to form a disulde bridge by means of an oxidative reaction:′→′−R-SH +SH-RR-S-S-R +2H+2e+

Introduction ◾ 9 It has been noted that small proteins tend to have more cys–cys bridges than larger proteins. An interesting example: snake venoms oen have an unusually high preponderance of cys–cys bridges to enhance stability. Venom from the Malaysian krait contains the protein 1VYC that has three such bridges even though it is only 65 residues in length (Figure 1.6).In this gure, we see that the protein uses cysteine bridges to provide more positional stability to various loop substructures that would tend to be more oppy without the bridges.e alpha carbon of all amino acids, except for glycine, constitutes a chiral center. In most biological systems this chirality is described as being in the -conguration (Figure 1.7). Amino acids with a -conguration are not found in the metabolic pathways of eukaryotic cells but have been observed in the structure and metabolism of some bacteria.*In Figure 1.7, we see that bonds attached to the alpha carbon between the NH2 and COOH groups are coming up from the page, whereas the other two bonds are going down into the page as they go from the alpha carbon to the hydrogen and beta carbon of valine. e - conguration of this amino acid would be the mirror image of what is seen in Figure1.7.* A list of D-amino acid residues in the PDB can be found at: http://chemistry.st-andrews.ac.uk/sta/jbom/group/d-res.html.FIGURE 1.6 Cysteine bridges in bucain (a krait venom).FIGURE 1.7 -conguration for an amino acid (valine).
10 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsAs a nal note to end this section, we mention that almost all life forms are restricted to the 20 amino acids listed earlier. However, recently it has been discovered that two other amino acids can appear, although rarely. ey are selenocysteine and pyrrolysine. ese unusual amino acids should not be considered as normal cysteine and lysine residues that have been chemically modied due to some posttranslational mechanism. e microbes that use these amino acids actually have specic transfer RNAs for them and they are inserted into the growing peptide chain just like any other amino acids [MG00].1.4.2 Secondary Structuree next level of structural description is referred to as secondary struc-ture. e idea behind secondary structure is to simply characterize resi-dues ofthe primary sequence, putting them into categories that reect the most obvious description of the shape of the peptide chain. ere is no attempt to establish the physical positions of residues in three-dimensional space as is done in the specication of tertiary structure. Generally there are three categories of secondary structure: alpha helices, beta strands, and loops.e rst two types of structure are mainly facilitated by the formation of hydrogen bonding between atoms in the backbone. e nitrogen atom in the backbone acts as a hydrogen donor and the carbonyl oxygen acts as a hydrogen acceptor.1.4.2.1 Alpha HelicesAlpha helices are formed when the backbone of the amino acid chain winds into a helical conformation with hydrogen bonds used to stabilize the structure. Figure 1.8 shows the formation of one such hydrogen bond. In this gure, the alpha carbon atoms have been numbered so that one can see how the acceptor and donor are related to one another. Specically, the carbonyl oxygen of residue 1 acts as the hydrogen acceptor for the nitro-gen donor in residue 5. e alpha helix is distinguished by this pattern: residue n interacting with residue n + 4 through a hydrogen bond. is arrangement gives the alpha helix 3.6 residues per turn. So, if one views the helix from a perspective that is parallel to its axis, one sees an angular increment of approximately 100° between successive alpha carbon atoms.It is also possible to have helical conformations that are more elongated. e 310 helix has a hydrogen bond between the carbonyl oxygen of residue n and the nitrogen atom in residue n + 3. is gives three residues per turn and

Introduction ◾ 11 is a type of secondary structure that occurs (rarely) in very short segments or at the ends of an alpha helix. e very rare pi helix has a hydrogen bond between residues n and n + 5 with 4.4 residues per turn. In this case, the helix is wide enough to have a hole along the axis of the structure and this is less energetically favorable when compared to the more compact alpha helix.Looking at Figure 1.8, we can pretend to grasp the helix with the right hand so that ngers are pointing in a direction that is the same as that specied by an increase in residue number. If the thumb is pointing toward the carboxyl end of the protein, then we have a right-handed helix, otherwise it is designated to be a le-handed helix. e latter is also a rare event because it forces L-amino acid side chains into an excessively close proximity with carbonyl groups.Figures 1.9 through 1.11 show an alpha helix that has been extracted from the protein 1QYS. e rst gure shows only the backbone atoms with longer roughly horizontal lines representing the hydrogen bonds. e side chains have been removed because they would certainly obscure a view of the helical nature of the structure. Even with this simplication, it is still dicult to assess the helix structure and so Figure 1.10 presents a ribbon model of the structure. Hydrogen bonding as dened by the Chimera visu-alization soware has been le intact. In many other gures to come, we will see ribbon diagrams that use a plain linguini type of ribbon to illus-trate a helix. Finally, to provide another visual representation, Figure 1.11 shows a ribbon structure with the side chains sprouting o the helix.It is interesting to note that Linus Pauling determined the structure of the protein helix in 1948. He is quoted as saying, “I took a sheet of paper and sketched the atoms with the bonds between them and then folded the paper to bend one bond at the right angle, what I thought it should be relative to the other, and kept doing this, making a helix, until I could form hydrogen bonds between one turn of the helix and the next turn of the helix, and it only took a few hours doing that to discover the alpha-helix.” Pauling realized that the Cα2Cα4Cα3Cα5Cα1FIGURE 1.8 Formation of a hydrogen bond in an alpha helix.
12 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsside chains would point outward from the protein helix.* In 1953, Pauling and Corey [PC53] published a paper that proposed a somewhat similar struc-ture for DNA based on a triple helix structure. e reader may consult a report written by Stephen Lawson (http://lpi.oregonstate.edu/ss03/triplehelix.html) to get a balanced perspective on this paper “which would turn out to be one of the most famous mistakes in 20th-century science.” In 1954, Pauling was given the Nobel Prize for Chemistry to recognize his work on molecu-lar structure. Pauling was also known for his signicant eorts on behalf of world peace and other issues related to humanitarian goals and social justice.1.4.2.2 Beta StrandsLike alpha helices, beta strands are secondary structures formed by hydrogen bonding. In this case, however, the consecutive amino acids interact with other consecutive amino acids that may be quite distant in the primary sequence. e hydrogen bonds provide a sequence of linkages * As noted by Anna Tramontano (Private communication): … the reason why Pauling could predict the alpha helix rst was because he was not trapped in thinking that the number of residues per turn had to be an integer number. is shows how common assumptions could mislead a lot of people! Introduction ◾ 13 between two chain segments, which may have one of two orientations with respect to one another as follows:• Parallel beta strands: e consecutive hydrogen bonds are such that the indices of amino acids on either end of the bonds are increasing in the same direction.• Antiparallel beta strands: e consecutive hydrogen bonds are such that the indices of amino acids on either end of the bonds are increas-ing in opposite directions.Figures 1.12 through 1.18 provide more details about beta strands.In Figure 1.12, we see the backbone atoms of three beta strands. Side chains have been removed for clarity but the carbonyl oxygen atoms have been inserted to get the hydrogen bonds. e amino acids for each strand have their indices increasing as we go from le to right. is is indicated by the amide group on the far le and the carboxyl group on the extreme right. Notice how the hydrogen bonds are formed: the carbonyl oxygen atoms being hydrogen bond acceptors while the nitrogen atoms of the adjacent strand act as hydrogen donors.Figure 1.13 presents a more simplied diagram of the same three strands each with a rounded ribbon arrow indicating the direction of increasing indices in the primary sequence.FIGURE 1.12 Hydrogen bonds in parallel beta strands.FIGURE 1.13 Ribbon diagram for parallel beta strands of Figure 1.12.
14 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsFigure 1.14 shows the same backbone atoms as those seen in Figure 1.12 but with oxygen atoms and hydrogen bonds removed. e view has been rotated so that the strands are almost behind one another and collectively they dene a beta sheet. e alpha carbon atoms have been colored black so that they are more obvious in the gure. e importance of this view is that it shows how the strands tend to rise and fall in unison. In this way, the strands form a pleated sheet (sometimes described as a corru-gated sheet). A signicant consequence of pleating is that the side chains cannot originate from an alpha carbon that is in a valley of the pleat if they are to avoid steric clashes. In other words, they will seem to sprout from the alpha carbons in such a way as to avoid the atoms on either side of the alpha carbon. Consequently, the side chains originate from the peaks of the corrugations and so they appear to line up in rows when the sheet is observed on edge. is line up eect is shown in Figure 1.15.Figure 1.15 shows the same three ribbons but with side chains present and drawn as stick structures. e scene has been rotated so as to make it more apparent that the three strands form a sheet. As just described, the side chains appear to sprout in rows that are determined by the pleats of the sheet and because of the nature of the pleats, these rows of resi-dues alternate above and below the sheet. e ribbons are drawn in such a way as to eliminate the pleated structure of the sheet but the atness of FIGURE 1.14 Side view of the previous ribbon diagram showing pleated conformation.FIGURE 1.15 Ribbon diagram with side chains for parallel beta strands of Figure1.12.
Introduction ◾ 15 the ribbons brings out another signicant observation: the sheet is not perfectly at; it has a slight twist. is twist is not a peculiarity of this protein*; rather, it is typical of many beta sheets.Let us now consider a set of beta strands that have an antiparallel con-formation. All of the gures for this discussion have been prepared by viewing an antiparallel beta sheet within the protein 1QYS. As before, the beta strands make up a sheet but now they run in directions that alternate as illustrated in Figure 1.16. e hydrogen bonds that stabilize this sheet are shown in Figure 1.17. e reader should compare the appearance of these hydrogen bonds with that of Figure 1.12. In general, the successive hydrogen bonds that join two beta strands in an antiparallel conguration tend to be more uniform in their direction. ey do not exhibit the slightly slanted appearance of hydrogen bonds in a parallel beta strand sheet as seen in Figure 1.12. e antiparallel construction of the sheet illustrated in Figure 1.17 has a pleated structure that is very similar to that seen in beta strands that make up parallel sheets. Loops are chains of amino acids that have no particular hydrogen bond-ing patterns with other parts of the protein. ere are proteins that con-sist of nothing more than a loop stabilized by disulde bridges. A good example of this is avoridin (PDB code 1FVL) which is a blood coagula-tion inhibitor from the habu snake. Another example is (PDB code 1ANS) a neurotoxin from the sea anemone, Anemonia sulcata. is protein has three disulde bridges and while it does not contain any helices or beta sheets it does have seven beta turns.FIGURE 1.18 Ribbon diagram with side chains for antiparallel beta strands of Figure 1.17.
Introduction ◾ 17 In Figure 1.19, the loop is portrayed as a ribbon diagram, whereas the disulde bridges have been drawn in ball and stick form.Proteins that are all loop are fairly rare. Most proteins will have beta sheets and helices forming a hydrophobic core and these secondary struc-tures will be interconnected by loop segments. An example of this is given in Figure 1.20, where we see short loops connecting the adjacent strands of an antiparallel beta sheet within the Escherichia coli ribonuclease 1V74. is is the simplest way to connect such strands; it is possible to nd other more complicated interconnections for adjacent strands involving helices and other beta strands.For a sheet made of parallel beta strands, the adjacent strands may be con-nected with simple longer loops. However, in most cases, one will nd that helices and other beta strands are used to connect adjacent parallel strands.1.4.3 Tertiary Structure1.4.3.1 What Is Tertiary Structure?e tertiary structure of a protein species the positional relationships of all secondary structures. For example, the tertiary structure will show how the beta strands and helices combine to form the hydrophobic core of a globular protein. Interconnecting loops also play an important role FIGURE 1.19 Loop structure and stabilizing disulde bridges of the neurotoxin 1ANS.FIGURE 1.20 Loops connecting adjacent beta strands in an antiparallel sheet.
18 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsand their placement is critical in various applications. Tertiary structure is essentially established once all the three-dimensional coordinates of the atoms are determined.In Section 1.4.4, we will see that tertiary structures can combine to form larger quaternary structures. However, many proteins are single peptide chains and can be described by specifying the tertiary structure of that single chain. If we want to describe a protein’s functionality from a biological perspective, then starting with the tertiary structure of the protein is a minimal requirement.Because proteins interact with other molecules, they will have bind-ing sites that must be organized in a way that facilitates some interac-tion while avoiding any other reactions that might produce harmful side eects. is is the most signicant raison d’être of tertiary structure but there are other issues as well. To illustrate this, Section 1.4.3.2 provides a quick sketch of the myoglobin binding site and aer that we cover some other topics related to tertiary structure.1.4.3.2 Tertiary Structure of Myoglobine three-dimensional structure of myoglobin provides an interesting example of a tertiary structure. It has a biological functionality that has been extensively studied and it also has the distinction of being the rst protein structure to be determined by x-ray analysis. is was reported by Kendrew et al. in 1958 [KB58]. e structure of the myoglobin molecule was not deposited into the PDB until later because the PDB was not established until 1971. Myoglobin appeared there in 1973 with identifying code 1MBN.Figure 1.21 provides a view of the entire molecule. It has 153 residues that form eight helices interspersed with short loops. ese helices are com-bined into a special arrangement called a globin fold. It consists of a hydro-phobic pocket that contains a structure called a prosthetic heme group (Figure 1.22). e heme group for this gure was extracted from the PDB le for oxymyoglobin (PDB code 1MBO), which has a molecule of oxygen hovering over the iron atom at the center of the heme group. e function of myoglobin is to carry oxygen in muscle tissue. More specically, it stores oxygen and carries it to the mitochondria for oxidative phosphorylation. For x-ray analysis, both proteins (1MBN and 1MBO) were extracted from the muscle of sperm whale (Physeter catodon), a mammal that has this protein in great abundance because of the necessity to stay under water for long periods of time. As any structural biologist can tell you, the myoglobin proteins and hemoglobin proteins share similar structures and functional-ity. Hemoglobin genes have been in the biosphere for at least 1.8 billion 
Introduction ◾ 19 years and may have originated as proteins that were meant to sequester oxygen thus protecting the cell from harmful eects of too much oxygen produced by the photosynthetic processes [SG99].At the center of the heme structure is an iron atom that is coordinated by the nitrogen atoms of the four pyrrole rings. In normal myoglobin the iron atom is ionized to the Fe2+ (ferrous) state. e heme group is not sym-metric and contains two highly polar propionate side chains that appear on the right side of Figure 1.22. A close inspection of Figure 1.21 will reveal that these carboxylic acid side chains (ionized at physiological pH) avoid the interior of the hydrophobic pocket. is is also shown with more clarity in Figure 1.23. Introduction ◾ 21 are have darker shades of gray. e dashed arrow indicates the position of the iron atom. If you look carefully, you can see H93 just below and to the le of the iron atom. e main point of this diagram is to see the several hydrophobic residues that surround the underside of the heme binding pocket. It should be noted that these nonpolar residues protect the Fe2+ from oxidation to the Fe3+ (hematin) state, which will not bind O2.Figure 1.25 shows the placement of the iron atom relative to the two histi-dine residues mentioned earlier. Coordination of the heme iron atom is done by the NE2 atom of H93 (called the proximal histidine) and the NE2 of H64 (called the distal histidine). e distal histidine crowds in on the binding space and causes the oxygen molecule to bind at an angle. is makes the bond to the iron atom weaker and so O2 is more easily removed when needed.1.4.3.3 Tertiary Structure beyond the Binding PocketPerhaps the most remarkable features of the molecule are its com-plexity and its lack of symmetry. e arrangement seems to be almost totally lacking in the kind of regularities which one instinc-tively anticipates, and it is more complicated than has been pre-dicted by any theory of protein structure.J. C. Kendrew et al. describing myoglobin, 1958Biologists must constantly keep in mind that what they see was not designed, but rather evolved.Francis Crick, 1988FIGURE 1.25 Heme group in pocket—cutaway view.
22 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsIn Section 1.4.3.2, we have seen that myoglobin tertiary structure provides a binding site that not only holds the heme group with its nonpolar residues but also has a particular geometry that manages the storage of O2 in a very precise fashion. It is fairly clear that all the residues near the binding site par-ticipate in the support of these activities. However, there are many other resi-dues that are considerably further away from the binding site. It is reasonable to ask about the participation of these residues in the primary functionality just described. Are they really needed? To add more signicance to the ques-tion, it should be noted that the protein cytochrome B562 from E. coli is only 106 residues in length and it also has a binding site with a heme group. To be fair, it should be noted that the B562 binding site lacks the critically positioned distal histidine seen in myoglobin and so the functionality is somewhat sim-pler. However, it is possible to nd other proteins that seem to be quite huge in comparison to their rather small binding sites.It is perhaps this type of additional complexity that prompted Kendrew and his associates to express the statement quoted at the start of this section. is quote was taken from the Nature paper [KB58] and it gives us some per-spective on the mindset of the early explorers venturing into the unknown territory of protein structure. e anticipated regularities of protein struc-ture seem to be reminiscent of the earlier expectations of Copernicus and his assumption that planetary orbits would be circular. In line with the admonition made by Francis Crick [C88] in the preceding quote, our cur-rent thinking provides a somewhat dierent perspective on the structure of the myoglobin molecule: It is the result of an evolutionary process driven by functional requirements with less attention paid to elegant design.At the macroscopic level, we see many examples of this function-driven evolution with problematic design principles. For example, breathing and food ingestion share a common tract, much as reproductive systems and urine elimination share the urethra in male mammals. ere are draw-backs to this tissue sharing: breathing will be accompanied by choking if food is taken into the trachea and in the case of shared reproductive/urinary systems, we can consider the unfortunate psychological associa-tions due to such an arrangement. Even though tissue sharing provides a kind of time-sharing eciency, this type of tissue parsimony does not always occur in the evolutionary process: everyone is born with a vestigial appendix that is not needed and men have nipples.At the molecular level, we may also see redundant substructures in a protein simply because evolutionary processes have maintained them as legacy contributions inherited from an earlier protein (see [KD99] in which 
Introduction ◾ 23 the authors entertain the idea that a particular folding topology may reect a vestigial structural remnant of an ancestral SH2-containing protein).Even though there is the possibility that various helices or strands might simply constitute vestigial excess baggage, there are other more signicant reasons to have extensive and very complicated structure in a protein.e functionality of tertiary structure relates to the following issues: 1. Formation and management of the binding site e residues (usually hydrophobic) create a pocket that promotes the docking of the protein with a ligand molecule. As just discussed in the myoglobin example, the positions of particular atoms in the binding site may be critical for the optimal functioning of the reac-tion. ere is also the necessity of forming a binding site that does not combine with other ligands in a way that produces a deleterious eect. Protein evolution can do only so much in this regard and may not protect a cell against toxins that are relatively new to the organ-ism. For example, hemoglobin will avidly take on carbon monoxide in lieu of oxygen molecules with disastrous results. 2. Flexibility ere are situations in which a protein interacts with a ligand or another protein and this interaction is accompanied by a change in protein conformation that facilitates the reaction with yet another molecule. Allosteric proteins demonstrate this functionality and the overall change in the protein’s conformation may involve residues that are quite distant from the binding site that initiates the change. 3. Control of a correct folding pathway In some sense, the folding of a protein involves many of the forces that contribute to the formation of a binding site. However, folding is functionality that must be accomplished before the binding site can be established. As such, the tertiary structure must have a level of orga-nization that will ensure the correct fold. It has been experimentally observed that various peptide sequences will simply not fold. For those that do fold, it may be necessary to have a primary sequence with key residues that foster particular intermediate transitory folds that act as transitional conformations. Presumably, these are necessary to guide the folding process so that the nal conformation is indeed the lowest energy conformation. e reader may consult [S05] for an opinion that stresses the importance of folding pathways and [D03] for a discussion 
24 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsabout transition state conformations that act as a quality control mech-anism in protein folding. A discussion of ultrafast and downhill protein folding may be found in [D07].* Models for folding and misfolding can be found in [D06]. Another aspect of the correct folding of proteins deals with cotranslational folding. is involves the assumption that proteins start to take on signicant aspects of their tertiary structure during translation when they are still attached to the ribosome. Such an assumption also has implications about the folding pathway ([FB97] and [K01]). It should be stressed that there is still considerable con-troversy about the various phases of the folding process (e.g., [LB98]). Much of this should be settled as more data becomes available. 4. Stability of the protein Aer the completion of folding, a complex interaction of the resi-dues must ensure that the nal structure will be maintained without the possibility of a dierent conformation being formed. In the ideal situation, a protein folds to a tertiary structure that is characterized as the lowest energy conformation and it stays in this conformation except for any exible changes that are necessary for its functional-ity. As described earlier, disulde bridging enhances the preservation of conformation. In less than ideal situations, it is possible that the protein undergoes a refolding that produces a dierent conformation. e endogenous cellular prion protein PrPC that can undergo a con-formational change to become the scrapie isoform PrPSc gives a good example of such metastable behavior. e PrPC conformation con-tains 43% alpha helix and no beta sheets, whereas the PrPSc form con-tains 20% alpha helix and 34% beta sheet. Unfortunately, PrPSc has a tendency to form aggregates in cerebral tissue and existing proteolytic mechanisms do not clear these aggregates. With unchecked accumu-lation, neurological damage occurs, ultimately leading to death.ese points emphasize that tertiary structure is necessarily complex because it must do a lot more than simply provide a foundation for the binding site. e additional responsibilities of tertiary structure include the support of delicate mechanisms that foster the proper behavior of the protein over its entire life cycle. is includes its creation by means of a guided folding pathway, the stable maintenance of its conformation and binding site, and its nal susceptibility to a degradation process that will lead to the reuse of its amino acids.* Current Opinion in Structural Biology, 17(Issue 1) has several papers devoted to protein folding issues.
Introduction ◾ 25 Meeting all of these requirements is a complex optimization prob-lem with multiple objectives. Furthermore, it is usually quite dicult to explain the functional signicance of any one amino acid in terms of its contribution to one or more of the four issues just described. e proximal and distal histidine residues in myoglobin, various other residues that act as secondary structure breakers, and particular residues that form hairpin structures [IA05] are exceptions to this statement, but in the general case, a particular residue serves along with several other amino acids to dene the various functional aspects of the tertiary structure.Despite the possible lack of regularity in a tertiary structure, the fold appearance of the structure may be replicated in another protein even though its primary sequence is reasonably dierent. In Figure 1.26 we can see an obvious similarity between myoglobin and a single chain within hemoglobin, which is also an oxygen carrying protein. is structural similarity is present even though the primary sequences have only 29% of their primary sequences matching identically. As noted in [OM97]: “At 30% sequence identity, proteins will almost certainly have the same over-all fold.” is is especially signicant for the globin family where similar folds have been seen for even lower sequence identities (less than 15%). e alignment (Figure 1.27) was done using the TCoee server [PO03]. is example underscores the observation that tertiary structure is more conserved than sequence in an evolutionary descent.Myoglobin (1MBN) One chain of hemoglobin (1JEB)FIGURE 1.26 Structural comparison of myoglobin and a single chain of hemoglobin.FIGURE 1.27 Sequence comparison of myoglobin and a single chain of hemoglobin.

26 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsEven though there are several amino acids that can be changed while still maintaining the functionality of the globin fold, there is a critical location at which a mutation can produce a signicant change in func-tional behavior. e reader may note that the sixth residue of 1JEB is “E” for glutamic acid. Sickle-cell anemia is an inherited disease that leads to single mutational change in which this glutamic acid is replaced by valine. is produces a small hydrophobic patch on the surface of the protein. Unfortunately, this patch is able to bind with a hydrophobic pocket in the deoxygenated conformation of another hemoglobin molecule. is sticki-ness of the hemoglobin proteins causes a clumping that leads to distortion of the red blood cell, which then takes on a sickle-cell appearance. ese blood cells have a shorter life span than normal cells and so the patient has anemia caused by low levels of hemoglobin. Sickle cells can also become trapped in small blood vessels preventing blood ow.As a nal note on tertiary structure, Figure 1.28 is presented to illus-trate a protein that does have a remarkable amount of regular structure. is is from a human placental ribonuclease inhibitor that binds the blood vessel-inducing protein human angiogenin [PS97]. e angiogenin mol-ecule is presented to show the extraordinary repetition of alpha helix and beta sheet structures that combine to form the large horseshoe tertiary structure. Although there is an extensive beta sheet present, it does not have the same twist that is oen seen for smaller sheets. e beta strands have collected into a cylindrical formation that almost goes full circle. e regularity of their positions seems to be coordinated by the hair roller placement of the helices in the structure.FIGURE 1.28 A tertiary structure involving repeated secondary structures (chain D of 1A4Y).
Introduction ◾ 27 Many other remarkable folds can be seen in [OM97] and especially [PR04]. Space limits the presentation of these diagrams but the interested reader may fetch and display the ribbon diagrams for the more picturesque titles: β Barrel (2POR), β 7 Propeller (2BBK chain H), β 2 Solenoid (1TSP), and αβ 4-Layer Sandwich (2DNJ chain A). For most of these examples, the regularities in the structure are best seen by using a protein visualizer that allows the viewer to freely rotate the structures.1.4.4 Quaternary StructureA protein has quaternary structure when it is an assembly of multiple ter-tiary structures. We refer to such structures as oligomeric complexes and we describe these structures using labels that start with a prex represent-ing the number of subunits followed by the sux mer. e progression is monomer, dimer, trimer, tetramer, pentamer, and so forth. If the subunits are dierent, then an additional hetero prex is used, as in heterodimer. If the subunits are the same, then the homo prex is used, as in homotet-ramer. From an eciency point of view, it is in the best interest of a cell to build complex structures from smaller building blocks that are all the same. Each unit will be prescribed by the same DNA sequence and so there is a parsimonious genetic representation of the full oligomeric structure.In some cases, the quaternary structure can show a low level of recur-sive assembly. For example, three heterodimers may be assembled to form a trimer of dimers. A beautiful example of this construction is the insulin protein illustrated in Figure 1.32 later in the chapter. e assembly of this hexamer is a bit complicated, so we will explain it as a series of steps start-ing with the monomer illustrated in Figure 1.29. is is a single molecule made up of two peptide chains that are joined together using two disulde bridges. ere is a third disulde bridge, but it connects two cysteine resi-dues within the same chain. To see how the three disulde bridges inter-connect the residues in the three chains, consider Figure 1.30.Two of these monomers make up the insulin dimer by having their beta strands combine in an antiparallel fashion to make a small beta sheet. is is illustrated in Figure 1.31, where the A and B chains appear in the background with C and D chains in the foreground. Chains C and D correspond to chains A and B in the dimer assembly, and they have the same type of disulde con-nectivity. In Figure 1.31, all disulde bridges have been removed for clarity.Figure 1.32 shows the full insulin hexamer made up of three dimers each one of the same construction as that shown in Figure 1.31. e positions of the three dimers are coordinated by means of two zinc atoms. To see how the zinc atoms are related to a dimer, they have been included in Figure 1.31.

Introduction ◾ 29 To understand how the dimers combine to make a hexamer, imagine taking the dimer of Figure 1.31 and tilting the entire assembly so that your line of view is such that the darker zinc atom is exactly behind the brighter zinc atom. is will put the dimer into the exact orientation that the upper third of the hexamer shows in Figure 1.32. Notice that in this orientation, the beta sheets are seen on edge and so they are somewhat dicult to discern.ere are many other examples of quaternary structure, some of which we will see later.1.4.5 Protein FunctionalityAs already stated, protein structure facilitates protein functionality. Even though this book does not consider functionality in any signicant depth, we now present a very high level overview of the various processes and structural elements that use proteins.e study of protein function includes the following:• Enzymatic catalysis• Almost all chemical reactions in a cell are facilitated by a cata-lytic mechanism involving a protein.• Rate of reactions is accelerated, sometimes by as much as a factor of 1017.FIGURE 1.32 Hexamer structure of insulin (PDB code 1ZNJ).
30 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics• Enzymes may use any of the following strategies: − Provide an alternative reaction route. − Stabilize reaction intermediates. − Facilitate alignment of reacting groups.• Signal transmission• Information can be moved from one part of a biological system to another. is is done, for example, by neurotransmitters, hor-mones, and cytokines. − Cytokines act as signals in immune systems and during embryogenesis. − ey typically bind to a cell-surface receptor and this initi-ates a signal cascade resulting in an upregulation or down-regulation of gene expression.• Regulation• Protein–protein interactions (PPI): PPI may enable or inhibit one of the proteins involved in an interaction.• Transcription regulation: Proteins may assemble to form tran-scription factors that regulate gene expression in DNA.• Immune system• e immunoglobulins are examples of proteins that play a cen-tral role in the defense mechanisms of the body. ey recognize foreign molecules and help the body to destroy viruses and bac-teria. Other components of the immune system include major histocompatibility complexes and various receptor proteins.• Molecular transport• Some membrane proteins are capable of moving small molecules into or out of a cell. For example, the Na+/glucose transporter is membrane protein that brings in both sodium ions and glucose molecules with sodium ions moving down their concentration gradient while the glucose molecules are pumped up their con-centration gradients.


===== [16/99] chapter-16.html =====

Introduction ◾ 31 • Motor elements• Changes in protein conformation produce mechanical move-ment, be it large muscle movement or the small agellar motors of a bacterium.• Structural systems• Proteins act as the building blocks of living systems.• In large complex systems, they contribute to the formation of large structures such as bone, hair (collagen), nails (keratin), and many other tissues.• Smaller structures include strands of silk, viral coats, and frame-works for the movement of molecules within cells.1.4.6 Protein DomainsWe have already seen the progression of structural organization: primary sequence, secondary structure, tertiary structure, and quaternary struc-ture. e protein domain is another structural concept that is important in the discussion of protein functionality. Although the denition of a domain varies somewhat in protein research papers, here is a reasonable denition:A domain is a region of a protein that has more interactions within itself than with the rest of the chain.ese interactions include the formation of a hydrophobic core. Indeed, it has been observed that protein domains can oen fold independently. e observation of independent folding is due to experiments that have shown that the subsequence of amino acids in a domain can oen fold into the nal conformation even when the rest of the protein is absent.e following points provide more information about the formation of domains:• Domains can be structurally similar even though they are in pro-teins that are otherwise quite dierent. Such domains usually show a high sequence similarity.• Domains tend to be compact and globular. Linkages between domains are oen loop structures and hardly ever helices or beta strands.
32 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics• Domains have distinct solvent accessible surfaces and are typically separated by water molecules.• Residues within a domain will contact other residues in the same domain. ere is very little contact, if any, between residues in dif-ferent domains.• Usually, a domain is formed from a contiguous residue sequence of the protein. In rare cases, a domain is made up of two or more regions of protein that are subsequences from one or more polypep-tide chains. is is why expert visual inspection is oen required to determine domains.Multiple domains within a single protein oen cooperate to accom-plish related tasks. For example, reverse transcriptase protein of HIV has various domains that work together to convert viral RNA to DNA followed by destruction of the viral RNA. So, by studying the domain structure of a protein we can strive to understand how design principles facilitate particular types of functionality. Although this is an important goal, the mechanisms of cooperative function are not fully understood in many cases.Figure 1.33 presents a simple example of the 6PAX protein that uses two domains to interact with a segment of DNA. is protein is part of a family called the homeodomain proteins. ey interact with DNA to provide transcription regulation and they are important in development and cell dierentiation. When the 6PAX protein interacts with DNA, the event initiates a cascade of protein interactions that lead to the develop-ment of various eye structures. In the gure it is clear that the protein has two hydrophobic cores each responsible for interacting with a par-ticular DNA segment. e two cores are linked by a rather lengthy loop structure.FIGURE 1.33 e two domains of 6PAX interact with DNA.

Cyclotides Essay assignment: Cyclotides are peptides that have a cyclic backbone. ey usually have short primary sequences and oen fold into loop structures that are stabilized by cysteine bridges. Heme anity for carbon monoxide Essay assignment: It has been observed that the heme prosthetic group shows an anity for CO that is up to 100,000 times higher than the anity for O2 in an unhindered model [JK95]. However, a distal histidine can provide some steric hindrance (discussed in [OM88]). It should be noted that the geometry of the Fe–C–O con-guration has been debated. For example, Lim et al. 39Chapter 2Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera2.1 MOTIVATIONPython scripting with Chimera allows the user to design, develop, and execute a variety of algorithms that can work with macromolecular data (e.g., the atom coordinates of these molecules), perform any needed cal-culations, and subsequently print results to the IDLE Shell window or to a le. A script can also change the molecular scene being displayed.As described earlier, Chimera provides the user with a wide range of capabilities that can be invoked using menus and command lines. In many situations, these high-level capabilities are sucient for many of the standard tasks that need to be done by the structural biologist. So, why would we need to work with the lower level computations provided by Python scripting? is is an important question because Python script-ing requires a time consuming commitment to develop skills in object-oriented programming.e following points give three reasons for such an investment of your time: 1. Specialized applicationsIn many situations you may need to develop your own special-ized applications that are not achievable by using current menus and 
40 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticscommand lines. For example, you might want to develop a dierent approach for the visualization of some molecular feature or molecu-lar functionality. In general, the ability to do scripting will reduce your dependency on canned programs and thus avoid the con-straints they may impose. 2. Standard procedures might need some customized modicationsIt might be the case that Chimera does provide the basic capabil-ity, but you simply need to extend this functionality to meet some extra project requirements. For example, Chimera does provide structure comparison tools but structure comparison is very compli-cated and there are several dierent criteria for measuring structural similarity. Your application goals might require a dierent approach to the problem. 3. Calculations that are repeated across several instancesVarious applications involve data collection to gather statistical information about macromolecules such as RNA or proteins. is might involve the processing of thousands of instances of side chains or even the processing of thousands of les. For these situations, simple menu invocations will not suce and you will need a script that can automatically si through the extensive data.2.2 PYTHON SKILLSis book will assume an understanding of object-oriented program-ming concepts. Although it is not necessary to be an expert Pythonista, you should be equipped with a basic level of Python programming skills. If you are new to Python, there are several freely available tutorials on the Internet. You can also purchase introductory books on Python, some with an emphasis on bioinformatics. A list of these sources is given in the Reference section. Fortunately, Python is very user friendly and is typically regarded as an easy language to learn. is is especially true if you already have some experience with an object-oriented language such as C++ or Java. Suggested references ([B10], [B09], [HM00], and [K09]) and website links can be found at the end of the chapter. You should have a working knowledge of the following concepts and topics before proceeding:Numbers: integer and oating point arithmeticStringsCollectives: lists, tuples, dictionaries, and sets
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 41 Conditionals: if statements, else statementse range() functionLooping techniques: for statements and while statementsOther control statements: break, continueDening functions: including default arguments and keyword argumentsInput and Output: including formatting and reading/writing of lesClasses: understanding instances, methods, attributes, and inheritanceA novice Python programmer may nd it dicult to follow the mean-ing and intention of a script and so I have tried to provide extra comments as the need arises. Application functionality has been expressed through function denitions and class denitions when object-oriented encapsula-tion was considered to be achievable. To make the scripts more compre-hensible, variable names have oen been given suxes as follows:_A Array_B Boolean_D Dictionary_L List_M Matrix_P Point_S Set_T TupleSo a variable such as hydrophobes_L is a list of objects and we would expect it to be used with methods such as append, count, or extend.2.3 PYTHON SCRIPTINGPython is part of the Chimera download. To get a Python Shell you can invoke the menu item Tools… General Controls… IDLE. You might nd it extremely helpful to set up an IDLE button on your Toolbar. is will allow you to get the Python Shell with a simple button click, thus avoiding the menu descent. To set up this, invoke the menu item Favorites… Add to Favorites/Toolbar…. You will be presented with a dialog box that can be used to put an IDLE button on your Toolbar.
42 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsConsidering the material that will be covered in the rest of this book, it is advisable to set up three more buttons in the Toolbar: the Model Panel, Side View, and Sequence buttons. Aer you complete these preferences, the top-le corner of your Chimera window should look something like that in Figure 2.1.e Python Shell has multiple uses as follows:• You can test syntax or execution of short Python scripts.• You can use the Shell window to get output results from a script that is in execution.• You can open an editing window that may be used to generate a new Python script or to modify a recently produced script.• You can also provide input to a running script.Let us discuss each of these in turn. You can consider the Shell win-dow to be a sophisticated calculator. Type in an expression or small code sequence and it can be immediately executed. e triple “>>>” is a command prompt that awaits input of Python statements. For example, consider the following statements:>>> x = 3.0>>> x**3 + 5.32.0>>>e variable x has been set to 3.0 and the next statement computes the value of an expression that uses x. Note that the result is given on a line without the prompt.FIGURE 2.1 Recommended toolbar buttons for Chimera.
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 43 Let us try something with more relevance to structural bioinfor-matics. To get a protein from the Protein Data Bank (PDB), we can execute:ourProtein = chimera.openModels.open('1AY7', type = 'PDB')[0]If you execute this script line, Chimera will fetch the 1AY7 PDB le fromthe RCSB and it will be displayed in the Chimera application window. We will deal with the details of this statement in Section 2.4. Incidentally, it is possible to delete the model holding the protein data and, as a result, clear the contents of this window by invoking the menu item File… Close Session in the Chimera window.Suppose we want to set up this code snippet as a script in a le. We would go through the following steps:• Use the File… New Window… menu item to get a new edit window.• Type in lines:import chimeraourProtein = chimera.openModels.open('1AY7', type = 'PDB')[0]• Use the File… Save As… menu item to get a Save As dialog. Give the file a relevant name with a “.py” suffix, for example, myTestProg.py. The file can be saved in some directory, which is appropriate for these initial test cases. Now, the edit window can be closed with the assurance that the script has been stored in a.py file.• Later, aer launching Chimera and getting a Python Shell win-dow, we can invoke the menu item File… Open… to open an edit window containing the contents of this le. By invoking Run… Run Module we can execute the script and it will bring in the 1AX7protein.So, that was our rst Python script. It was a very short program, but it did have the power to fetch data from the PDB and display it on the screen. If we wanted to fetch a PDB le from a local directory, we would simply replace the PDB ID string with a string that contained the pathname of the target PDB le.

Aer that we can get back to other functions that can be used to extract data from a PDB le.2.4 CHIMERA OBJECT HIERARCHYPython is an object-oriented programming language that supports the use of data structures and their associated methods. By starting our scripts with the statementimport chimera
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 45 we immediately gain access to all the objects that are available within Chimera. For example, the Chimera object openModels will track the molecular models that have been extracted from a le that has been opened. For example,model_L = chimera.openModels.open('1AY7', type = 'PDB')will set up a list of models that we have designated as model_L. e list contains only one model because that is all there in this PDB le. is can be veried by looking at the Model Panel (brought to your screen by the menu invocation Tools… General Controls… Model Panel or by simply clicking on the Model Panel button if you have that set up on your Toolbar). For example, fetching the le 1ZNF will give you 37models. Look at the Model Panel to verify this. Any one of these mod-els can be accessed by using an index in the range of integers running from 0 to 36 inclusive.Chimera takes full advantage of Python’s object-oriented provisions by creating an object hierarchy that reects the hierarchy of constituents in chemical structures. For example, a protein is a molecule that would have a list of residues, atoms, or bonds. is hierarchy is illustrated in Figure 2.2.Each of the objects just mentioned (chimera.Molecule, chimera.Residue, chimera.Atom, chimera.Bond) has a set of methods. An important aspect of object-oriented programming is that computations dealing with an object are constrained and must use one or more of the methods that are encapsulated with the object. In Figure 2.2, each box contains a set of attributes for the object named in the header of the box. For the sake of simplicity, many of the attributes have been le out. To view a full list for a molecule object, type the following line in the Python Shell:>>> ourProtein = chimera.openModels.open('1AY7', type= 'PDB')[0]

48 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics2.5 ATTRIBUTES FOR MOLECULE OBJECTSAs described at the end of Section 2.4, we are assuming that qorProt is a molecule object. Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 49 resObj = qorProt.findResidue(0)while resObj: print resObj.type resObj = qorProt.residueAfter(resObj)If you execute this code in the Python Shell, you will notice that nal “residues” in the list are all “HOH.” If the programmer wants a residue list that is free of water, then the code should be modied to avoid the “HOH” entries.To get the name of the molecule, we can use:qorProt.nameIn this case, the Python Shell would return the character string “1qor.”Finally, to delete the model we can use:chimera.openModels.close([qorProt])is removes the molecule object and clears it from the display.Chimera does not explicitly support a Chain class. 50 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsIf you happen to know the chain identifiers for the chains (not always “A,” “B,” …) you can get the sequence by using the sequence() function:seqA = qorProt.sequence('A')To get a list of chain identiers, you can demand that the sequences() function provides its results as a dictionary and then you may ask for the keys to the dictionary:chainId_L = qorProt.sequences(asDict = True).keys()2.6.2 Identication of a SequenceIf the sequence was extracted from the list of sequences, then you might not have the chain identifier. This is available with the chain attribute:>>> seqA.chainu'A'ere are various other attributes that will provide more information describing the sequence:>>> seqA.fullName()u'1qor (#0) chain A'>>> seqA.molecule.nameu'1qor'>>> seqA.nameu'chain A'2.6.3 Sequence EntriesTo get the single letter residue types that are in the sequence, we can work with the sequence attribute:>>> seqA.sequence['M', 'A', 'T', 'R', …., 'L', 'I', 'P']To conserve space on this page, most of the sequence entries have been replaced with “….”
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 51 2.6.4 Getting Residue Objects from a SequenceFinally, we can get a list of the residue objects within the chain by using:seqA.residuesYou have to exercise a bit of care here. e length of this list is the same as the length of the sequence but some of the residue entries may be Python “NoneType” objects. Consider the following code snippet that accesses the protein with ID = “2FIF,” builds a dictionary of sequences and then attempts to put all the alpha carbon atoms of chain “A” into an atom list:alphaCarbs_L = []prot = chimera.openModels.open(“2FIF”, type=“PDB”)[0]chains_D = prot.sequences(asDict = True)for r in chains_D['A'].residues: alphaCarbs_L.append(r.findAtom(‘CA’))e snippet produces the following error display in the Python Shell window:Traceback (most recent call last): File “<pyshell#30>”, line 2, in <module> alphaCarbs_L.append(r.findAtom('CA'))AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'findAtom'Run the same code again with a slight change in the last line: if r: alphaCarbs_L.append(r.findAtom(‘CA’))Now the code works. If you use the menu invocation: Favorites… Sequence and then show the sequence for chain “A,” you can see why this happens. e sequence ends with the residues …RLRGG. However the last three entries (RGG) do not have structure information. e PDB le says that the protein has these amino acids in the sequence, but the x-ray analysis of the protein does not give coordinate information for the nal three residues in the chain. e change in the code snippet uses an if statement to check whether the residue object actually exists before attempting to access any of its structural attributes.
52 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics2.6.5 Residue Maps for Sequencese attribute resmap is a dictionary that maps residue objects to a zero-based sequence position. Consider the following Shell interactions:>>> resAtIx2 = qorProt.findResidue(2)>>> resAtIx2.typeu'ARG'>>> seqA.resMap[resAtIx2]3>>> seqA.sequence[3]'R'You might see this as a curious inconsistency because there are twodierent indices associated with the same object. e PDB le for 1QOR states that the residue sequence starts with MATRIEF… (see Figure 2.4b). However, residues corresponding to the 3D structure (com-plete with all atomic coordinates) start with ATRIEF… (see Figure 2.4a). e findResidue(2) expression is nding the residue with index 2 in the 3D structure as displayed. is is zero-based indexing, so ALA is at index 0, THR is at index 1, and ARG is at index 2.So, resAtIx2, the residue object at index 2 is ARG. e resMap attri-bute is reporting that this residue object is at index 3 in the sequence. is is rearmed by the last expression that assures us that seqA.sequence[3] is indeed “R” (the single letter specication for ARG). is is also zero-based indexing. Of course, zero-based indexing is what programmers expect. A biologist looking at the sequence will want to see the number-ing start at 1. is is what we see in Figure 2.4b. In this situation the ARG residue has position 4. is can be determined in a script by using the id.position attribute:>>> resAtIx2.id.position4A2.AR4.AT3.A(a) (b)FIGURE 2.4 (a) Labels on the rst three. (b) Start of sequence for 1QOR.
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 53 So, we have the cautionary note: Be careful! Depending on the context, a residue can be located using any one of three dierent numbers. In our running example with 1QOR, the ARG residue is located using 2,3,or 4.ese issues have implications for the execution of code that is work-ing with the residues attribute of a sequence object. You might not get a residue object if the index corresponds to a residue that is miss-ing from the 3D structure. is is illustrated in the following Shell interactions:>>> seqA.residues[1].typeu'ALA'>>> seqA.residues[0].typeTraceback (most recent call last): File “<pyshell#5>”, line 1, in <module> seqA.residues[0].typeAttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'type'>>>In this last example, we have only one residue missing from the struc-tural display, namely, the methionine at the beginning of chain A. It is also possible to have missing structural information within a chain. If you inspect the sequence for 3ZY7, you will see that the residues NTNP have been outlined in a red rectangle to indicate that they are missing from the 3D representation of the structure. In the display, the missing backbone is represented by a straight dashed line.
