54 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics2.7.2 Identication and Characterization of Residuese following Python Shell interactions show the results for various use-ful attributes of a residue object:>>> resObj.typeu'ARG'>>> resObj.isHelixFalse>>> resObj.isSheetTrue>>> resObj.isStrandTruee previous three statements produce Boolean values that specify the type of secondary structure containing this residue. To avoid the delays that occur when a script must access these 
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 65 les from the PDB, it is convenient to have the data les stored on your own disk. By accessing all the les and putting them on your drive, these delays are encountered once and not during the repeated execution of your script (as might happen when you are debugging your script).Script 2.2 will read a simple text le containing the PDB ID’s of several proteins. en it will use Chimera to fetch the les and then store them into the directory containing the script. e text input has a simple for-mat, for example,Myoglobins1a6m Sperm whale1mba Slug sea hare1mbs Common seal1mwc Pig1ymb Horse2mm1 Human1myt Yellow n tunae rst line is to explain the signicance of the list and will be ignored by the script. 66 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics elif hasattr(e, 'code'): print 'Error code: ', e.code else: pdbFileContents = fileHandle.read() fileHandle.close() outFile = file(pdbID[0:4] + '.pdb', 'w') outFile.write(pdbFileContents) outFile.close() pdbID = listFileHandle.readline() # Go for next pdbID in the listlistFileHandle.close() # Close the list file.Script 2.2: Getting a Batch of Files from the Protein Data BankNote the use of the tkFileDialog.askopenfilename() method to launch a dialog window that will allow the user to specify the pathname of the le containing the PDB ID list. We will not discuss the details of the script because it is reasonably straightforward. Instead, we will describe the typical use of HiLiter. e class can be accessed by doing the following import:from StructBio.Utilities.hiLite import HiLiterSuppose that prot is a molecular object that has been previously dened. e statementhiLiter = HiLiter(prot, “white”)
68 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticscreates the object hiLiter as an instance of the HiLiter class. e ini-tialization routine for the class will cause the molecule to be displayed in the wire representation and the background will be colored white. If this second argument is omitted, the default color is black. Aer the object is created, we can use the emphasize method to highlight various atoms in the molecule:hiLiter.emphasize(primaryList, secondaryList)e primaryList is a list of atom objects that will have their repre-sentation changed from wire to ball & stick. e secondaryList is a list of atom objects that will have their representation changed from wire to stick. All atoms not in these lists go back to the wire representation if not already present in that representation. Figure 2.7 (produced by the script for Exercise 11) shows the typical appearance of these changes. In this gure, the larger ball is an alpha carbon that was the single entry in the primary list, while the two smaller spheres (ball & stick representation) are the car-bon and nitrogen atoms that were the two entries in the secondary list.e function clearEm ph asis() can be used to bring the entire scene back to a wire representation without any emphasis. e function call setVerbose(True) can be used to put HiLiter instance in verbose mode. In this mode, the Python Shell window will get a print out of all atom names in both primary and secondary lists whenever the emphasis changes.e use of HiLiter during debugging and testing will help you to settle questions such as: “Did my last computation correctly identify the atoms that my algorithm needs for further computation?” Putting the empha-size method in the body of a loop that is subject to a stepping interaction (such as waiting for a user keystroke) generates an animation that helps you understand the behavior of the program.FIGURE 2.7 Example of a HiLiter instance putting emphasis on three atoms.

Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 69 2.12 HBONDDICTS CLASSIn a protein, it is possible for bond lengths and bond angles to change but typically with very small alterations. Protein exibility is mainly achieved by changes in the phi and psi dihedral angles. However, these changes are not arbitrary and are subject to various constraints mostly present due to the existence of hydrogen bonds. For example, beta strands tend to stay in sheet structures that are held in particular formations by the existence of hydrogen bonds.e StructBio package, available from the book’s website, contains a class called HBondDicts that is useful for the creation of dictionaries that describe the hydrogen bonds in a protein. Use the following to access the class:from StructBio.HBonds import HBondDictse interested reader can view the source code to get more details about its operation. Instead of a line-by-line analysis of the program, we will provide a higher level description of both its activities and the dictionaries that have been provided for user applications.When an HBondDicts object is instantiated, the __ini __ method will execute the findHBonds method in the FindHBond class that is part of the Chimera installation. is will generate a list of tuples that specify all the hydrogen bonds. e initialization method then builds the hBond_D dictionary to be described in Section 2.12.1. Some private sup-port functions such as __SScode and __categoryCode are used to help in the construction of these dictionaries.2.12.1 Using the HBondDicts ClassAer fetching a protein using a statement such as:prot = chimera.openModels.open(protID, type=“PDB”)[0]e protein object can be passed to the class __init __ function by instantiating an HBondDicts object, for example,hBds = HBondDicts(prot)is instantiation builds all the required dictionaries for fur-ther use in the program. Generation of hydrogen bonds is actually a 
70 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticscomplicated procedure that involves several issues. If you pull down the Chimera menu item Tools… Structure Analysis… FindHBond you will get a dialog window that shows several of the options that are available in the creation of hydrogen bond representations. In the HBondDicts initialization just shown, default values are used for the geometric constraints placed on the bond. e denition line for the __init__ function isdef __init__(self, prot, dist_Slop = 0.4, angle_Slop = 20.0):so, you can use constraints that are dierent from the default values if desired. Going along with our example, let us describe the dictionaries that would be available for the hBds object:• e dictionary hBds.hBond_D provides a mapping from donor atoms to acceptor atoms. e keys for the dictionary are atom objects representing the donor atoms (usually a subset of the nitro-gen atoms in the protein backbone). e dictionary value corre-sponding to a key is a list of acceptor atoms with each atom having a single hydrogen bond going to the donor atom specied by the key. Usually, a list will have only one entry, but occasionally one may encounter a situation involving more than one acceptor for a single donor.• e dictionary hBondSameChain_D provides some descriptive information about the hydrogen bonds. Keys for this dictionary are (donor, acceptor) tuples. Each entry in the tuple is an atom object. Naturally, each tuple species a hydrogen bond and the value cor-responding to a key is True if and only if the donor and acceptor are in the same chain.• e dictionary hBondCategory_D provides more informa-tion about the hydrogen bond. e keys are the same as for the hBondSameChain_D dictionary. e value for a key is a string that holds a code describing the signicance of the bond:• “HE”: e donor and acceptor atoms are both in the same helical secondary structure.• “PL”: e donor and acceptor atoms are in beta strands that are running in parallel.
Accessing and Displaying Molecular Data with Chimera ◾ 71 • “AP”: e donor and acceptor atoms are in antiparallel beta strands.• “NA”: “None of the above,” this code is a catchall for any other H-bond scenario.See Exercises 13 and 15 for problems related to this class.2.13 EXERCISES 1. ===== [25/99] chapter-25.html =====

74 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticse interesting observation here is that the (x, y) coordinates of all seven atoms are almost the same. In fact, the average x coor-dinate has a value of 155.335 and the average y coordinate has a value of 155.330. Allowing for a small amount of noise in the (x,y) coordinates, we see that the potassium atoms are all arranged along a line that is parallel to the z axis. Consider what happens if the 3D position of every atom in a molecule is modied by sub-tracting (155.335, 155.330, 0.0) from the coordinates. Note that the z coordinate does not change. Each potassium atom will end up on the z axis.To get the four models, you should do the following: a. Generate four models each a copy of 1K4C. Consider the models to be indexed as 0, 1, 2, and 3. b. Move the atoms in all molecules so that the potassium atoms line up on the z axis. c. Rotate model 1 about the z axis through an angle that is equal to 90°, rotate model 2 about the z axis through an angle that is equal to 180°, and nally, rotate model 3 about the z axis through an angle that is equal to 270°.Relevant notes: a. e rotation does not require any sophisticated use of matri-ces or trigonometric calculations. Chimera has a function called xform that provides an easy way to do translation and rotation, but you are not allowed to use this approach. Instead, you should note that because the angles are all mul-tiples of 90°, there is a very simple relationship between the initial coordinates of an atom and the nal coordinates aer such a rotation operation. Your script should work with the actual coordinates to achieve such a rotation. Why avoid xform if it is available? e goal of this question is for you to learn scripting techniques that deal with the access of atomic coordinates and their use in subsequent calculations. ese techniques will be very important for structural calculations in later exercises.

B-factors and exibilitye B-factor of an atom is linearly related to the mean square displacement of an atom from its average position. Various research papers have used the B-factor of an alpha carbon atom as a rst approximation for the assessment of the exibility of the protein backbone at that position: a larger B-factor indicates more exibility. Note that bfactor is an attribute for an atom in the Chimera object model.

Introducing the Surfaces classe next exercise makes use of the Surfaces class (see AppendixB) that can be used to display various surfaces in your molecular scene. For this exercise, you should experiment with the add Polygon function that can be used to display convex polygons. Each polygon is specied by an ordered list of vertex coordinates. A polygon is drawn as a set of triangles. Each triangle is comprised of three vertices: two consecutive vertices in the polygon vertex list and the centroid of all vertices in the list. Note that the vertices do not have to be coplanar, but in most applications they will be either coplanar or fairly close to being coplanar.e class can be instantiated with an empty argument list, for example,pDisp = Surfaces()Aer this, we can bring polygons into the display by adding them to the instance, for example,pDisp.addPolygon(vertices_L, polyColor, transparency) Here, vertices_L is a list of numpy arrays specifying the 3D coor-dinates of the vertices of a polygon in the display space. e color is designated by polyColour. is is a three component tuple speci-fying the color in RGB format. e transparency parameter is a value between 0.0 and 1.0 specifying the transparency of the surface.pDisp.display()For example, running the next script will produce the two poly-gons in Figure 2.10.

Drawing H-Bond PlatesA worthwhile objective in structural bioinformatics is to represent complicated structures with simplied visual components. e clas-sic ribbon diagram is an example of this goal. In this exercise, we will generate rectangular plates that bridge neighboring beta strands. e visual objective is to get a beta sheet to look like a sheet. In Section 3.7, we will provide a more compelling argument for the construction of FIGURE 2.10 Displaying two polygons.
82 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsH-bond plates. is visualization is especially useful for larger pro-teins that have the beta meandering motif, for example, PDB ID = 1FJ1.To give these plates some biological relevance, we will stipulate the following rules: a. Each plate corresponds to a single hydrogen bond involving a backbone nitrogen donor and its associated oxygen acceptor. Both donor and acceptor atoms must be in a beta strand. e strands need not be in the same chain. b. e four vertices of a plate will be determined by the alpha carbon atoms on either side of the donor and acceptor pair. c. Each of the short sides of a plate will have vertices that are consecutive alpha carbon atoms and in the same chain.For example, Figure 2.11 shows three strands in a parallel beta sheet. e hydrogen bonds are represented by thin gray lines. In this gure, the four sided plate outlined with dashes corresponds to the hydro-gen bond donor labeled with Ni where the subscript i denotes the index for the residue containing that atom. e short sides of the plate are dened by the pairs (CAj, CAj+1) and (CAi–1, CAi). e nal rule stipulating that each member of this pair must be in the same chain is necessary to avoid peculiar situations near the start and end residues of a chain. Although the pairs can be in dierent chains, we want to avoid the undesired situation in which a short side jumps from one chain to another.Ci–2Ni–1CAi–1CAj+1Nj+1CAjNj+2Cj+1Cj–1CAiCi–1CiNiOjNjCjFIGURE 2.11 Atom numbering for parallel strands.

87Chapter 3Algorithms Dealing with Distance3.1 MOTIVATIONDistance measurements are fundamental operations in the analysis of molecular structure. Most of these measurements are done to com-pute interatomic distances that are part of a more extensive analysis such as studies dealing with protein flexibility, docking of ligands, or evaluation of energy functions. All these measurements assume that the coordinates of the atoms are accessible. For example, coor-dinates would be available when a Protein Data Bank (PDB) file has been accessed and processed. This chapter also considers other cal-culations dealing with atomic coordinates such as the computation of the centroid of a collection of atoms. More esoteric computations would involve the perpendicular distance between an atom and a plane determined by other atoms in the system.3.2 CALCULATING INTERATOMIC DISTANCESere are several ways to calculate interatomic distances. One can use the following menu invocation: Tools… Structure Analysis… Distances. is will launch the Structure Measurements dialog window, and one can then designate atom pairs for the distance calculation. Since our emphasis is on Python scripts, we cover the techniques that allow one to calculate distances that can be used for further calculations in a script. All distance 
88 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticscalculations will be based directly or indirectly on the familiar Euclidean formula given by Equation 3.1: ,xx yyzz222dabababab()()()()=−+− +− (3.1)where atom a has coordinates (ax, ay, az) and atom b has coordinates (bx, by, bz).To illustrate the various techniques, we repeat some of the distance cal-culations that were done by Watenpaugh and others [WS80] in their study of rubredoxin (PDB identier: 4RXN). Rubredoxin is a metalloprotein containing a single iron atom coordinated by four cysteine sulfur atoms. In this beautiful arrangement, the cysteine residues act like the prong set-ting of a diamond ring holding the iron atom at the center of a tetrahedral structure (Figure 3.1).Calculations done by Watenpaugh and others [WS80] included the computation of distances between the iron atom and each of the four coordinating sulfur atoms. As a running example, we pose the following simple question: what is the interatomic distance between the iron atom and the sulfur atom of CYS 6 in 4RXN?We start with the following code snippet, which derives the coordinates of the two atoms:p = chimera.openModels.open(“4RXN”, type = “PDB”)[0]for r in p.residues: if r.type == “FE”: feCoords = r.findAtom(“FE”).coord() if r.id.position == 6: sgCoords = r.findAtom(“SG”).coord()FIGURE 3.1 An iron atom held by cysteine residues in rubredoxin (Protein Data Bank identier: 4RXN).
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 89 is script nds the target atoms, and for each atom the atomic coordi-nates are accessed as an attribute of the corresponding atom object. Note that the coordinates are returned by the coord() function as a Point object. With the Point objects stored in the variables feCoords and sgCoords, we can compute the interatomic distances using a straightfor-ward application of Equation 3.1. is formula uses the individual compo-nents of the coordinates of the atoms. In our case, ax, ay, and az correspond to feCoords[0], feCoords[1], and feCoords[2], whereas bx, by, and bz correspond to sgCoords[0], sgCoords[1], and sgCoords[2], so calcula-tion of the distance becomess = 0.0for i in range(3): s += (feCoords[i] - sgCoords[i])**2distance = s**0.5Experienced Python programmers will typically avoid loops when-ever possible. To get a more concise implementation of the distance func-tion, we note that some calculations can be done on Point objects. For example, subtracting a Point object from another Point object will pro-duce a c h i m e r a.Ve c t or object. So the previous four lines of code can be replaced by the following single line:distance = ((feCoords - sgCoords)*(feCoords - sgCoords))**0.5where the “*” operation is computing the inner product of the two vectors.Another approach is to use the norm function within the numerical Python module. Aer importing the linalg module (which may be needed for other calculations as well),import numpyfrom numpy import linalgwe can derive the distance using the following simple calculation:distance = linalg.norm(feCoords-sgCoords)We should also note that Chimera has a distance function for coordi-nate objects:distance = feCoords.distance(sgCoords)
90 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsIn all four cases, the value of the distance will be 2.333. is is consis-tent with the observations made in [WS80].3.3 APPLICATIONSDistance calculations are oen the rst steps performed in more extensive calculations, for example, the evaluation of potential energy between two atoms that share some sort of bond. For example, the hydrogen bonds discussed in Chapter 2 will have a bond strength that is dependent on interatomic distance. Chimera works with this dependency when it has to determine whether a hydrogen bond has enough signicance to be displayed. e threshold for this decision can be altered by the user: the menu invocation Tools… Structure Analysis… FindHBond will launch a dialog window that allows you to relax these distance constraints by an amount of your choosing.Many algorithms in structural bioinformatics rely on the collection of statistical information related to interatomic distances. For example, Zhang and others [ZL04] use a database of collected statistical informa-tion to derive a distance-dependent, pairwise statistical potential uijr(),, for the atom type pair specied by indices (i, j). ey refer to this as the distance-scaled nite ideal-gas reference state energy function. eir anal-ysis uses the inverse-Boltzmann formula: uijr RTNijrNijrobs()()()=−,, In,,,,exp (3.2)where R is the gas constant, T is the temperature, Nijr(),,obs is the observed number of atom type pairs (i, j) within the distance shell specied by the range rr−∆ 2 to rr+∆ 2 in the database, and Nijr(),,exp is the expected number of atom type pairs (i, j) in the same distance shell if there are no interactions between the atoms.In building such a database, it would be very useful to have an algo-rithm that can rapidly determine the atoms that are contained within a given distance shell. is algorithm is the topic of Section 3.4.3.4 RAPID DETERMINATION OF ATOM MEMBERSHIP IN SHELLSFor this application, we are given a radius value (maxR) and we wish to construct a data structure that will facilitate rapid access of nearby atoms. Specically, when a center atom is specied, the data structure should help 
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 91 us to generate a list of all atoms that are within maxR of the center atom. A slight modication of the algorithm will allow us to avoid nearby atoms that are closer than a radius value (minR).e algorithm is to be implemented as a Python class called Shell and stored in a module called shellNeighbors. e instantiation of the Shell object will set up a data structure that is suitable for shells with arbitrary centers but with prespecied radii (minR and maxR). In practice, maxR will be some small oating point value such as 5.0. To get the neigh-boring atoms within this distance shell centered on a particular atom, we will invoke a Shell method called getAtomsInShell. More precisely,Input for Shell object initialization:A list of atom objectsTwo nonnegative numbers minR and maxR, with minR < maxRe Shell class should have a method called getAtomsInShell:Input for getAtomsInShell():An atom object acting as the center atomOutput for getAtomsInShell(): A list of atoms such that the distance between any atom in the list and the center atom is a value that falls in the range dened by the interval [minR, maxR]e initialization of the Shell object should execute in O(n) time, wheren is the number of atoms in the list passed to the __init__ function of the Shell object. e execution time for the getAtomsInShell query function should be O(1), that is, constant time. is requirement will demand a strategy that is much faster than the straightforward approach that involves a simple scan of all the other atoms in the list, this strategy taking O(n) time to satisfy the query.3.4.1 Implementation Strategye algorithm will behave as if the atoms in the atom list are contained in a rectangular three-dimensional (3D) bounding box. To be more specic, we want the smallest bounding box with sides having lengths that are


===== [29/99] chapter-29.html =====

92 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsexact multiples of maxR. We will consider the inside of the box to be a 3D array of cubes, each cube having a length, width, and depth equal to maxR. Each cube in the box will have 3D integer grid coordinates (cx, cy, cz) that identify the cube’s position within the bounding box. Figure3.2 shows the front of the bounding box as a red grid, the bottom of the bounding box as a blue grid, and the le side of the box as a green grid. e other three sides of the box are not shown, and (as we will see) they are not needed in the computations.ese three sides will be determined by the minimum values of the atomic coordinates. ey can be computed using a small function that is private for the Shell class:# Function to compute the three minimum values of # the coordinate entries.def __minValsOfCoords(self): # Initialize to infinite values: xMin = yMin = zMin = float(“inf”) for atm in self.atoms_L: cc = atm.coord() if cc[0] < xMin: xMin = cc[0] if cc[1] < yMin: yMin = cc[1] if cc[2] < zMin: zMin = cc[2] return [xMin, yMin, zMin]Cube with integer coordinates (0,0,0)FIGURE 3.2 (See color insert.) ree sides of the bounding box.
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 93 Having these minimum values and knowing the dimensions of a cube, we can compute the integer coordinates of a cube containing an atom when we are given the coordinates of the atom:# Function to convert 3D coordinates into # grid coordinates.def __convCoordsToGridCoords(self, coords): fV = floor((numpy.array(coords) - self.minVals)/self.maxR) return (int(fV[0]), int(fV[1]), int(fV[2]))How are we going to use the grid coordinates of the atoms? A possibility is to have them act as indexes for a huge 3D array, each entry of the array containing a list of protein atoms that are in the cube with these grid coordi-nates. ere is a big drawback to this scheme: it would require a lot of space and many of the entries would be empty because the protein typically does not intersect all the cubes in the bounding box. Instead, consider a code that builds a dictionary. is dictionary will map grid coordinates to lists of atoms. More specically, a dictionary key will be a 3-tuple of integers representing the grid coordinates of a cube inside the bounding box. e dictionary contents associated with a key will be a list of atoms that can be found in that cube.e dictionary can be built as part of the initialization for the Shell object:self.gridDict = {}for atm in self.atoms_L: # Generate integer coordinates for the cube # containing this atom. gridCoords = self.__convCoordsToGridCoords(numpy.array(atm. coord())) if not self.gridDict.has_key(gridCoords): self.gridDict[gridCoords] = [] self.gridDict[gridCoords].append(atm)How do we use the dictionary? If you have a query atom object, you can easily compute a near neighbor list specifying all the atoms that are located within the distance maxR from the query atom. is can be done in the fol-lowing way: rst convert the 3D coordinates of the query atom to its grid coordinates. is will designate a central cube that is surrounded by 26 other cubes having grid coordinates that are easily computed. Central cubes near the sides of the 3D grid will need special consideration. We can use grid-Dict to getall atoms in the 3 × 3 × 3 set of cubes. Some of these atoms will be within the distance maxR of the query atom and others will be beyond this threshold, so we will have to do a nal check to verify that an atom is 
94 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticswithin threshold. We can also eliminate any neighboring atoms that are beyond the minR constraint. If the distance between an atom and the query atom is less than or equal to maxR, it is put into the return list. e return list should not include the query atom. Note that the amount of computation that is done to handle a query is not related to the total number of atoms because (realistically) there is a small upper bound on the number of atoms that can be found in a set of 27 cubes. Consequently, a query runs in con-stant time. We have just described the operation of the following function:def getAtomsInShell(self, centerAtom): # This function accepts an atom and returns a list # of all atoms that are within the shell surrounding the centerAtom. # The shell is specified by minimum radius minR and # maximum radius maxR. atomsInShell_L = [] # Generate integer coordinates for the cube containing # the center atom. gridCoords = self.__convCoordsToGridCoords(centerAtom.coord()) xI = gridCoords[0] yI = gridCoords[1] zI = gridCoords[2] # Inspect the 27 cubes to get the atoms that are within maxR. for i in range(xI - 1, xI + 2): for j in range(yI - 1, yI +2): for k in range(zI - 1, zI + 2): coordTuple = (i, j, k) if self.gridDict.has_key(coordTuple): nbrList = self.gridDict[coordTuple] for atm in nbrList: interAtomDistance = \ numpy.linalg.norm(centerAtom.coord() - atm. coord()) if interAtomDistance <= self.maxR: # Do not include the center atom. if interAtomDistance < 0.01: continue # Eliminate atoms closer than the # minimum radius. if interAtomDistance < self.minR: continue atomsInShell_L.append(atm) return atomsInShell_Le Shell class is in the StructBio package and can be imported usingfrom StructBio.Utilities.shellNeighbors import Shell
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 95 e module will prove to be useful for various other applications in the remainder of this book.3.5 CONTACT MAPS FOR PROTEINSIf we have the coordinates of all the atoms in a protein, then we can compute a distance matrix. is is a square symmetric matrix of cells that are indexed by the residues of the protein being studied. In simple versions of a distance matrix, the cell D[i, j] at row i and column j is the distance between alpha carbon [i] and alpha carbon [j]. Depending on the requirements of the application, other denitions may be used, for example, working with the beta carbons in lieu of the alpha carbons with the alpha carbon atoms being used only for glycine. Another possi-bility is to measure distances between the centers of mass of the various side chains.A contact map is essentially a visual discretization of the distance matrix. A cell is colored black if the distance between alpha carbon [i] and alpha carbon [j] is less than some particular threshold (say, 10 Å); other-wise, it is le white. Note that cell D[i, j] will have the same coloration as cell D[j, i], and so the matrix is symmetric.Contact maps provide a reduced two-dimensional (2D) representation of a protein and have the advantage of being invariant to rotations and translations of the coordinates. Contact maps may be used to describe similarity between protein structures. An example of a contact map is shown in Figure 3.3.e protein used here is chain A of a bovine Rabex-5 fragment cocrys-tallized with ubiquitin (PDB identier: 2FIF). It was chosen because it is a small protein that has a good selection of secondary structural ele-ments: helices and a beta sheet made up of both parallel and antiparallel strands.Note that row numbering starts at the top and increases as you go down to the bottom of the gure. is is the simplest type of contact map with only two levels of distance information. In Figure 3.4, we show the top half of the contact map, but this is using ve levels of distance infor-mation. As values from the distance matrix are being mapped to a range of gray levels, we will refer to this type of representation as a distance map. By comparing Figures 3.3 and 3.4, you can see the similarities of the main structural elements with Figure 3.4 giving a ner representation of the same data.
96 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics0Contact plot for 2ﬁf102030405060700 10 20 30 40 50 60 70FIGURE 3.3 Contact map for chain A of 2FIF.FIGURE 3.4 (See color insert.) Distance map for chain A of 2FIF.

Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 97 Below the top half of the distance map, there is the strung out version of 2FIF chain A with the Chimera visualization of the protein in the lower le corner. e secondary structure elements (SSEs) have been labeled as A, B, C, …, H. Notice the contributions made by these secondary struc-tures in the distance map. Dark areas in the map correspond to secondary structure pairs that are near to each other. e areas labeled as AB, EF, and EH represent antiparallel beta strands. e area labeled as BC is caused by the B strand coming near the C helix. Note that this is also seen as an antiparallel interaction, and if you follow the direction of increasing alpha carbon indices in the helix you will see why this is so. ere is a sudden backbone turn between helix C and helix D to the extent that D comes up against C, thus producing the darker area in the distance map labeled as CD. As seen in the structure image, strands A and H run parallel to one another and this is seen in the map as area AH, which runs parallel to the main diagonal but some distance away from it. Finally, it should be noted that the helices C, D, and G produce dark areas along the diagonal, a typical distance map signature for a helical structure where residues are interact-ing with other nearby neighboring residues. Below the diagonal, we have inserted the primary sequence of the protein along with the correspond-ing residue positions ranging from 1 to 73 inclusive.3.5.1 Script for Generating Contact and Distance MapsSuppose we have a 2D array of numbers with each number in the range [0, 1]. We wish to display a 2D dot plot of these numbers subject to the fol-lowing rule: if the matrix number at row i and column j is between 0 and 0.5, then the corresponding dot plot cell [i, j] is colored black; otherwise, it is colored white. e following function will draw a 2D plot that follows this rule:import matplotlib.pyplot as plotfrom matplotlib.colors import ListedColormapdef generate2Dplot(zG_A, plotTitle): # [0,.5) black; [.5, 1.] white: cmap = ListedColormap([‘#000000’, ‘#FFFFFF’]) n = int(zG_A.shape[0]) xSeq = arange(0, n+1, 1) ySeq = arange(0, n+1, 1) xG, yG = meshgrid(xSeq, ySeq)
98 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics plot.ion() plot.pcolor(xG, yG, zG_A, cmap = cmap) plot.axis([0, n, n, 0]) plot.axes().set_aspect(‘equal’) plot.title(plotTitle) raw_input(“Hit Enter to dismiss the plot.”) plot.close()is function is an adaptation of the density plot code (Script D.2 in Appendix D). Here is an explanation of the code. e rst argument for the function call is the array of numbers, and the second argument is a title that will be displayed at the top of the plot. e rst executable state-ment denes a list of colors that will be used in the plot. Hexadecimal numbers “#000000” and “#FFFFFF” designate black and white, respec-tively. is color map will be used in the pcolor function. e value of n is the number of atoms in the atom list, and the statements dening xSeq, ySeq, xG, and yG are used to set up a grid of coordinates that will act as the independent variables specifying locations for the cells in the dot plot.e plot.ion() function call establishes interactive mode. With this established, each drawing command will immediately update the plot, so there is no need for a draw command. Aer all the updates are displayed in the plot, the script can issue a close() command so that control returns to the Python shell window.e pcolor function works with the mesh grid and the distance map matrix to color the plot according to the color map specied by cmap. Since two colors are specied, the distance range [0.0, 1.0] is split into two equal subranges, and colors from the color list are given to cells according to the following rule: distance maps in the range [0.0, 0.5] color a cell black, whereas distance maps in the range [0.5, 1.0] color a cell white. If you wish to make a correspondence between a color and the interatomic distance, you need only multiply the range corresponding to the color by the value of the distanceThreshold. For example, with a distanceThreshold value of 16, any plot cell colored black corresponds to an interatomic dis-tance between 0.0 and 8.0 Å.e axis function establishes tick values for the horizontal and vertical axes of the plot, whereas the command axes().set _ aspect(‘equal’) makes each of the plot cells a perfect square. e remaining lines have obvious functionality.
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 99 Script 3.01, available from the book’s website (www.structuralbioin formatics.com), contains the generate2Dplot function along with a mainline script that fetches a PDB le, extracts the alpha carbon atoms from a specied chain, and then displays a contact or distance map plot depending on the choice of cmap statements. e mainline works with the Shell class to illustrate the use of this class, and it does save some time because interatomic distances are only evaluated for atom pairs with an interatomic distance that is less than the distanceThreshold value. is allows us to compute all needed distances in O(n) time because the number of atoms that are within the distance threshold from some specic alpha carbon is bounded above by some small con-stant. Strictly speaking, the entire algorithm is O(n2) because the ini-tialization of the distance map array [done by the o n e s(( n ,n ), f l o a t) call] is intrinsically O(n2).To adapt the script to your own requirements, it is expected that you would choose the appropriate denition of cmap and also establish the value of distanceThreshold.3.6 INERTIAL AXES3.6.1 MotivationVery oen in a structural analysis, we want to approximate a secondary structural element with a single straight line. For example, Figure 3.5 shows a straight line that acts as the longitudinal axis of a helix.Figure 3.6 shows the alpha carbon atoms of the same helix and gives us an indication of a reasonable strategy for the determination of the axis: we should have the straight line positioned among the atoms so that it is closest to all these atoms in a least-squares sense. is is called the inertial axis.FIGURE 3.5 Longitudinal axis for a helix.
100 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsVarious research studies have used the inertial axis as a simplifying geometric construct to represent secondary structural elements. is is especially important when one wishes to compare the tertiary structures of related proteins. e following references are good starting points: [A86], [AM88], [OB92], and [YS99].Finally, here is an interesting note related to the principal axes of inertia computed for an entire protein (taking into account atomic masses): some researchers have observed that, with high frequency, at least one principal axis penetrates the surface of a protein in a region used for ligand binding (see [FR00]).3.6.2 Mathematical AnalysisOur strategy for computing the inertial axis is to have the axis pass through the centroid of the atoms with a direction chosen to minimize the sum of squares of the perpendicular distances from the atoms to the helix axis.More precisely, if di represents the perpendicular distance between atom a(i) and the helix axis, then we choose the axis direction so that the sum S is minimized with S dened as SdiiN∑==21 (3.3)Depending on the needs of the application, it is possible to dene the position of this axis using a dierent set of atoms, for example, all the atoms in the backbone or even all the atoms in the helix (backbone plus side chains). Before going further in this discussion, it should be stated that such an axis could also be computed to go among the atoms of a beta strand or, in fact, any arbitrary set of atoms.zyxFIGURE 3.6 Longitudinal axis dened relative to the alpha carbon atoms of thehelix.

104 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsSetting this to 0 gives us the following eigenvalue problem: Tw w=λ (3.15)e 3 × 3 tensor matrix T is symmetric, and considering that S is always positive for any vector w we see that T is also positive denite. Once T is calculated, we compute its eigenvalues and the required w (specifying the directions for our axis) will be the eigenvector corresponding to the smallest eigenvalue. It should be noted that three eigenvectors are produced by this procedure and, because of the symmetry of T, they are mutually orthogonal and can be used as an orthogonal basis for the set of atoms under consideration. Insome applications, this inertial frame of reference is a useful construct and the eigenvectors are called the principal axes of inertia.3.6.3 Script for Computing the Inertial AxisChimera already has a menu item (Tools… Depiction… PipesAndPlanks) that provides this functionality, but it is instructive to develop the code that computes the inertial axis and, of course, having your own code means that you can modify the algorithm to meet any individual needs. e algorithm has been implemented in the class called InertialAxes, which is imported as follows:from StructBio.LeastSquares.inertialAxes import InertialAxesSpecications for the class are as follows:Input for InertialAxes object initialization:A list of atom objectsInput for projectionOnAxis() an InertialAxes method:An atom objectInput for distanceToAxis() an InertialAxes method:An atom object

In the code that follows, we assume that atoms_L is a list of atoms prepared by the mainline script.As seen in Figure 3.6, we need to compute the centroid for atoms inthelist:def centroid(self): sum = zeros(3, float) for a in self.atoms_L: sum += a.coord() return sum/float(len(self.atoms_L))Code for computing the centroid is quite straightforward and is presented mainly to illustrate the utilization of the collection of atoms that will deter-mine the inertial axis. In the discussion that follows, it is important to realize that atoms will have coordinates dened in one of two frames of reference. ese are the original PDB frame of reference and a frame of reference estab-lished by shiing the origin to the centroid. e latter is needed when doing computations for the inertial axes corresponding to a particular atom list. e next function, tensorMatOneAtom, will be responsible for the calcula-tion of the matrix in Equation 3.8. First, note the following identity: AHGHB FGFCaa aa aaaa aa aaaa aa aaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa aaaaaa aa aaaaIaaaaaayz xy zxxy zx yzzx yz xyxyzxyzxyzxxyzxx yyyzzx yz zxyzxyzxyz()−−−−−−=+− −−+−−− +=++++++−=++ −000000222222222222222222222So, given the coordinates of an atom, we can do some simple calcula-tions on vectors to derive the matrix result. If you are not familiar with the various array calculations available in numpy, then it may be of benet to review some of the statements in this script: # This computes the contribution made by one atom to the tensor # matrix. # The return value is a matrix that will be added to other matrices # corresponding to other atoms. # The 3D coordinates of the atom are provided by coordArray. # This routine relies on the observation that this contribution is # equal to: # the identity matrix multiplied by the norm squared of the # coordinates minus the outer product of the coordinate vector # with itself. # This routine assumes that coordArray holds the coordinates # of the atom relative to the centroid. # Note: array([coordArray]) is a 1 by 3 array. def __tensorMatOneAtom(self, coordArray): sumSquares = sum(coordArray*coordArray) return sumSquares * numpy.identity(3) - \ array([coordArray])*array([coordArray]).transpose()With these two functions being available, generation of the inertial axes can be done in the _init_ function for the class:def __init__(self, atoms_L): self.atoms_L = atoms_L self.centroid = self.centroid() # List of vectors that are the inertial axes: self.inertialAxes_L = [] # The primary axis will be at index 0. self.primaryAxis = None tensorMat = numpy.zeros((3,3)) for atom in self.atoms_L: tensorMat += self.__tensorMatOneAtom(numpy.array(atom.coord()) – self.centroid) # With the tensor matrix now computed, we extract the eigenvector # corresponding to the smallest eigenvalues: eigvals, eigvecs = linalg.eig(tensorMat) ind = eigvals.argsort() # Sort the eigenvalues. # Be careful here to extract eigenvectors # from the transpose of eigvecs! for i in range(3): self.inertialAxes_L.append(eigvecs.transpose()[ind[i]]) self.primaryAxis = self.inertialAxes_L[0]

Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 107 e initialization function sums the matrix contributions made by all the atoms in the atom list to get the matrix in Equation 3.12. It then solves the eigenvalue problem described in Equation 3.15 and denes the primary axis (the inertial axis that we want) to be the eigenvector corre-sponding to the minimum eigenvalue.On completion of the initialization function, we have the primary axis dened as a direction vector that originates from the centroid. Referring to Figure 3.7, the direction vector is shown as w and the origin of the x, y, and z axes are positioned at the centroid. For many applications, we will need some extra functionality, which is provided by the following two functions:# Given an atom object this function gets the coordinates of # the atom and projects these coordinates onto the primary axis. # It returns the coordinates of this projection.# The coordinates are given in the PDB frame of reference.def projectionOnAxis(self, atom): # Compute atom coords relative to centroid. atmCoords = numpy.array(atom.coord()) - self.centroid return sum(self.primaryAxis * atmCoords) * self.primaryAxis + \ self.centroid # The centroid was added to convert back to the PDB # frame of reference.# ------------------------------------------------------# Given an atom object this function computes # the perpendicular distance from the atom to the primary axis.def distanceToAxis(self, atom): atmCoords = numpy.array(atom.coord()) return numpy.linalg.norm(atmCoords - self.projectionOnAxis(atom))e rst function projectionOnAxis computes the coordinates of the projection of an atom onto the primary axis. Referring to Figure3.7, we are computing the coordinates of the point labeled p. is is done by shiing the coordinates of the atom so that its frame of reference has origin at the centroid. is essentially gives us the vector going from the origin to the atom labeled a in Figure 3.7. Computing the inner product of this vector with the vector w will give the distance from the origin to the point p. is distance can be used as a scaling factor that stretches vector w out to point p. en, adding the centroid to this p vector gives us the coordinates of p in the PDB frame of reference.e function distanceToAxis computes the distance between an atom and the primary axis. e computation is very straightforward. We use the norm function to calculate the distance between the atom and its projection on the axis.
108 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticse InertialAxes class will be used in Exercises 7, 8, and 9 at the end of this chapter.3.7 DEHYDRONS3.7.1 MotivationAs discussed in [C05], hydrophobic attraction between two nonpolar groups involves a major electrostatic contribution because it increases the hydrogen bonding among the surrounding water molecules. As a reminder on the strength of hydrophobic interactions, recall from Section 1.4.3.3 that a disease state (sickle-cell anemia) can be induced by the cre-ation of a hydrophobic surface patch due to a mutational change in a single amino acid. It is well known that proteins fold due to electrostatic forces [A73], [FS02], [C05], [JP03]. In particular, hydrophobic collapse into the folded state is likely coupled with hydrogen bond formation [FK03]. is is supported by recognizing that the backbone of an unfolded pro-tein is highly polar due to the amide and carbonyl groups in each residue. Inan unfolded state, these groups are typically surrounded by polar water molecules, so the hydrophobic collapse and its concomitant removal of water (dehydration) leads to the thermodynamically favored formation of hydrogen bonds between the carbonyl and the amide groups.Empirical evidence has shown that the strength and stability of a hydrogen bond depends on various structural attributes such as the atom types acting as donor and acceptor; distance between donor and acceptor; and angle formed by the three atoms (donor, hydrogen, and acceptor). But this is not the full story. e strength and stability of the hydrogen bond also depends on the local microenvironment of the bond. In particular, the strength of the bond is best preserved when it is shielded from the weaken-ing eects of nearby polar water molecules.Consequently, a sustainable hydrophobic collapse involves the forma-tion and protection of hydrogen bonds occurring between the various backbone amides and carbonyls.Using the terminology and procedures introduced by Fernández and Scott [FS03], we consider a hydrogen bond to be protected when it is suf-ciently surrounded or wrapped by hydrophobic carbonaceous nonpolar groups having the form CHn, n = 0, 1, 2, 3. e microenvironment con-taining these groups will be dened as the interior of two intersecting desolvation spheres with radii 6.5 Å and centered at the alpha carbons of the residues containing the hydrogen bonded amide and carbonyl groups. 
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 109 Using the guidelines stipulated in [FS03], we consider a hydrogen bond to be deciently wrapped, or under wrapped, if the number of carbonaceous wrapping atoms is less than or equal to 19. Deciently wrapped hydrogen bonds are referred to as dehydrons. As noted in [FS03], these simple rules provide a fast but not exact strategy for the determination of deciently wrapped hydrogen bonds. Figures 3.8 and 3.9 show well-wrapped and deciently wrapped hydrogen bonds. In both gures, the atoms and bonds are represented in wire form with the following exceptions: dehydrons appear as green lines, well-wrapped hydrogen bonds appear as gray lines, the very large translucent desolvation spheres are colored blue (red) if the central alpha carbon is in the residue providing the hydrogen bond donor (acceptor), the smallest spheres designate the alpha carbon centers of the desolvation spheres, the middle-sized spheres represent the carbonaceous FIGURE 3.8 (See color insert.) Well-wrapped hydrogen bond.FIGURE 3.9 (See color insert.) Dehydron: a deciently wrapped hydrogen bond.
110 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticswrapping groups, and the spindle designates the hydrogen bond under consideration (colored green if it is a dehydron, otherwise gray).Counting the carbonaceous atoms (25 in Figure 3.8 and 14 in Figure3.9) reveals the categorization of the hydrogen bond when each count is com-pared with the value 19. Figure 3.8 shows the CYS 26–PRO 22 hydrogen bond of 1CRN, whereas Figure 3.9 shows the GLY 20–ARG 17 hydrogen bond of 1CRN.Why are dehydrons important? ere is mounting evidence that wrap-ping deciencies constitute areas on the protein surface that are essen-tially an invitation for other molecules to form a local binding that reduces or eliminates the wrapping deciency. Consequently, the wrapping defect is a sticky substructure that can play a role in various protein associa-tions such as protein–protein interactions, ligand binding, and deleterious aggregations such as the formation of amyloid plaques.Over the last decade, several papers have been written dealing with dehydrons and their relevance to various protein topics. e reader may consult the following references: dehydrons and their utility for drug design [F10], [FC08], [FR04], protein evolution and complexity [FS04], and protein–protein interfaces [FSS03].Work done by Fernández and Berry [FB10] indicates that an abundance of dehydrons oen represents a structural deciency that is compensated by an inordinately large number of disulde bonds. We return to this dis-cussion in Exercise 14.3.7.2 Script for Identifying DehydronsFigures 3.8 and 3.9 were created by a program (presented as Exercise11). It makes use of the Dehydrons class, which is contained in the StructBio package. e class can be accessed by using the following import statement:from StructBio.HBonds import Dehydronse arguments for the instantiation of the Dehydrons class are as follows:prot: the protein molecule object that will be processed to nd dehydrons.
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 111 distSlop: the distance slop used when this instantiation calls FindHBond.findHBonds (default value 0.4).angleSlop: the angle slop used when this instantiation calls FindHBond.findHBonds (default value 20.0).sphereRadius: the radius used for the desolvation spheres (default value 6.5).wrapThreshold: if the number of carbonaceous atoms in the desolva-tion spheres is less than or equal to this threshold, then the hydrogen bond is considered to be a dehydron (default value 19).lineWidth: the width of the lines used in the wire representation of prot when designating a hydrogen bond (default value 5.0).Aer the instantiation of the Dehydrons class, we can use the follow-ing attributes to access data related to the hydrogen bonds:hBonds_L: this is a list containing 2-tuples, each tuple holding a pair of atom objects corresponding to the donor and acceptor atoms for a hydrogen bond. As the description of a dehydron involves alpha carbon atoms, the hBonds_L list will only contain hydrogen bonds between standard amino acid residues. Consequently, hydrogen bonds between standard residues and water molecules will have been ltered out.dehydrons_L: this is a list of dehydrons each designated by a 2-tuple. It is a subset of the entries that are listed in hBonds_L.wrapper_D: this is a dictionary that allows one to determine all the carbonaceous atoms in the microenvironment of a hydrogen bond. A key will be a (donor, acceptor) atom pair that denes a hydrogen bond, and the corresponding dictionary value will be a Set that holds the atom objects designating all the carbonaceous atoms in the microenvironment.See the nal exercises of this chapter to nd examples of scripts that use the Dehydrons class.

Cysteine tetrahedrons Consider the rubredoxin protein from Clostridium pasteurianum (PDB ID 4RXN). is structure supersedes an earlier structural study reported by Watenpaugh and others [WS80] in 1980 when the PDB had only 70entries (current content is over 102,000 structures). e pro-tein has four cysteine residues (positions 5, 8, 38, and 41) surrounding an iron atom to make up the FeS4 complex. In their paper, the authors report on various geometric properties of the complex including the Fe–S bond distances. Recall that Figure 3.1 shows this complex. If you 
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 113 fetch the protein and view it with Chimera, you will see that the sulfur atoms seem to be at the vertices of a tetrahedron. is is to be expected because of the coordination forces of the iron atom. A perfect tetra-hedron is undoubtedly compromised by the extraneous forces placed on the sulfur atoms by their cysteine residues that have to meet the positional constraints imposed by the protein backbone. In the spirit of this investigation described in [WS80], we pose the following additional question: how well do the sulfur atoms in these cysteine residues coordinate with the iron atom to form a tetrahe-dron? To answer this question, compute all six S–S distances and calculate the average distance. en, calculate the percentage devia-tion of each distance from this average. e output format should be three columns with appropriate headings: Column 1: a pair of integers identifying the two chain positions for the CYS residues Column 2: the distance between the sulfur atoms in these two CYS residues Column 3: the deviation of this distance from the average (expressed as a percentage) See also 1RB9 and 1BRF. Note: to make the code simpler, you can take advantage of the fact that the structures each contain only four cysteine residues. 3. Finding disulde bridges Various researchers have been involved in the protein fold pre-diction problem. In some studies, algorithms can predict the con-formation of the backbone but without specication of the disulde bridges between cysteine residues. As a nal step in the renement of positions for the backbone atoms, a dierent algorithm will then try to predict the occurrence of these bridges and make adjustments to the backbone atoms so that the bridges can be put into the nal predicted model. To do this, it is usually necessary to collect statis-tics that characterize the geometry of disulde bridges. is seems to be a simple task, but it can be complicated if you have to deal with redundancy in the data. For example, if a protein is a dimer com-prising two structurally identical chains, do you include disulde bridges from both the chains in the collection of statistics? We will consider these issues to be beyond the scope of the question. To make 
114 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsthe exercise more straightforward, our goal will be to write a Python script that deals with all the sulde bridges in the entire protein. Your script should fetch a protein le specied by user input typed into the Python Shell. It should nd all cysteine residues, and for each such residue it should determine whether or not that residue is involved with a disulde bridge. Be careful, not all cysteine resi-dues are involved with bridges (e.g., the D chain of 1A4Y has sev-eral cysteine residues that are not involved with disulde bridges). In Figure3.10, we can see a disulde bridge linking CYS 191.A with CYS 220.A in chain A of 1BQY. For each bridge found in the protein, your script should print out the following information: a. Residue names and positions of the cysteine residues that are involved (e.g., CYS 191.A and CYS 220.A). b. Distance between the alpha carbon atoms in the cysteine resi-dues involved with the bridge. c. If a cysteine residue is in a backbone but does not participate in a disulde bridge, then it should be part of the output with a note indicating that there is no bridge structure associated with this residue. Use the following as input for your program: 1BQY (all CYS involved with disulde bridges), 1TCR and 1A4Y (a mix of bridged and non-bridged CYS residues), and 1ZNJ (note the bridges between chains). Distances between beta carbons in beta sheets When considering beta sheets, are consecutive residues of a strand closer than neighboring residues on dierent strands? To answer this question, let us assume that the coordinates of a residue are approxi-mated by the coordinates of the beta carbon atom within a residue. Write a program that reads a PDB le and calculates the average dis-tance between Cβ atoms of consecutive residues in a beta strand. See the dotted line in Figure 3.11 for an example of one such distance. e program should also calculate the average distance between neighboring Cβ atoms of hydrogen bonded residues that are in dier-ent strands of the beta sheet (see the dashed line in Figure 3.11). Test your program on the parallel and antiparallel beta sheets of a glycosyl hydrolase (PDB ID 1QBA). To test your program on other beta sheet–rich proteins, you can consult [ZK00] for some interesting examples. Note: nding Cβ atoms of consecutive residues in a beta strand is fairly easy. Finding neighboring Cβ atoms of parallel and antiparallel strands will be more of a challenge, but you can apply the following strategy: generate an instance of the HBonds class and pull out all the hydrogen bonds that are in the category “PL” or “AP.” Each donor and acceptor pair will correspond to atoms Ni and Oj in Figures 2.11 and 2.12. Now you can compute each of the needed distances (once and only once) by nding the residues containing Ni and Oj. For example, the neighboring Cβ atoms in consecutive residues are in the donor residue and the residue just before the donor residue. e Cβ atoms for the residues in dierent strands can be determined by studying Figures 2.11 and 2.12. One of them will be in the donor residue, whereas the partner will be in the residue holding the accep-tor atom if the strands are running antiparallel or in the residue just aer the acceptor residue if the strands are running parallel. Generating test data for distance geometry algorithms e distance geometry problem can be stated as follows: can you derive the coordinates of all the atoms in a molecule if you are given the distances between pairs of atoms? It can be shown that the problem is fairly easy if all possible O(n2) distances are accurately known. e problem becomes much more dicult if only a subset of the distances is given, for example, if we only get as input the distances between any atom and its nearby neighbors. Nearby in this context might mean within 5 Å. Researchers developing algorithms to solve this problem typically build input data sets that are used to test the capabilities of the algorithm. ey start with a protein le from the PDB, generate the input test data, apply the distance geometry algorithm to calculate the coordinates of the atoms, and then measure the agreement between the calculated coordi-nates and the original coordinates from the PDB le. In this exercise, you are to write a script that asks the user for a PDB le identier and a threshold. e script should fetch the PDB le and generate a le that lists all atom pairs such that the distance between the atoms in a pair is less than the specied threshold. A line in the output le should fully identify the atoms in an atom pair followed by the distance and then a code that indicates the type of interaction between the atoms. For example, the following are four lines taken from the test data le generated for 1CRN with threshold = 3.0 Å:THR 1.A O ILE 35.A N 2.859 HTHR 1.A O THR 1.A N 2.668 NTHR 1.A O THR 1.A CA 2.355 GTHR 1.A O THR 1.A C 1.237 B An output line contains the residue type, residue position, residue chain identier, and atom name within the residue for both atoms in the pair. is is followed by the distance (less than the threshold, of course) and the atom pair code. Use the following codes: B: bonded (the two atoms are covalently bonded) G: geminals (the two atoms are both bonded to a third atom)
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 119 H: hydrogen bonded (the two atoms have a hydrogen bond between them) N: none of the above e codes are of value if the test data distances are to be subsequently modied by adding some small amount of noise. Usually, the amount of noise to be added will depend on the nature of the atom pair. is exercise should make use of the HBonds and Shell classes. e test data should be written out to a text le that has a le name indi-cating that it contains near neighbor data, the threshold value, and the PDB identier. For example, processing 1CRN with threshold 3.5 Å uses the le name NN_3_5_1CRN.txt. 7. Introducing the Solids class Exercise 8 makes use of the Solids class,* which can be used to display a set of elongated bullet-like structures that we will call tubes (shapes are similar to test tubes). In the next exercise, they will be used to represent the inertial axes of helical secondary struc-tures. e class can be instantiated with an empty argument list, for example,tubeScene = Solids() Aer this, we can bring tubes into the display by adding them to the instance, for example,tubeScene.addTube(startPoint, endpoint, tubeRadius, tubeColour) Here, startPoint and endPoint are two numpy arrays specify-ing the 3D coordinates of two points in the display space. e radius of a tube is dened by the oating point number tubeRadius, and its color is given by tubeColour, which is a three-component tuple representing the color in RGB format. When all the tubes are added into the scene, we can display it using the following function call:tubeScene.display() To get familiar with this class, you should try it with various arguments. If you run Script 3.08, available from the book’s web-site (www.structuralbioinformatics.com), you should get a scene that looks like Figure 3.14.* A full description of this class can be found in Appendix B.

Using the InertialAxes class: inertial axis tube visualization Very often in a structural analysis, we want to approximate a secondary structural element with a single straight line or some type of visual structure that is positioned using such a line. Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 121 e code should then generate the end points dening the 3D posi-tions of tubes that are to be drawn in the display. is can be done by using the projectionOnAxis function of the InertialAxes class. Aer all the tube points are set up in a list of pairs, they can be used to generate tubes in the display by making use of the addTube function. b. 122 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics For each secondary structure, compute the distances between the alpha carbon atoms and the inertial axis. If the maximum distance is less than some prespecied tolerance (say 3.5 Å), then the axis is considered to be a faithful representation of the secondary structure. If the maximum distance is greater than the tolerance, then the set of residues in the secondary structure is split into two shorter helices and the end point generation function is called for each one. is exercise should not require any modication of the InertialAxes and Solids classes. Computation of helical hydrophobic moment e papers by Eisenberg and others [EW82a], [EW82b] deal with the spatial distribution of hydrophobic side chains in a protein. At this point in our studies, we have an excellent opportunity to extend this FIGURE 3.16 Using recursive tubes.
Algorithms Dealing with Distance ◾ 123 work so that computation of the hydrophobic moment is accompanied by a visual representation of the hydrophobic moment as an arrow that starts at the inertial axis of the helix and goes through the center of the side chain. ere are several ways to dene the hydrophobic moment of the side chains in a helix. Let us go with the following procedure: a. Aer fetching a protein le, use the TubesForSSEs class to con-struct tube solids for all the helices and strands of the protein. b. Note that you can access attributes of an object of this class to derive the primary axis of each SSE. e attribute axes.inertialAxes _ L will be a list of objects instantiated from the InertialAxes class. Attributes of this class will give you access to the atom list (atoms _ L), the centroid of these atoms (cen-troid), and the primary axis (primaryAxis). c. For each residue Ri associated with an axis, compute si, dened as the center of the atoms in the side chain (exclude backbone atoms N, Ca, C, and O). Skip any glycine residues. d. For each helix residue, project si onto its inertial axis. Designate the projection point on the axis as pi. Compute Ni, which is a unit nor-mal vector rooted at pi with the same direction as the vector going from pi to si. Dene Hi = hi Ni, where hi is the Kyte–Doolittle hydro-phobicity value for residue Ri. Note that Hi will point away from si if the residue is hydrophilic. Use the Solids class to visually display the normal Hi as an arrow. For a helix, you should get a display that looks something like that seen in Figure 3.18. Note that the coloring is consistent with the hydrophobicity of the corresponding residue.FIGURE 3.17 (See color insert.) Plates and tubes for 2BYS.
124 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics e. At the request of the user, the display should change so that all the arrows are translated along the principal axis so that they are rooted at the centroid (Figure 3.19). is produces the helical wheel seen on page 372 of [EW82a]. In our case, the arrows in the wheel can be related to the surrounding structure. In par-ticular, one can oen see a clear indication of amphiphilicity with the hydrophobic arrows pointing to the core of the protein. f. For each axis, compute the hydrophobic moment vector Mi as the vector sum of all Vi vectors, where each Vi is the Hi vector trans-lated from pi to ci. Compute the sum of all Vi vectors that corre-spond to hydrophilic residues. Display both arrows. is should produce a result such as that shown in Figure 3.20. g. Finally, the user should have a fourth display option that gener-ates the previous vector sums divided by the number of residues involved. ese average vectors will have an appearance that is similar to Figure 3.20 (the arrows are simply shorter). Since the script involves a more extensive interaction with the user, it is best to implement two dialogs to facilitate user interaction. 135Chapter 4Algorithms Dealing with Angles4.1 MOTIVATIONIn addition to distance calculations, computations that derive bond angles and dihedral angles are fundamental to the evaluation of molecular struc-ture. Usually, these evaluations are done for a static molecule. In a more dynamic setting, we may attempt to evaluate these quantities as they change with time due to exibility of the molecule. For now we assume that the model is static and that we know the positions of atoms in three-dimensional (3D) space. In particular, we will be dealing with proteins for which the coordinates of all atoms are known with a fairly reasonable accuracy. Typically, we will get these coordinates from a le that is down-loaded from the Protein Data Bank (PDB).4.2 BOND ANGLERecall from linear algebra that an inner product of normalized vectors u and v can be viewed as the cosine of the angle between these vectors. is is expressed using the formula: cos, .()θ= uv uv (4.1)Consider two atoms a and c (with coordinate vectors a and c) both bonded to a third atom b with coordinates given by vector b as in Figure 4.1.
136 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsIf we set =− =−anduabvcb, then the cosine of the bond angle will be given by ()()θ=−−−−cos.Tabcbabcb (4.2)Once this quantity is calculated, the required bond angle can be easily derived by taking the inverse cosine of the quantity on right-hand side of the last equation.4.2.1 Calculating Bond Angles in a ScriptIf you need to calculate a very small number of bond angles, then it is advisable to use the Chimera menu invocation: Tools… Structure Analysis… Angles/Torsions, which will launch a dialog window that allows you toselect atoms followed by a request for the angle calculation. In this section we are assuming that you need several, perhaps thousands, of these calculations and so a script is the only reasonable approach. e following code follows the formula given in Equation 4.2 and serves to calculate the bond angle formed when atomB is connected to both atomA and atomC:def bondAngle(atomA, atomB, atomC): baVec = atomA.coord() - atomB.coord() bcVec = atomC.coord() - atomB.coord() baVec.normalize(); bcVec.normalize() angleRadians = arccos(baVec * bcVec) return angleRadians * 180.0/pie return value is the bond angle measured in degrees. An obvious modication of the code will allow you to get the bond angle in radians. acbbθacFIGURE 4.1 Bond angle dened via vectors.
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 137 As one might expect, Chimera has a built-in function to compute angles. e following two lines will generate the same results:angle = bondAngle(atomA, atomB, atomC)angle = chimera.angle(atomA.coord(), atomB.coord(). atomC.coord()4.3 DIHEDRAL ANGLES4.3.1 Dening Dihedral AnglesConsider atoms in the protein backbone: ααNC CNC1112 2 αCNCCN23 334. As we move from residue to residue, the corre-sponding bond lengths do not change much.For example: αN-Cii has approximately the same bond length as αN-Cjj for arbitrary i and j. Similarly, corresponding bond angles tend to be the same (see Exercise 1 at the end of this chapter). You cannot make this same statement for dihedral angles. Dihedral angles are due to a “swivel” action around a single bond and are primarily responsible for dening the position of the backbone atoms in 3D space. Be sure to under-stand that changing a dihedral angle will not aect bond angles between successive atoms in the backbone. In fact, we could keep all bond angles constant while moving Ni relative to Ni+1 by using a swiveling rotation around the αC-Cii bond.While a bond angle is determined by a sequence of three bonded atoms, a dihedral angle is dened by a sequence of four consecutive bonded atoms. In general, the rst three atoms and the last three atoms of this sequence dene two planes that intersect in a line that is coincident with the bond between the middle two atoms. For example, Figure 4.2 shows how the four consecutive atoms αCNCC-1iiii of the backbone dene two planes that intersect in the line that is coincident with the αN-Cii bond. e angle between these two planes is referred to as the φ (phi) angle associated with residue i. In a similar fashion, Figure 4.3 shows how the four consecutive atoms α+NC CN1iiii of the backbone dene two planes that intersect in the line that is coincident with the αC-Cii bond. e angle between these two planes is referred to as the ψ (psi) angle associated with residue i. Since the backbone atoms go through consecutive repetitions of the atoms N, Cα, and C, we have discussed only two of the three possible starting positions for a sequence of four atoms that dene a dihedral angle. In fact, there is another dihedral angle to be considered, namely the dihedral 
138 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsangle that is dened by the four-atom sequence αα++CCNC11iiii. is is called the ω (omega) angle and it has the carbon and nitrogen of the peptide bond as the middle two atoms. e peptide bond has a partial double-bond character due to resonance eects. Because of this and the steric interactions between the two side chains attached to the alpha car-bon atoms at either end of the sequence, the ω dihedral angle typically shows a trans conguration for this four-atom sequence. In other words, ω= °180. As a consequence, αα++C,C,N,C11iiii, the O atom attached to the carbonyl Ci atom and the H atom on the nitrogen are all very close to Unit normal ofCi-1, Ni, Cαi planeUnit normal ofNi, Cαi, Ci planeCαi–1CαiCαiCβiCi–1Ci–1Oi–1Hi+1Ni+1NiHiCiCiOiNiφ dihedralFIGURE 4.2 (See color insert.) Phi dihedral angle.Unit normal ofNi, Cαi, Ci planeUnit normal ofCαi, Ci, Ni+1 planeCαi–1CiCαiCαiCβiCi–1NiOi–1Hi+1Ni+1Ni+1NiHiCiOiψ dihedralFIGURE 4.3 (See color insert.) Psi dihedral angle.
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 139 being coplanar. is planar nature of the peptide bond usually puts the side chains in positions that are as far apart as possible.4.3.2 Mathematics for the Computation of a Dihedral Anglee next few sections give a complete presentation of the theory needed for the computation of a dihedral angle. It is included for the sake of com-pleteness and can be skipped if you simply need the script that does the calculation (Section 4.3.7).Our denition of a dihedral angle as the angle between two planes has to be made more precise. Since an angle is determined by the intersection of two lines, we will need to derive two such lines from the planes dened by the sequence of four atoms. For example, we could have each line lie within a plane and have both lines perpendicular to the line of intersec-tion of the two planes. e analytic derivation required by this approach is somewhat lengthy and is less preferable to a strategy that generates the needed lines by computing the normals of the two planes. ese normals are illustrated in Figures 4.2 and 4.3. A short deduction using Euclidean geometry will show that the dihedral angle between the two planes is equal to the angle between the two normals.Since the normal to a plane dened by two intersecting lines (in our case, two consecutive bonds) is easily computed using a cross product for-mula, we can use this formula as a convenient “algebraic subroutine.”4.3.3 Computation of a NormalTwo lines that intersect in 3D space dene a plane. To construct a normal to this plane, we calculate a cross product. Suppose we have two vectors u and v. e cross product is a vector that is perpendicular to both u and v and it has a magnitude that is equal to the area of the parallelogram spanned by the vectors.
