Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 145 4.4 RAMACHANDRAN PLOTSWhile the dihedral angles in a polypeptide can change with the conforma-tion of the chain, they cannot freely adopt just any arbitrary values. It has been known since the early 1960s that steric collisions will prohibit certain combinations of the phi and psi angles on either side of a given alpha car-bon atom. An observed combination of phi/psi angles is oen represented by a single point in a two-dimensional plot that has a horizontal axis for phi ranging over values []−π π, (i.e., −180° to +180°) and a vertical axis for psi ranging over the same values. A collection of many such points can be used to record the observed phi/psi combinations corresponding to a set of alpha carbon atoms. is type of scatter plot is called a Ramachandran plot since Ramachandran and his colleagues (see [RR63]) were the rst people to study the allowable phi/psi angles and the dependency of their values on the type of residue associated with the alpha carbon.Figure 4.7 is derived from a Ramachandran scatter plot of 121,870 phi/psi combinations taken from 463 protein structures [MM92]. e 180135BbaAbL1b~b~a~pp~b~b~190450–45–90–135–180–135–90–4509013518045ψϕFIGURE 4.7 (See color insert.) Ramachandran plot for 121,870 residues (463 protein structures). A - Core alpha L - Core left-handed alpha a - Allowed alpha l - Allowed left-handed alpha~a - Generous alpha ~l - Generous left-handed alpha B - Core beta p - Allowed epsilon b - Allowed beta ~p - Generous epsilon~b - Generous beta
146 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsfollowing procedure was used to generate this pixilated gure: e area of the original scatter plot was subdivided into square “pixels,” each having a side length of °10. In this way, the entire plot is covered with 362=1296 pixels. A pixel was given a dark color and characterized as “core” if it cov-ered at least 100 points in the scatter plot. A pixel was given a color of medium intensity and characterized as “allowed” if it was not a core pixel but covered at least eight points in the scatter plot. Pixels in the “gen-erous” region are obtained by extending an allowed region with a bor-der area of width °20. e generous region is given a color that is lighter than an allowed region. Any pixel that is not core, allowed, or generous is given the lightest color or simply le as white. So, in summary, the darker areas correspond to regions of the plot where the density of points is high-est and other colors represent regions of lower density. White regions or those with the lightest color are used to denote areas of the plot where there is the least likelihood of observing a point, for example, φ= °90 and ψ=−°90. e plot of Figure 4.7 was created using PROCHECK,* a program that checks the stereochemical quality of protein structures (see [LM93] and [MM92]).As indicated in the list following Figure 4.7, various regions of the plot are labeled with single-letter designations that indicate some secondary structure properties such as alpha helices, beta strands, and epsilon turns. Alpha helices are typically right handed although le-handed helices do occur but with considerably less frequency. e epsilon region of the Ramachandran plot is for phi/psi combinations that are usually associ-ated with a turn that is just prior to a helix or beta strand. A good example of this occurs in the protein 1JAL for residue VAL287, which occurs on a tight turn between two antiparallel beta strands. More information about two-residue turns can be found in [MP94].Since an angle rotation of 180 + n degrees is the same as an angle rotation of n − 180 degrees, any Ramachandran plot has a “wraparound” continuity. We could consider the uppermost row of pixels to be adjacent to the lowest row of pixels and the lemost column of pixels is essen-tially adjacent to the rightmost column. Inspection of Figure 4.7 will verifythis.As noted earlier, PROCHECK checks the stereochemical quality of a protein structure. An example of such processing is given by Figure4.8, where the Ramachandran scatter plot for 1A4Y is superimposed on the * http://www.ebi.ac.uk/thornton-srv/soware/PROCHECK/.
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 147 background distribution discussed earlier and illustrated by Figure 4.7. Note that most of the points reside in the core alpha and core beta regions. Aer the protein’s backbone conformation has been computed, PROCHECK soware can be used to detect the existence of any anoma-lous dihedral angles. For example, Figure 4.8 indicates that residues SER 15 (chains A and D), GLU 58 (chain E), and SER 4 (chain E) all have phi/psi angles that produce points in the white region. A point in the white region may indicate an error in the computation of the conformation or it may simply be a valid entry that rarely occurs.To some extent, Ramachandran plots can also show how the dihedral angles on either side of an alpha carbon atom are aected by the type of residue associated with that alpha carbon. For example, the glycine residue essentially lacks a side chain and so it does not have the steric constraints of the other amino acids. Figure 4.9 shows the scatter plot for phi/psi angles that are limited to glycine residues. One can see that the distribu-tion is somewhat dierent from the general case illustrated in Figure4.7. Although there are fewer constraints on dihedral angles for glycine, there are still some distribution patterns that can be observed and Figure 4.9 180Ramachandran plot pdbla4yBab~b~bb~11ASN 59(E)GLU 58(E)GLU 353 (A)GLU 353 (D)SER 15 (D)SER 15 (A)SER 4(B)HIS 65(B)ARG 65(B)HIS 65(E)ASN 82(D)ASN 82(E)~pp~b~bbSER 4(E)13590450–45–90–135–135–180–900Phi (degrees)Psi (degrees)4590135180–45FIGURE 4.8 Ramachandran plot for residues of protein 1A4Y.
148 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsshows ve clusters within the scatter plot. An in-depth analysis of this plot can be found in [HB05].While glycine provides an example of dihedral angles that are subject to few steric constraints, we nd that the phi/psi angles for proline are severely constrained. e PROCHECK version of the Ramachandran plot for the 42 proline residues of 1A4Y is given in Figure 4.10. We see that phi is constrained to values that are within a narrow band typically ranging from −°60 to −°90 depending on the secondary structure of its local envi-ronment. Inspection of Figure 4.11 shows why this is expected. e phi dihedral angle just prior to the proline alpha carbon is severely restricted because the chain arising from the alpha carbon loops back to bond with the backbone nitrogen atom bonded to that alpha carbon. e psi angle –180αϕψαL–180–900–90180900βPRβPβPβPRβS90180FIGURE 4.9 Ramachandran plot for glycine residues.1801800Pro (42)ϕ9090ψ0–90–180 –90FIGURE 4.10 Ramachandran plot for proline residues.
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 149 aer the alpha carbon has much less restriction as can be seen in the plot of Figure 4.10.Another interesting example of a constraint is seen in “pre-proline” residues. In this case, the side chain of the residue prior to proline bends away from the proline loop to avoid a steric clash. is is clearly illustrated in Figure 4.11, where the phenylalanine ring of residue 70 in 1AG9 veers away from the proline loop in the next residue. 150 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural BioinformaticsScript 4.1: Generating a Ramachandran Plot for a ProteinScript D.3 can be adapted to a variety of applications that need a scatter plot output. e next script shows how we can generate a Ramachandran plot for a given protein:import matplotlib.pyplot as pltdef ramaPlot(res_L, saveFilePath = ""): plt.ion() fig = plt.figure(figsize = (8,8)) ax = fig.add_subplot(111, aspect = 'equal') ax.set_title("Ramachandran Plot " + saveFilePath, fontsize = 14) ax.set_xlabel("PHI (degrees)", fontsize = 12) ax.set_ylabel("PSI (degrees)", fontsize = 12) ax.set_xlim([-180, 180]) ax.set_ylim([-180, 180]) ax.set_xticks([-180, -135, -90, -45, 0, 45, 90, 135, 180]) ax.set_yticks([-180, -135, -90, -45, 0, 45, 90, 135, 180]) ax.grid(True, linestyle = '-', color = '0.75') ax.hlines(0, -10, 10, colors='k', linestyles='dashed') ax.vlines(0, -10, 10, colors='k', linestyles='dashed') # Initialization: # List indicies: 0 for Helix, 1 for Strand, and 2 for Coil. colors_T = ('k', 'g', 'b') # Marker colors for Helix, Strand, Coil. shapes_T = ('o', 's', 'd') # Marker shapes for Helix, Strand, Coil. phi_L = [[], [], []] # Phi lists for Helix, Strand, and Coil psi_L = [[], [], []] # Psi lists for Helix, Strand, and Coil # Generate the phi, psi coordinate lists: for r in res_L: # Skip if residue has no phi, psi attributes: if r.phi is None or r.psi is None: continue if r.isHelix: phi_L[0].append(r.phi) psi_L[0].append(r.psi) if r.isStrand: phi_L[1].append(r.phi) psi_L[1].append(r.psi) if not (r.isHelix or r.isStrand): phi_L[2].append(r.phi) psi_L[2].append(r.psi) # Drop in the markers: for i in range(3): ax.scatter(phi_L[i], psi_L[i], s = 20, color = colors_T[i], marker = shapes_T[i]) # Save file if the saveFilePath parameter is not the empty string. if saveFilePath != "": fig.savefig(saveFilePath + "_RamachandranPlot", dpi = fig.dpi)

154 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics4.5 LEAST SQUARES PLANE4.5.1 MotivationIn biochemistry, we have seen several structures in which the atoms are almost coplanar, for example, aromatic rings and heme rings. e last example is seen in the myoglobin protein: 1MBO (see Figure 4.13). In this section, we wish to calculate the equation of the “best” plane that goes among the “almost coplanar” atoms. In this case, the best plane will be the plane that minimizes the sum of the squares of the distances between the almost coplanar atoms and the plane. We call this the least squares plane.Suppose we wish to nd the equation of the least squares plane for the heme ring of 1MBO. We know that three points can dene a plane, but which three should we pick? Since making a choice of some particular set of three atoms would introduce some bias, a reasonable approach would be to use more than three atoms, the idea being to nd a plane that goes among the atoms of the heme ring and minimizes the sum of the squared distances measured from the atoms to that plane (Figure 4.16).We will see that a mathematical representation of this least squares plane has several uses. For example, we may dene a line in 3D space (such as the line coincident with a bond) and then we can measure the angle that this line makes with the plane.4.5.2 Least Squares Plane: Mathematical AnalysisWhile the analysis of this problem follows a development that is similar to that for the inertial axis, there are some mathematical niceties that merit an individual treatment.We will consider the almost coplanar atoms to make up a target list. In the discussion to follow, we will designate the target list atoms as =()1,2, ,ai ni. e ith atom in the list, ()ai will have coordinate vector FIGURE 4.16 Hemoglobin ring with “almost coplanar” atoms.

Consider P to be any point in the plane. en the vector P − R can be con-sidered to lie in the plane and hence perpendicular to R, as shown in the next gure (Figure 4.18). 156 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsis is a realistic expectation: We only need a single vector R to determine a plane. e direction of R will determine the orientation of the plane, while the length of R will establish the perpendicular distance from the origin to the plane.A naive computational strategy could now proceed as follows: Given ,()ai we can derive 2di in terms of ()ai and the components of R. We can use Equation 4.18 to express S in terms of these components and then we would seek to determine the components of R that minimize S. If we assume that ()= ,,,TRrrrxyz this minimization can be attempted by calculating partial derivatives of S with respect to the components of R and then setting these partial derivatives to zero. Unfortunately, the equations arising from the par-tial derivatives are quite complicated and dicult to solve. You might want to verify this just to see how bad the situation can become. It so happens that a better representation of R is R = mN, where ()= ,,TNnnnxyz is a unit vector that essentially species the direction of R (much like w did for the inertial axis in Section 3.6) and m is a simple scalar that species the length of R. is representation of R is somewhat counterintuitive because, instead of only three variables (),, ,rrrxyz we now have to solve for four variables: nx, ny, nz, and m. However, the number of degrees of freedom in the solution is still only 3 because N is a unit vector and so we have NTN = 1, which is a con-straint acting on N. e presence of a constraint turns this into a Lagrange multiplier problem that has a reasonably straightforward solution.e reader should note that the naive strategy demands that the com-ponents of R essentially dene both the direction of R and its length. Setting R = mN allows us to “disentangle” length information from direc-tion information leading to a more eective mathematical analysis.We now derive the Lagrange formulation. Suppose ()ai represents the coor-dinates of an atom that is not on the plane dened by R = mN. We can derive the formula for ()di in terms of m, N, and ()ai by observing that the length of R is m, which is the sum of ()di and the projection of a(i) onto the unit nor-mal N, which is parallel to R. is is illustrated in Figure 4.14. So how do we use it to build soware that implements a least squares plane? Before going any further, let us review what has been accomplished. e input for the least squares plane is simply a collection of Chimera atom objects. e mathemat-ical analysis tells us that we can use the coordinates of all these atoms to build the A matrix, which leads to the matrix Equation 4.32. Aer computing the eigenvalues, we end up with two arrays (each a three-component vector) that constitute a solution of the problem, namely: the centroid C of the atoms and the eigenvector N that determines the direction of a line that is normal to the plane. As discussed earlier, this point in 3D space and the normal vector are enough to implicitly specify a plane. ey will be stored as attributes for a


===== [43/99] chapter-43.html =====

160 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsclass that will allow us to instantiate objects that “behave like” least squares planes. To understand that these attributes, C and N, are sucient for our needs, let us consider the methods that such a class would support:• projectionOnPlane(): Given a point P not on the plane, deter-mine Q, the projection of P onto the plane. is will be the intersec-tion of the plane with a line from P that is normal to the plane.• distanceToPlane(): Given a point P not on the plane, calculate the distance between P and its projection Q on the plane.• angleToPlane(): Given two points P and Q, calculate the angle made by the line through P and Q with the plane.We now show how each of these calculations would use the vectors C and N.projectionOnPlane(): Consider Figure 4.19. We are given the arrays N, C, and P, representing the vectors CN, OC, and OP, respectively. Both OC and OP are dened relative to the frame of reference established by the PDB le, while CN is considered to have an origin that is the centroid C. We need to derive the coordinates of Q (the projection of P onto the plane) represented in Figure 4.19 by the vector OQ (again in the PDB frame of reference). Since we know both C and P we can compute P − C to get an array that is represented in the gure by the vector CP (as an equation: CP= OP − OC). e vector CP essentially represents the point P in a frame of reference that has C as its origin. Now we are able to compute the dot product of CP with CN (the normal to the plane) to get PN, which is the projection of P onto that normal. If we subtract the vector CPN from OP, we get the vector OQ, which represents the required projection (relative to the PDB frame of reference). In other words: PQ = −CPN and adding this to OP (as in Figure 4.19) gives us the needed projection.distanceToPlane(): Again, consider Figure 4.19. We are given the coordinates of P and we wish to nd the distance from P to the plane. is will simply be the length of vector PQ, so this function will call the projectionOnPlane() function to get Q and then calculate the norm of PQ.angleToPlane(): Consider Figure 4.20. We are given the arrays PA and PB representing the vectors OPA and OPB, respectively. If the line segment from PB to PA is extended until it intersects the plane at point V, then our goal is to nd the angle θ between this line and the line from QB to QA, which also goes through point V in the plane. In the gure, it is intended 
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 161 that QA and QB are the projections onto the plane of PA and PB, respec-tively. We can obtain θ by rst evaluating the cosine of θ, which is the ratio VQ VPBB. Note that the dotted line above QAQB has the same length as QAQB. We can use a similarity of triangles deduction to show that ()θ=cosQQPPABAB (4.35)In summary, this function will use the projectionOnPlane() function to calculate both QA and QB and then calculate ()arccos QQ PPAB AB toget θ.We now have enough mathematical background to implement the LeastSquaresPlane class. However, before doing so, it should be Origin in PDBframe of referenceQPCONPNFIGURE 4.19 Calculating a projection onto the plane.Origin in PDBframe of referenceOVQAQBPBPAθθFIGURE 4.20 Calculating the angle between a line segment and a plane.
162 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsnoted that there are applications that need the generation of a plane object that is simply dened by means of a point and plane normal. For example, in Exercise 8, we need a plane that is to be perpendicular to the inertial axis of a membrane protein. ese considerations give us a won-derful opportunity for the illustration of how Python can elegantly sup-port both strategies for the specication of a plane. We use the Python object-oriented inheritance mechanism. We will implement a base class called Planes with an __init__ method that accepts a point C and a normal N. It will oer the methods just described. We then imple-ment a derived class called LeastSquaresPlane with an __init__ method that accepts a list of Chimera atom objects. is class will be derived from the Planes class and so it will also have the methods just described.Both classes are in the le called planes (available from the book’s web-site) usingfrom StructBio.LeastSquares.planes import Planesfrom StructBio.LeastSquares.planes import LeastSquaresPlaneHere is the script:__import chimera, numpyfrom numpy import array, linalg, zeros, dot, arccos#===================================================================# Planes CLASSclass Planes(object): def __init __(self, c, n): # The next two arrays (3-component vectors) define # a plane in the PDB frame of reference. self.center = c self.normal = n/linalg.norm(n) # -------------------------------------------------------------------- # Given an atom object or an array object this function # returns the coordinates. def getCoords(self, atomORarray): if type(atomORarray) is chimera.Atom: coords = array(atomORarray.coord()) if type(atomORarray) is numpy.ndarray: coords = atomORarray if not(type(atomORarray) is chimera.Atom or type(atomORarray) is numpy.ndarray): print “Bad argument type in getCoords function call.” return None return coords
Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 163 # -------------------------------------------------------------------- # Given an atom object or an array this function gets the # coordinates (or directly uses the array) to define the # coordinates of a point P (coordsP) in the PDB frame of reference. # It then projects these coordinates onto the plane by: # 1) Subtract the center c array to get the “local” coordinates # localP (in a frame of reference that has c as the origin. # 2) Calculate the projection of localP on the normal vector # (s e l f. n o r m a l ). # 3) Subtract this result from coordsP to get the projection # on the plane relative to the PDB frame of reference. def projectionOnPlane(self, atomORarray): # Compute coordinates relative to an origin at the centroid. coordsP = self.getCoords(atomORarray) localP = coordsP - self.center # Step 1 projOnNormal = self.normal*dot(self.normal, localP) # Step 2 return coordsP - projOnNormal # Step 3 # --------------------------------------------------------------------- # Given an atom object or an array object this function computes the # perpendicular distance from that point to the least squares plane. def distanceToPlane(self, atomORarray): return linalg.norm(self.getCoords(atomORarray) - self.projectionOnPlane(atomORarray)) # -------------------------------------------------------------------- # Given two atom objects or array objects this function computes # the angle between the line joining these two points and the # projection of this line in the LS plane. def angleToPlane(self, a _ atomORarray, b _ atomORarray): # Compute the coordinates: a _ coords = self.getCoords(a _ atomORarray) b _ coords = self.getCoords(b _ atomORarray) distBTWNcoords = linalg.norm(a _ coords - b _ coords) a _ coordsProj = self.projectionOnPlane(a _ atomORarray) b _ coordsProj = self.projectionOnPlane(b _ atomORarray) distBTWNprojections = linalg.norm(a _ coordsProj - b _ coordsProj) return arccos(distBTWNprojections/distBTWNcoords)*180/numpy.pi#===================================================================# Least Squares Plane CLASSclass LeastSquaresPlane(Planes): def __init__(self, atoms_L): self.atoms_L = atoms_L self.ce ntroid = self.centroid() # Vectors providing local frame of reference: self.localAxes_L = [] # The normal to the plane will be localAxes_L[0]: self.normalToPlane = None
164 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics # List of atom coordinates relative to centroid: coords _ L = [] for a in self.atoms _ L: co ords _ L.app end(array(a.coord()) - self.centroid) # Use coords _ L to build matrixA: matrixA = array(coords _ L) eigvals, eigvecs = linalg.eig(dot(matrixA.transpose(), matrixA)) # Sort the eigenvalues to get the needed eigenvector: ind = eigvals.argsort() # Important: We have to extract eigenvectors # from the transpose of eigvecs! We consider the # first eigenvector to be the normal to the plane # and (in the coordinate system set up at centroid), # it will be the z-axis. The other two eigenvectors # will be passed back to give us the unit vectors # defining the x-axis and y-axis. # They are kept in self.localAxes_L (just in case # they are needed for some other application). for i in range(3): self.localAxes_L.append(eigvecs.transpose()[ind[i]]) self.normalToPlane = self.localAxes _ L[0] Planes. __init__(self, self.centroid, self.normalToPlane) # -------------------------------------------------------------------- # Function to compute the centroid of the atoms in the list. d e f c e n t r o i d( s e l f ): sum = zeros(3, float) for a in self.atoms _ L: sum += a.coord() return sum/float(len(self.atoms _ L))Reviewing the code for the initialization function, we see that the list of atom objects is established in self.atoms _ L and the centroid is computed. When the eigenvalue problem is solved, we will get a new frame of reference with origin established at the centroid. e axes for the frame of reference will be kept in self.localAxes _ L. For ease of reference we will put the rst axis (index 0) into the variable named self. normalToPlane. We consider this normal to the plane to be the z axis and so the plane itself will have a coordinate system dened by the x and y axes stored in self.localAxes _ L[1] and self.localAxes _ L[2]. ese are kept as class attributes because they may be needed by some other applications that require an orthonormal basis set with origin at the centroid.

Algorithms Dealing with Angles ◾ 165 e temporary variable coords _ L stores a list of atomic coordi-nates each one being the PDB coordinates shied so that the origin of their new frame of reference is at the centroid. ese coordinates are assembled into an array referenced as matrixA. is gives us the matrix A needed in Equation 4.32. e script then forms the product AAT and solves the eigenvalue problem. We extract eigenvectors from the trans-pose of eigvecs and store them in self.localAxes _ L as promised earlier.e methods projectionOnPlane, distanceToPlane, and angle-ToPlane follow the implementation details described earlier. Note that, for the sake of convenience, these methods will take arguments that are either simple arrays or Chimera atom objects. e function getCoords checks the Python type of these objects and returns the PDB coordinates for either case. Note that an object instantiated from this class will not produce any visual representation in the Chimera window, but the object can be used in conjunction with additional code to draw planar objects such as disks.4.6 EXERCISESe following exercises will deal with angle calculations and will include problems that incorporate the various classes covered in this chapter. 1. In particular, the components of the GUI must work together to manage the state settings of the various widgets during the user interactions. Consequently, the complexity of the logic is more signicant than that seen in Exercise 13 (for example, widgets are enabled and disabled depending on activities related to the Accept button). Furthermore, we will be using this dialog as a starting point for a more capable dialog dealing with rotamers (see Chapter 7).REFERENCES[BR98] R. Bau, D. C. Rees, D. M. Kurtz, R. A. Scott, H. 181Chapter 5Structure Overlap and Alignment5.1 MOTIVATIONStructure comparison deals with various algorithms that analyze two or more proteins in an eort to describe structural similarities. Why should we want to do this? e primary goal is to understand functionality, but this is a very complicated endeavor. In Chapter 1, we presented various behavioral aspects related to a protein’s structure. On reviewing these points, we see that protein structure must provide for the following “life cycle functionalities”:• Uniqueness: e peptide chains must fold into a particular low-energy conformation that has the desired biological utility. e protein should not have other competitive low-energy conformations that are characterized by biological dysfunction.• Stability: Once the protein is in its folded state, it must retain that folded state even though some changes in conformation may occur due to exibility requirements.• Interaction: e nal conformation must have an interaction capa-bility, for example, a ligand binding site or a surface for protein–protein interactions. is capability may also include the exibility noted in the previous list item.
182 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques • Degradation: In many cases, the structure should be degradable so that the protein is conveniently disassembled, its constituent amino acids used in the construction of other proteins.Unfortunately, each of these functional behaviors is generally dicult to relate to structure. In some cases, the mechanism of a binding site may be thoroughly studied in a drug design project and the interactions of the drug with the binding site are known with high precision. e conforma-tional changes of a protein when it interacts with a ligand are one of the motivations for structural comparisons.However, in most situations the connection between function and structure is much more dicult to assess and we must rely on observation in lieu of theory. In other words, when a new protein is being studied we typically compare its structure with that of similar proteins in an attempt to draw inferences about its behavior based on the known behavior of the other proteins in the same category.But how do we organize this categorization? Since the final struc-ture must satisfy all of the aforementioned requirements, evolution-ary processes will have produced structures that attempt to optimize fitness criteria with multiple objectives. Since a particular protein strives to satisfy multiple objectives, it is very difficult to determine the structural properties that are most important to its functionality. For example, do we focus on the configuration of atom types in a binding site or do we stress the organization of secondary structural elements that lead to the stability of a hydrophobic core? The question has no definitive answer.As an example, consider the overlap of 1MBN and 1JEB, both illustrated in Chapter 1, Figure 1.26. If we focus on this pair of proteins, it seems that the region of highest sequence conservation corresponds to the 16 residues at the very beginning of the protein. is is a helix that does not seem to be directly related to the heme binding site. e region does contain the sixth amino acid that leads to sickle-cell anemia if it mutates to valine in hemoglobin, but this is only a single residue. One may speculate that there seems to be some additional critical functionality that is being preserved by this helix. To investigate this issue more completely, it would be neces-sary to consider other proteins in the globin family.In summary, we do structural comparisons for various reasons: we may wish to study the conformational change that is seen in an interaction 
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 183 between a protein and another molecule. We may also study the geometry of binding sites and seek to understand the chemical mechanism of that interaction. e other issues (uniqueness, stability, and degradation) are somewhat more dicult to assess. We understand that a hydrophobic core is necessary for stability, but understanding the attainment of all three objectives and its relation to the consecutive arrangement of amino acids in a peptide chain is a major challenge. In other words, we do not have algorithms that can examine a primary sequence and then accurately pre-dict whether the peptide would fold to a unique and stable conformation that could be enzymatically degraded at a later time. To understand or predict a protein’s behavior, we must consider its membership in a fam-ily of proteins with similar structure. erefore, we are concerned with the structural dierences between a given protein and other proteins that have an evolutionary relationship with it. In particular, what has been structurally conserved?We may also study the structural plasticity of a family. is topic deals with the degree of structural variability seen in a family. It seems that some families show more acceptance of such diversity than do others. Algorithms that deal with multiple structure alignments are important for these investigations. Experiments such as those reported in [VB96] and [SB06] have demonstrated the eect that amino acid insertions have had on stability and the tendency to maintain local helix formation by propagating the eects of the insertion into a bend or loop. Another inter-esting discussion about structural plasticity and its relation to evolution may be found in [MO78].Finally, it should be mentioned that proteins can assume similar folds even though there is no obvious evolutionary relationship among them. ese analogs may be the result of a limited number of congurations available when packing helices and sheets to form a hydrophobic core (see [OJ03]).Research in structural comparison seems to reect the wide variety of concerns and viewpoints on the topic. To get some perspectives on this, the reader may consult [BO96] written by Brown, Orengo, and Taylor. is paper suggests a systematic nomenclature that helps to organize the diverse topics contained in approximately 50 or so research papers writ-ten on protein structure comparison methodologies prior to 1996. Since then, considerably more papers have been written with a corresponding increase in the range of comparison methods.
184 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques 5.2 INTRODUCTION5.2.1 Specifying the ProblemTo understand the wide range of topics related to structural comparison, we list some of the attributes that characterize a study of the problem:• Level of detail: A comparison study is done at a particular resolution or level of structural detail. Comparison algorithms may deal with the positions of various types of structure:• Individual atoms• e locations of residues (specied by coordinates of the alpha carbon atoms or beta carbon atoms or the center of mass of the side chains)• e position and orientation of secondary structural elements (e.g., treating helices as cylinders)• e similarity of folds at the tertiary structure level• Physicochemical properties: e quantication of similarity may only involve the relative positions of structural entities in three-dimensional (3D) space, or it may also include additional descriptive attributes that are important for some application. For example, an atom may be categorized with a physicochemical label that describes it as being a hydrogen bond donor, being a hydrogen bond acceptor, having a posi-tive charge or a negative charge, or being hydrophobic. Hydrophobicity or hydrophilicity may also be assigned to entire residues. Depending on the objectives of the comparison, a property may be associated with a point in space or it may be spread out over some portion of a surface constructed around the underlying atoms.• Extent of comparison: e comparison may be global, dealing with the entire protein, or more local, being restricted in some fashion. Various types of restriction may be applied to limit the comparison to a protein domain, a contiguous subsequence of amino acids, or the subset of atoms in a binding pocket.• Number of proteins: A comparison study may involve one, two, or several proteins. In the case of a single protein, the study typi-cally compares the change in atom positions when the protein changes its conformation. Good examples of this would be the 
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 185 conformational change associated with an allosteric movement or the shi of side chains in a binding pocket during ligand docking. When two proteins are involved, the objectives of the study usually depend on how dierent the proteins are in sequence composition. A simple case would be the study of conformational change due to the substitution of one or two residues. A more complicated com-parison would deal with two proteins that have dierent lengths and perhaps very little sequence identity when a sequence align-ment is performed. In the latter case, it is typical that the proteins have a homologous relationship and the study is designed to assess the extent of structural similarity that has been conserved in an evolutionary descent. Studies involving many proteins are oen designed to evaluate similarities in proteins that are closely related as established by sequence alignment. Possible objectives include an analysis of all proteins in the group with the intention of char-acterizing atom types in a binding site (looking for similarities) or an analysis of evolutionary divergence (this time looking for dif-ferences). Conversely, studies may establish similarities in proteins due to structural conformations only independent of sequence similarity. If the sequence similarity is then found to be very low, it is reasonable evidence that structural similarity is due to evolu-tionary convergence.ese points should convince the reader that there are many approaches to structural comparison. In fact, an extended treatment of the various methodologies that are used could form the subject matter of an entire book. To set a limit on this extensive material, we continue with the dis-cussion of only a few comparison algorithms.5.3 TECHNIQUES FOR STRUCTURAL COMPARISONSuppose we are given two proteins that we will designate as P and Q. Algorithms for structural comparison of P and Q generally fall into two broad categories: 1. Superposition algorithms: ese algorithms try to maximize some type of overlap of two proteins in 3D space. e overlap may deal with all the residues of the protein or some subset of the residues. If subsets of residues are being considered, then there must be some specication of atom pairs that will participate in the overlap.


===== [49/99] chapter-49.html =====

186 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques 2. Algorithms that compare geometric relationships within a protein: We start by dening a set of relationships between elements in the protein P. For example, we might calculate for each alpha carbon in P a set of distances to all the other alpha carbons in P. Each dis-tance is seen as a relationship between the two atoms. en, we do the same calculations for Q. e comparison algorithm then com-pares the relationships within P with those within Q. ere are many variations on this theme: instead of atoms, we can use fragments or secondary structure components such as helices or strands. Instead of all such elements, concentrate on only those that are nearby or within the same domain. e strategies for comparing the relation-ships can also vary from one algorithm to the next.5.4 SCORING SIMILARITIES AND OPTIMIZING SCORESIn all comparison algorithms, we try to evaluate some successful demon-stration of similarity. All comparison algorithms must clearly specify the following: 1. Score evaluation: Given two proteins P and Q, a comparison algo-rithm may reveal several dierent possibilities, each demonstrating the similarity between P and Q. For example, there may be several ways to overlap two proteins in 3D space. Consequently, it is neces-sary to have some measure of similarity. is evaluation of similarity is usually called a score. 2. Score optimization: Typically, there will be a strategy to optimize this score. For example, the algorithm will derive a best structural overlap, or compute some type of correspondence for the atoms of P and Q, so that the score will be optimized.When we discuss the various comparison algorithms, both of these points are covered.5.5 SUPERPOSITION ALGORITHMS5.5.1 OverviewA frequent concern is whether two proteins have the same or a very simi-lar structure. An assessment of this can be done by attempting to super-impose the two proteins in 3D space. e proteins may have the same residues, or they may be fairly similar (e.g., homologs).
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 187 ere are various situations when such a comparison would be calculated:• e proteins may have the same sequence but dier in conformation. e dierence might be due to a backbone exing of the protein or may be due to a change in conformation when a ligand is present in a binding site.• e proteins may have almost the same sequence. For example, a mutation has caused an amino acid to change. What is the eect on conformation?• e proteins may have more extensive dierences in their sequences, but it is possible that they nonetheless show a lot of similarity in conformation. e question is as follows: How similar are these conformations?• e proteins may have considerable dierences in their sequences, but it is possible that they share similar structure in various regions. We would want to compare these similar regions. Recall that struc-ture is more conserved than sequence.In some situations, a transformation of coordinates is necessary to compare dierent conformations of the same protein because the coordi-nates are derived from protein data bank (PDB) les that assume dierent orientations of the protein. For example, 1IZI, 1MSN, and 1EBW all deal with the same protein, that is, HIV protease. A casual comparison of the coordinates of the rst two atoms in each le gives the following:1IZI:ATOM 1 N PRO A 1 -12.600 38.218 3.719ATOM 2 CA PRO A 1 -12.444 38.367 2.2441MSN:ATOM 1 N PRO A 1 0.421 40.709 18.682ATOM 2 CA PRO A 1 -0.422 39.511 18.9051EBW:ATOM 1 N PRO A 1 29.101 40.309 5.484ATOM 2 CA PRO A 1 30.105 39.343 4.986So, it is clear that the PDB does not put proteins in any standard orientation. is was also obvious if you viewed both conformations of the MscS pro-tein (2OAU and 2VV5) in Exercise 8 of Chapter 4.
188 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques 5.5.2 Characterizing the Superposition AlgorithmIn this algorithm, we consider the structural comparison of two pro-teins that we designate as P and Q. We will not consider any physico-chemical properties, just atomic coordinates. So, at this level of detail it is expected that the input to the algorithm will be 3D coordinates of the atoms involved in the comparison. The algorithm will require us to specify a set called an equivalence. This is a set of matching atom pairs that are to be brought into 3D superposition. Each pair will designate an atom in P and its corresponding atom in Q. The algorithm will then find the translation and rotation operations that minimize the sum of squares of the distances between the atoms in the pairs.In the description of the algorithm, we assume that this equivalence is generated using some technique that is appropriate to the applica-tion that uses the structure overlap algorithm. Various approaches can be listed:• If the proteins have the same sequence, then the equivalence can be generated simply by matching up the corresponding atoms. en, we can extract a subset of these pairs that is appropriate to the needs of the study. For example, we might deal solely with the alpha carbons in the backbone or some part of it.• If the proteins have a dierent sequence, then a sequence alignment between P and Q can identify matching pairs of residues and then the corresponding alpha carbon atoms can be used to dene the equivalence.In some cases, the sequence similarity is so vague that it is quite dif-cult to generate the equivalence set by doing a sequence alignment. In these cases, a dierent algorithm is required, one that attempts to do a structural comparison using coordinate information with little or no help from a sequence alignment.Before going any further, it is necessary to settle on some terminol-ogy. e terms overlap and superposition are used interchangeably and we assume that the computation that performs the superposition has access to a set of atom pairings that are used to guide an optimal overlap. For pro-tein structures (this will not apply to protein sequences), we use the term structural alignment when dealing with an algorithm that strives to nd 
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 189 common structure in two proteins with little or no reliance on the protein sequences involved.5.5.3 Formal Problem DescriptionAlthough dierent choices could be made for the atoms in an equiv-alence set, we assume that the superposition algorithm deals with alpha carbon atoms. We are given two sequences of alpha carbon 3D coordinates: 11PpQqiiPiiQ{} {}==() ()== (5.1)where |P| and |Q| are the number of residues in protein P and protein Q, respectively.We start with a formal description of the equivalence set followed by the notion of an alignment. Selection of λ must be made with due attention to two issues that have not yet been addressed: 1. e rotation matrix must not introduce a reection that changes chirality. a. Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 197 Why does this work? e determinant of R has changed sign because the determinant of the diagonal matrix has the value −1. Now it is a proper rotation. However, the value of H is s1 + s2 − s3 and so it is not as large as s1+ s2 + s3. Both sets of coordinates were given to the Chimera molecular modeling system, which then displayed a visualization showing the overlap of sec-ondary structures in rounded ribbon form. Only one of the proteins pro-vided the heme structure.5.5.5 SummarySteps for the 3D overlap of proteins P and Q are as follows: 1. Determine the subsequences of alpha carbons to be used in the 3D overlap: ,,,,,,1212()()()()==() () ()() () ()αα αββ βMP pp pMQ qq qNN (5.36) 2. Calculate the centroids p(c) and q(c) using Equation 5.5.FIGURE 5.1 Structure overlap of 1MBN and 1JEB. (Courtesy of Shuo [Alex] Xiang, a former graduate student at the University of Waterloo, Ontario, Canada.)
198 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques 3. Shi the proteins so that the centroids are at the origin (using Equation 5.6). We are then working with x(i) and y(i) coordinate sets. 4. Calculate the C matrix as dened by Equation 5.20, and compute its SVD. is gives C = USVT. If necessary, reorder the singular values so that s1 > s2 > s3. 5. Compute the rotation matrix =TRUV (5.37) 6. Check to see if det (R) = 1. If this determinant is negative, then we must redene the rotation matrix to be ()=−diag 1,1, 1TRU V (5.38) 7. Apply the rotation matrix to the x(i) coordinates.5.5.6 Measuring Overlap5.5.6.1 Calculation of the Root Mean Square DeviationAer the rotation matrix is applied to the x(i) coordinates, we can com-pute the squared distance between each x(i) point and its corresponding y(i) point: =−() () ()22dRxyiii (5.39)Having done this, we can compute the root mean square deviation (RMSD) for that set of corresponding points: ∑()=()=RMSD ,21PQdNiiN (5.40)One should be careful about the interpretation of this calculation. When P and Q represent dierent conformations of the same protein and N is the number of atoms of this protein, RMSD represents the conventional measure of the similarity of P and Q as measured by the degree of overlap. If the RMSD is 0 (or very close to 0), we regard P and Q as being identical. If the RMSD is between 1 and 3 Å, then we consider P and Q to be very similar. If the RMSD is over 3 Å, then we typically consider P and Q to have similarity that is low or not present.
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 199 If P and Q have the same lengths but some dierences in primary sequence, then we cannot compute d(i)2 for atoms in corresponding but dierent residues. is is true because it is impossible to get a correspon-dence between the atoms in these residues. In this case, one may compute the RMSD with respect to the alpha carbons only. is is still reasonable in that we would have an evaluation of the similarity of the backbone con-formations of P and Q.If P and Q have dierent lengths, then the RMSD calculation can still be applied to the atoms in the equivalence set. In this case, one must bear in mind that it is not measuring overlap across the entire extents of P and Q.5.6 A SIMPLE SCRIPT TO DO STRUCTURAL SUPERIMPOSITIONStructural superimposition requires that we translate and rotate one struc-ture so as to get maximal overlap with another structure, the goal being to minimize the distances between particular atom pairs. is is the simple scenario in which we know which atom pairs to use and these pairings do not change as the algorithm progresses.Before discussing scripts that can do structural superimposition, it must be emphasized that Chimera has menu invocations that provide a sophisticated level of structural alignment capabilities. If you are working with only a few protein structures that require a conventional approach to structural alignment, then it is much easier to use a menu invocation and thus avoid the extra work of dealing with a script. So, why bother with a script? Here are some reasons:• e script may be needed within a larger program that needs a struc-tural superimposition capability.• Your application might need to carry out several, perhaps thousands of, structural superimpositions.• Your algorithm is using some alignment strategy that is dierent from that oered by Chimera.Our script to accomplish structural superimposition has been imple-mented as a class called Overlapper. Instantiation of the class creates an Overlapper object that has data structures tracking atomic coordi-nates, centroids, and the rotation matrix involved in the superimposition. 
200 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques When an Overlapper object is instantiated, it is given a Chimera molecule object designated as mol in the __init__ function (e.g., a protein molecule object):class Overlapper(object): def __init__(self, mol): self.molecule = mol self.atomCoords_D = {} # Overlap list for this molecule (molecule P to be moved): self.overlapCoordsP_L = [] # Overlap list for target molecule (P moved to target Q): self.overlapCoordsQ_L = [] self.newOverlapCoordsP_L = [] self.centroidP = zeros(3) # Centroid of overlapCoordsP_L self.centroidQ = zeros(3) # Centroid of overlapCoordsQ_L self.rotationMatrix = zeros((3,3)) for a in self.molecule.atoms: # Saving original coordinates: self.atomCoords_D[a] = a.coord()e moveToOverlap method within the Overlapper class can be used to translate and rotate the molecule so that it achieves optimal overlap with another molecule when moveToOverlap is given a list of atom pairings. e undoMove method allows us to move mol back to its original position in the 3D space. By using both moveToOverlap and undoMove methods, we can cause mol to overlap with any number of target molecules one aer another in a sequential fashion. e following is the code for the moveToOverlap and undoMove methods: def moveToOverlap(self, pairs_L): # Get lists of coordinates: # We assume the first entry of a pair refers to the self molecule and the second entry refers to the target protein. for pair in pairs_L: self.overlapCoordsP_L.append(getCoords(pair[0])) self.overlapCoordsQ_L.append(getCoords(pair[1])) self.centroidP = sum(self.overlapCoordsP_L)/len(pairs_L) self.centroidQ = sum(self.overlapCoordsQ_L)/len(pairs_L) # Compute shifted cverlap coords (centroids at origin): shiftedOverlapCoordsP_L = [] shiftedOverlapCoordsQ_L = []


===== [52/99] chapter-52.html =====

Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 201 for i in range(len(pairs_L)): shiftedOverlapCoordsP_L.append(self.overlapCoordsP_L[i] - self.centroidP) shiftedOverlapCoordsQ_L.append(self.overlapCoordsQ_L[i] - self.centroidQ) self.rotationMatrix = computeRotationMatrix \ (shiftedOverlapCoordsP_L, shiftedOverlapCoordsQ_L) # Apply the transformation to all atoms in the molecule: for a in self.molecule.atoms: newCoords = dot(self.rotationMatrix, array(a.coord())-self.centroidP)+self.centroidQ a.setCoord(Point(newCoords[0], newCoords[1], newCoords[2])) def undoMove(self): for a in self.molecule.atoms: a.setCoord(self.atomCoords_D[a])e operation of undoMove is quite straightforward, but move To Overlap requires some additional explanation. e function get Coords increases the usefulness of moveToOverlap by allowing the atom pairings to be expressed in any one of three dierent ways: an entry in a pair can be a Chimera atom object or its 3D coordinates expressed as a Point object or as a three-component array. Details of its implementation can be found in the Overlapper class (available from the book’s website) accessed viafrom StructBio. StructureOverlap.overlapper import OverlapperOnce all the atom pairings have been used to dene the two lists shiftedOverlapCoordsP _ L and shiftedOverlapCoordsQ _ L, generation of the rotation matrix is accomplished by invoking the func-tion computeRotationMatrix. Subsequent statements apply the rotation matrix to get the translations and rotation of mol to achieve the optimal overlap. Note that our mathematical derivation of the overlap algorithm involved a translation of both molecules so that their centroids were moved to the origin of the 3D space. In the implementation, this is not done because we do not want to shi the atoms in the target protein (just those in mol). Consequently, mol is shied and rotated and then it is translated by a distance that is determined by the centroid of the target molecule.
202 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques Code for computing the rotation matrix is as follows:def computeRotationMatrix(coordsP_L, coordsQ_L): coordsP_A = zeros((3,len(coordsP_L)), float) coordsQ_A = zeros((3,len(coordsQ_L)), float) for ix in range(len (coordsP_L)): coordsP_A[:, ix] = coordsP_L[ix] coordsQ_A[:, ix] = coordsQ_L[ix] c_A = dot(coordsQ_A, coordsP_A.transpose()) u, s, vt = linalg.svd(c_A) # NOTE: we are assuming that s has # singular values in descending order. rotMatrix = dot(u,vt) if linalg.det(rotMatrix) < 0: rotMatrix = dot (u*array([1.0, 1.0, -1.0]), vt) return rotMatrixis is a reasonably straightforward implementation of the steps 4, 5, 6, and 7 of the summary in Section 5.5.5. If the molecules to be overlapped have an obvious set of atom pairings, then it is easy to generate a list of these pairs to produce an optimal overlap (see Exercise 1 at the end of this chapter).5.7 PROTEIN SEQUENCE ALIGNMENTUp to this point, we have been assuming that structural overlap is preceded by some algorithm that determines the pairs of atoms (see Equations 5.3) that are to be structurally overlapped. If the two proteins have the same sequence, then this is a trivial exercise. If they are very dif-ferent, then the pairing can be rather dicult. Although there are various purely structural approaches to this problem, a simple strategy is to start with a local alignment of the primary sequences. We do not cover this in minute detail because the information is readily available from other sources such as [DE98].Our objective is to derive a short (hopefully simple) script that pro-vides a sequence alignment class that can be readily embedded into other scripts that need this type of preprocessing prior to a structural overlap. In eect, we are striving to initiate a structural alignment by starting with a sequence alignment. Such an alignment is really an attempt to assess sequence similarity.
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 203 As an example of similarity, consider the following two amino acid sequences:P = WATERMANMIKE and Q = MERMANETHELTo show the similarity of these two sequences, we can write them as two rows with vertical bars between matching letters.P = WATERMANMIKE- ||||| :|Q = --MERMANETHELese two rows represent a global alignment of the two sequences. As just noted, we want a local alignment, but we rst discuss global alignments because they are simpler to explain.Denition: An optimal global alignment of two sequences P and Q is obtained by writing the two sequences in such a way as to maximize the occurrence of matching pairs of letters, one in P and the other in Q. Gaps (written as dashes) may be introduced into either sequence or at either end of a sequence in such a way as to have both strings of equal length and to facilitate the largest numbers of matches.5.7.1 Partial MatchesAnother consideration in the generation of an alignment is that amino acids may be accepted as a pair even though they do not match identically. is approach takes into account the biochemical properties of the amino acids. For example, D (aspartate) and E (glutamate) are both acidic amino acids and so they are good candidates for a partial match. Other partial matches would be biologically justied: hydrophobic amino acids (W, F, Y, L, I, V, M, A), and basic amino acids (K, R, H). For long sequences, it may be possible to generate many dierent opti-mal alignments, each having the maximal number of exact matches. When partial matches contribute to the score of an optimal alignment, the analysis becomes more complicated because we must decide how much they contribute. For example, should the score increase if the align-ment brings in three partial matches at the expense of foregoing one exact match? To answer such questions, researchers have constructed substitu-tion matrices that essentially dene the score contributions of both exact and partial matches.
204 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques Figure 5.2, adapted from http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Class/FieldGuide/BLOSUM62.txt, gives the BLOSUM62 (see [HH92]) version of such a sub-stitution matrix. ere are other BLOSUM matrices with suxes such as 45, 50, 80, and 90. e BLOSUM62 matrix is oen used as the stan-dard matrix when doing ungapped alignments, whereas BLOSUM50 is oen used for gapped alignments. In general, BLOSUM80 works best for sequences that show less divergence, whereas BLOSUM45, at the other end of the application scale, is used for sequences that show more evolu-tionary divergence. e score contribution of an exact match can be found on the main diagonal. ese entries have integer values ranging from 4 to 11 and take into account the relative frequency of occurrence of an amino acid. For example, the score contribution made by an exact match for W (tryptophan) is 11, which is the highest value since tryptophan has the lowest frequency of occurrence. In general, the entries of the substitution matrix are based on issues other than simple frequency of occurrence. More precisely, it describes the frequency of observed changes of charac-ters in related protein segments or blocks that have been conserved in an evolutionary descent.ARNDCQEGHILKMFPSTWYVA4−1−2−20−1−10−2−1−1−1−1−2−110−3−20R−150−2−310−20−3−22−1−3−2−1−1−3−2−3N−2061−30001−3−30−2−3−210−4−2−3D−2−216−302−1−1−3−4−1−3−3−10−1−4−3−3C0−3−3−39−3−4−3−3−1−1−3−1−2−3−1−1−2−2−1Q−1100−352−20−3−210−3−10−1−2−1−2E−1002−425−20−3−31−2−3−10−1−3−2−2G0−20−1−3−2−26−2−4−4−2−3−3−20−2−2−3−3H−201−1−300−28−3−3−1−2−1−2−1−2−22−3L−1−2−3−4−1−2−3−4−324−220−3−2−1−2−1−1I−1−3−3−3−1−3−3−4−342−310−3−2−1−3−13K−120−1−311−2−1−3−25−1−3−10−1−3−2−2M−1−1−2−3−10−2−3−212−150−2−1−1−1−11F−2−3−3−3−2−3−3−3−100−306−4−2−213−1P−1−2−2−1−3−1−1−2−2−3−3−1−2−47−1−1−4−3−2S1−110−1000−1−2−20−1−2−141−3−2−2T0−10−1−1−1−1−2−2−1−1−1−1−2−115−2−20W−3−3−4−4−2−2−3−2−2−3−2−3−11−4−3−2112−3Y−2−2−2−3−2−1−2−32−1−1−2−13−3−2−227−1V0−3−3−3−1−2−2−3−331−21−1−2−20−3−14FIGURE 5.2 e BLOSUM62 substitution matrix.
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 205 5.7. 2 G apsConsider the following two alignments:Alignment #1:M I C H A E L S W A T E R M A N | | | | : | | | | |- E T H - E L M - - - E R M A N0 -3 -1 8 0 5 4 -1 0 0 0 5 5 5 4 6Alignment #2:M I C H A E L S W A T E R M A N | | | | : | | | | |- E T H - E L - - M - E R M A N0 -3 -1 8 0 5 4 0 0 -1 0 5 5 5 4 6e numbers in each column of the alignment are the score contribu-tions given by the BLOSUM62 matrix. e score for the entire alignment is simply the sum of these contributed scores. is means that alignment #1 has a score of 37 while alignment #2 also has a score of 37. However, most alignment programs will oer alignment #1 as the better alignment because alignment #2 initiates a gap four times, whereas the former does so only three times. To support this choice, it is possible for the alignment score to incorporate a term that leads to an extra penalty whenever a new run of gap symbols is started.Let us consider why a gap appears. A gap is a sequence of one or more spaces drawn as dashes in the alignment display. Each space can represent an insertion or a deletion. For example, the space below the rst A repre-sents either of the following: 1. e insertion of an A into the rst string, which would have been MICHELSWATERMAN prior to the insertion 2. e deletion of some letter that was originally between H and E of ETHELMERMANVery oen, we do not have enough evidence to determine which scenario occurred and so either possibility is acceptable and the space is referred to as an indel (insertion or deletion). e key fact is that indels are assumed to be much less likely than substitutions from a biological perspective. Consequently, their initiation and extension lead to the addition of nega-tive contributions (penalties) in the score computation. ese penalties


===== [53/99] chapter-53.html =====

206 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques have been assessed using empirical observations gathered from several examples of homologous proteins that have been previously aligned.5.7.3 SummaryWe are now in a position to summarize the main points of the mathemati-cal model for global sequence alignment.Given two strings representing the residues in two proteins P and Q, an optimal global alignment seeks to demonstrate maximal similarity by writing one string beneath the other so that residues in columns either identically or partially match. e objective of attaining maximal similar-ity must address the following issues:• Gaps (written as dashes) may be introduced into either sequence or at either end of a sequence in such a way as to have both strings of equal length and to facilitate the largest numbers of matches.• Although gaps may be introduced, we attempt to do this in a par-simonious fashion and, so, in the formulation of the problem we reduce the score of an alignment by introducing a gap penalty.5.7.4 Computatione optimal global alignment problem can be solved using dynamic pro-gramming. is solution of the problem was done in 1970 by Needleman and Wunsch (see [NW70]). A modern computer scientist would see this algorithm as an application of dynamic programming, a strategy that was pioneered by Richard Bellman in 1954 (see [Be54]). A review of dynamic programming is provided in Appendix E.In the design of a dynamic programming algorithm, the most impor-tant issue is the specication of a subproblem (see Appendix E). We know that a subproblem should be smaller in some sense and capable of contrib-uting to the solution of a larger problem.It is possible to dene subproblems that have too many degrees of free-dom in their specications. For example, we might state that a subproblem should be the global alignment of two substrings extracted from P and Q, that is, P[i..j] and Q[k..m], where the notation [i..j] represents the string of contiguous characters between and including character positions i and j. is subproblem would be parameterized by four integers, i, j, k, and m. is approach would involve evaluation of all cells in a four-dimensional array, and this would be an extremely costly evaluation for lengthy strings.
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 207 In fact, we need not go to such an extreme when specifying substrings. It is shown in Lemma 5.1 that the selected substrings can be prexes of the given strings.Denition: the ith prex of a string X denoted by Xi is the substring of X consisting of the rst i characters of X, that is, ,,,,12 1=−Xxxxii ,1=−xX xiii Note the use of comma-separated lowercase letters to repre-sent consecutive characters within the prex. With this agreement about notation, we proceed with the specication in Section 5.7.4.1.5.7.4.1 Subproblem Specicatione dynamic programming subproblem will be parameterized by two arguments i and j, and its solution will be the optimal global alignment of the two prexes Pi and Qj.5.7.4.2 Scoring AlignmentsWe score an alignment by adding the scores derived from each column in the alignment. Suppose that aer adding dashes we get an alignment that involves mij columns when we do a global alignment of Pi and Qj. en, the score for this alignment will be given by ∑()()==,1NW ijskckmij (5.41)where ()skc is the score for the kth column in the alignment. We have used the notation NW (i, j) to emphasize that this score is for the global alignment algorithm due to Needleman and Wunsch. Based on the earlier discussion concerning gaps and partial matches in Section 5.7.2, we can dene the column score using ()()=′′−,inacolumn with amatchin acolumnwithadashskBp qdckk (5.42)In this equation, the quantity d represents the penalty that is given for the occurrence of a gap. e gap penalty d is a positive quantity, so the pres-ence of any gap in the alignment will cause this penalty to be subtracted from the total alignment score. We use the notations ′pkand ′qk to indicate the residues of Pi and Qj that appear in the kth column when the global 
208 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques alignment is written out, complete with dashes. e function B (row_ residue, column_residue) is used to extract an entry from the row and column of the substitution matrix that is being used for the alignment. In Section 5.7.1, we used the BLOSUM62 matrix as an example of such a substitution matrix.5.7.4.3 Suitability of the Subproblemere are undoubtedly many ways to dene a subproblem, many of them ill-tted to the needs of a dynamic programming algorithm. However, our specication of a subproblem is appropriate for the dynamic program-ming algorithm. is is demonstrated by proving that there is a relation-ship between the subproblem specied as the optimal global alignment of prexes Pi and Qj and other optimal global alignment subproblems that work on shorter prex strings. is gives us a recursive evaluation lead-ing to the computation of the full solution: the full problem depends on smaller subproblems, each of which depends on even smaller subprob-lems, and so on until eventually we have subproblems so small that they are trivial to solve. We need the following lemma:Lemma 5.1Let Pi and Qj be prexes of the strings P and Q, respectively. Ifwe considerthe optimal global alignment of Pi and Qj, there are three possible cases when describing the appearance of the last column in the alignment: 1. Character pi matches character qj in the optimal global alignment, which can then be drawn as follows: −−,,11PpQqiijj (5.43) Claim: in this case, the matching of characters seen prior to pi and qj is, in fact, the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj – 1. 2. Character pi does not participate in any match, that is, the optimal global alignment can be drawn as follows: −−,,1PpQiij (5.44)
Structure Overlap and Alignment ◾ 209 Claim: in this case, the matching of characters seen prior to pi is the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj. 3. Character qj does not participate in any match, that is, the optimal global alignment can be drawn as follows: −−,,1PQqijj (5.45)Claim: in this case, the matching of characters seen prior to qj is the optimal global alignment of Pi and Qj – 1.Proof of claim for case (a):Assume that the matching of Pi – 1 and Qj – 1 as seen in case (a) is not the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj – 1. en, suppose that we replaced this alignment with the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj– 1. is will raise the score associated with the columns prior to the last column. en, by simply appending pi to Pi – 1 and qj to Qj – 1, we would get an alignment with a higher score than that seen in case (a). is is a contradiction because we have already assumed that case (a) represents an optimal global alignment.Proof of claim for case (b):Reasoning along similar lines: suppose that the matching of Pi – 1 and Qj as seen in case (b) is not the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj. en, suppose we replaced this alignment with the optimal global alignment of Pi – 1 and Qj. is will raise the score associated with the columns prior to the last column. en, by simply appending pi to Pi – 1 and writing a dash below it we would get an alignment that is characterized as being case (b) but with a higher score. is is a contradiction because we have already assumed that case (b) represents an optimal global alignment.Proof of claim for case (c):e proof is a simple variation of the proof for case (b).Computation of NW (i, j): Lemma 5.1 is a formal discussion that brings out the relationships among subproblems and serves to justify the formula-tion of our subproblem as the optimal global alignment of Pi and Qj. At the moment, the implications these cases provide for the dependencies among the scores associated with the subproblems are more important for us. Each case in Lemma 5.1 denes a possible relationship between NW (i, j) and the score derived from the solution of some smaller alignment problem.
210 ◾ Computational and Visualization Techniques Case (a):By matching the last characters in each string of residues, the score will be incremented by an amount that is accessed from the substitution matrix: ()() ()=−−+,1,1 ,NW ij NW ij Bpqij (5.46)Case (b):By placing a dash in the bottom row of the alignment, we decrease the score in going from NW (I − 1, j) to NW (i, j): () ()=−−,1,NW ij NW ijd (5.47)Case (c):By placing a dash in the top row of the alignment, we decrease the score in going from NW (i, j − 1) to NW (i, j): () ()=−−,,1NW ij NW ij d (5.48)Considering these possibilities, we see that there are three ways to dene NW (i, j). Since we wish to maximize the alignment score, it makes sense to always choose the option that will maximize NW (i, j). Consequently, we use the following equation for the computation of (),NW ij: ()()()()()=−−+−−−−,max1,1,,1, ,,1NW ijNW ij BpqNW ijdNW ij dij (5.49)We now have enough information about the mathematical model to go through the dynamic programming design steps suggested in Section E.4 of Appendix E. ese steps are summarized as follows: 1. Identication of subproblems: e subproblem will be parameterized by two arguments i and j, and its solution will be the optimal global alignment of the two prexes Pi and Qj.
