===== [71/99] chapter-71.html =====

Residue Networks ◾ 291 function userSpecificationOfChains. ese two protein chains will represent the structural information for the inactive and active states. 2. For each protein, extract a list of standard residues with the require-ment that corresponding entries from the lists must be the same resi-due types. One might think that this is easily accomplished because each protein le represents a dierent state of the same protein. Ideally, both les have the same number of residues and the same set of position numbers to specify the locations of residues in a chain. Unfortunately, this ideal situation might not hold because the inac-tive and active versions of the protein may have been deposited in the PDB by two dierent research laboratories. In fact, it is possible that some parts of a protein chain may simply be missing in one le while being completely intact in the other le! To get around this situation, you should read both les and then do a sequence alignment to get corresponding pairs of residues that have all their structural informa-tion present. Use a simple alignment that relies on identical match-ing. In other words, do not use matching scores such as BLOSUM 62. Consider working with the function SequencesLocalAlignment. 3. Calculate RF(i, j) for all pairs of residues that are in contact. You should not calculate or store values of RF(i, j) when Ri and Rj are not in contact. Since the RF(i, j) values are not to be stored in a 2D array, you will likely use a dictionary or list of tuples each containing values such as: i, j, RF(i, j) for RF(i, j) ≥ T. Note that i and j are the indices for the lists created in the previous step. ey may or may not correspond to the position numbers of a residue in its chain and they may or may not correspond to the indices of a residue in the Chimera residue lists for the proteins (because of the sequence alignment and extraction of corresponding residues done in the previous step).Implementation notes:• Use the parameter settings stipulated in Section 8.3 (d = 5.0, T = 0.3).• e script should execute in time O(n), where n is the number of resi-dues in the protein. So when evaluating residue contact, you should not use a loop within a loop with each loop ranging over all possible residues. Use the Shell class.
292 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformatics• In the calculation of ()−∩CCCijIijAijI, it is natural to use the Set class in Python but you have to be careful. If CijI and CijA contain atom objects, then the intersection will always be empty because Chimera will have dierent atom objects for the two proteins. So the sets should contain integers, each integer representing a tracking identier that uniquely species an atom within a particular resi-due of the list created in Step 2. For example, atom “CA” in the resi-due at some particular index i, for example, i = 22 would be given a unique tracking identier but it would be valid for both the inactive and active residue lists. Of course, similar considerations hold for ()−∩CCCijAijAijI.To complete this exercise, you should have your program compute the RF(i, j) values that are equal to or greater than T and then print them out. Since the RF(i, j) function is symmetric with respect to i and j, you should only print out the RF(i, j) values for which i < j. For example, here are ve lines from the 1HH4 and 1MH1 case:37 PHE 58 THR 0.83338 ASP 57 ASP 0.75037 PHE 59 ALA 0.75036 VAL 57 ASP 0.84621 ILE 37 PHE 0.684Residues are specied by position in the chain of the protein followed by residue type. is output is of limited use, but you can use it to test progress thus far. e main purpose of this exercise is to serve as a pre-amble for the next exercise.8.4.6 Displaying the Residue Network for Rearranged ContactsNote that the list of RF(i, j) values computed in the previous exercise con-tains enough information for you to build a graph, where each node ni corresponds to a residue Ri and there is an edge between node ni and node nj if RF(i, j) ≥ T. In the paper by Daily et al., this graph is considered to be a small-world network and various graph-related operations can be per-formed to assess the properties of the network.** For example, you could extract the subgraph that is induced by all the nodes having degree larger than some given threshold or you could use a depth-rst search to derive all the connected components of the graph.
Residue Networks ◾ 293 Use the Graph, Node, and Edge classes to place a residue network into the display. Start with the import statements:from StructBio.StructureOverlap.overlapper \ import Overlapperfrom StructBio.Utilities.shellNeighbors \ import Shellfrom StructBio.Utilities.miscFunctions \ import userSpecificationOfChains, colorByNamefrom StructBio.Utilities.miscFunctions \ import residueKDhColor, residueFletterickColorfrom StructBio.StructureOverlap.sequencesLocalAlignment \ import SequencesLocalAlignmentfrom StructBio.Graphs.graph \ import Graph, Node, Edgeand do the same three steps as in the previous exercise followed by 4. Work with the Overlapper class to derive an overlap of the inac-tive and active versions of the protein. e sequence alignment done in the previous exercise can be used to get lists of the correspond-ing alpha carbon atoms that will dene the overlap. Since a transi-tion between the inactive and active states is oen accompanied by an extensive conformational change, the overlap may show a poor structural alignment across its entire length. It is better to have a tight structural alignment over a smaller range of residues with the rest of the chains then showing a more extensive movement relative to the tighter overlapped regions. Consequently, it is recommended that the initial overlap operation be followed by a scan that selects a set of alpha carbon pairs that have the shortest interatomic dis-tance (e.g., the rst third of the pairs aer sorting the pairs to get ascending order by interatomic distance). Use these pairs to redo the overlap. 5. Use the list of RF(i, j) values computed in the previous exercise to build a graph, where each node ni corresponds to a residue Ri and there is an edge between node ni and node nj if RF(i, j) ≥ T. Work with the Graph, Node, and Edge classes to construct nodes and edges for the display. Each chain should have its own graph. e nodes should be spheres that are centered over the alpha carbon atoms of the residues in a chain. To provide more visual informa-tion, the radius of a sphere can be made proportional to the number of atoms in the corresponding residue. e color of the sphere can 
294 ◾ Computational Techniques for Structural Bioinformaticsbe determined by using the residueFletterickColor function, which will give it a unique color for each residue type. e spindles that act as links between nodes can be given a radius that is pro-portional to the RF(i, j) value corresponding to that edge. Color the spindle light green if the corresponding contact interface is going to gain atoms in the transition to the other state and color it deep pink if the corresponding contact interface is going to lose atoms in the transition to the other state. 6. ere will be several nodes in the graphs that have either no edges or are members of connected components that are too small to be of interest. is nal step should decompose each graph into a set of connected components each with a size that is at least 3.e Model Panel can be used to selectively determine the visual ele-ments to be shown in the scene. You may wish to streamline this user interaction by designing a GUI that is more appropriate for this appli-cation. Depending on the amount of structural movement in a transi-tion, one graph may look better than the other. Figure 8.8 shows a graph visualization for proteins 1XTU and 1XTT (inactive and active forms of the protein uracil phosphoribosyltransferase). With close inspection, you FIGURE 8.8 (See color insert.) Graph visualization of a contact rearrangement network (1XTU and 1XTT).
Residue Networks ◾ 295 should be able to see the various spindles that have been drawn between nodes that correspond to residues that undergo contact rearrangement in a transition. Note that these residues tend to be close to the ligands and, in fact, represent a reasonable path of rearrangement between the two bind-ing sites helping to elucidate the allosteric phenomenon.REFERENCES[BK07] C. 297Appendix ASimple DialogsA.1 MOTIVATIONIn the initial chapters, most of the exercise scripts require interaction with a user. For the most part, this has been accomplished by the user typing text into the Python Shell and then getting results back in the same window when the script executes a print statement. At times, interaction with the user has been done with the help of a dialog window provided by the tkFileDialog module as shown in Script 2.2 and Script 2.3.e purpose of this appendix is to introduce the reader to scripting techniques that will give her program the ability to launch dialog windows that provide a graphical user interface (GUI). ere are two possible scenarios (depending on how the GUI is designed): 1. A Python script is opened from the Python Shell and subsequently run. While this application is running, it may launch a dialog window that acts as a GUI for the user. Typically, such a GUI gives the user a richer interactive experience than simply interacting with the Python Shell and it can also be used to eliminate any unfortu-nate or confusing input coming from the user. 2. If the GUI dialogs (typically more than one) provide a complete inter-action with the user, then execution of the script can be initiated by the File… Open… menu of the Chimera window. In this case, the Python Shell can be avoided if the script does not execute any print statements, all output from the script being displayed in the main Chimera window or in some dialog window.
298 ◾ Appendix AIn the subsequent sections, we consider the construction of some simple dialogs (implemented via Tkinter) that may be used to improve the more frequent user interactions that would otherwise be done by means of the Python Shell. It is not meant to be a complete lesson covering GUI design principles, and it certainly does not give a complete treatment of Tkinter. More extensive coverage of Tkinter can be found in various Internet sites or you could read Grayson’s book [G05].A.2 MODAL AND MODELESS DIALOGSere are two types of user interface dialogs: modal and modeless. A modal dialog typically requires user input and ideally, execution in the main win-dow will not continue until that interaction has been completed. If the dia-log window has been launched and the user does not wish to proceed with the interrogation, then a Cancel button is usually provided so that an appro-priate resumption of execution can proceed without the information. A modeless dialog window allows the user to interact with both the dialog and the main Chimera window without rst having to close or cancel the dialog.e logic behind a modal dialog is normally simpler than that used for a modeless dialog: the modal dialog is displayed, the information is obtained from the user, the dialog is closed, and execution proceeds with the script that had launched the dialog. By contrast, all permitted interactions with a modeless dialog must have a meaningful relationship with the current state of the executing script associated with the main window. In an eort to properly manage this interaction, the modeless dialog will oen use strategies that prohibit certain dialog activities that are not appropriate for the current state of the program’s execution. is can be done by disabling (graying out) various controls (also called widgets) in the dialog window.A.3 BASIC DESIGN GUIDELINESDialogs can be classied into various categories according to their functionality:• Dialogs launched by the user:• Property dialogs use nouns in various titles so that the user can understand the signicance of the information being displayed (e.g., “File size on disk:”).• Function dialogs have titles involving a verb that describes the command that the user can invoke (e.g., “Select” or “Apply”).
Appendix A ◾ 299• Dialogs launched by the application:• Process dialogs give information about current status (e.g., time remaining in a lengthy le transfer or space remaining on a disk drive).• Bulletin dialogs provide transitory status or may provide information and then block further processing until the user acknowledges that information (an example that you have probably seen already when installing new soware would be the “Accept” button to indicate acceptance of the terms in a license agreement appearing in a text box widget).In all cases, the dialog will contain control and status widgets that should be accompanied by text that informs the user about the signi-cance of the widget or group of widgets. e text may be a title that is in a Label widget located near a group of other widgets (e.g., a title explaining the signicance of a group of buttons each having additional text on each button to explain what that particular button does).e layout of widgets within a dialog should present a clear visual hierarchy typically dened by means of nested groupings. A grouping may be implicit or it may be visually explicit. For example, function-ally related buttons may be grouped within a parent frame that provides a visual indication of this grouping (e.g., the Frame and LabelFrame widgets are visually represented by showing a raised border that sur-rounds the buttons in a group).A.4 A MODELESS DIALOG WITH SEVERAL WIDGETSWe will start by discussing the widgets of the dialog window illustrated in Figure A.1. To see the script for this dialog along with a short mainline script that launches the dialog, nd the le: Script A_01 WidgetBuffet.py in the Appendix A scripts.e goal of this Appendix is to see how Python script snippets can be used to produce the various widgets in the modeless dialog of Figure A.1. Since the purpose of this dialog is simply to show a selec-tion of widgets, the layout design of the dialog does not adhere to any particular guidelines derived from HCI (human–computer interaction) principles. Nonetheless, it does illustrate how we can achieve logical and visual nesting of widgets through the use of frame widgets (function calls tk.LabelFrame and tk.Frame).
300 ◾ Appendix AAer studying the script and understanding how it works, you should be able to copy and modify relevant parts of the Script A_01 WidgetBuffet script so that your own program can work with a simple GUI. e script contains most of the widgets that you might need in a GUI. Menu widgets are not covered because they are somewhat more complicated to design and implement.e script for this dialog, contained in Script A_01 WidgetBuffet, covers about three pages and may be somewhat daunting for the program-mer who is not familiar with Tkinter. However, it is quite modular in its con-struction and easily understandable if you approach it one section at a time.e script can be separated into four parts: 1. A preamble (imports modules, provides the class denition header, and initiates the ll of the dialog window). 2. Denitions for the widgets (organized on a frame-by-frame basis).FIGURE A.1 e widget buet modeless dialog.
Appendix A ◾ 301 3. Denitions for the callback functions (invoked when a widget is activated). 4. e statements for the mainline program.We will rst describe the preamble and then discuss the various wid-gets and their corresponding callback functions.A.4.1 Preamble Statementse script starts with the following statements:import chimera, Tkinter as tkfrom chimera.baseDialog import ModelessDialogclass WidgetBuffet(ModelessDialog): buttons = ("Apply", "Close") help = False # Leave this out to get a Help button. title = "Window title goes here" def fillInUI(self, parent): # Text to be displayed in the dialog window: topText = tk.Label(parent, text = " Text at top of dialog", font = ("Helvetica", 11, "normal")) topText.grid(row = 0, column = 0, columnspan = 2, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W)e rst statement imports both chimera and Tkinter. Note that a Tkinter prex will be designated by using tk. e next statement brings in the ModelessDialog class and the next statement denes the WidgetBuffet class that indicates inheritance from the ModelessDialog class. e next three statements specify the buttons that will appear (or not appear) at the bottom of the dialog and the title that will be placed in the window pane. Note that the help button does not appear because of the help = False statement.e initialization function fillInUI sets up the widgets in the dialog. e rst widget is an optional Label that provides some text to describe the purpose of the dialog. e arguments for the Label instantiation are reasonably self-explanatory. e statement aer that uses the grid manager to specify the location of the Label widget within the dialog window. We next consider Tkinter strategies for placement of the other widgets.


===== [74/99] chapter-74.html =====

302 ◾ Appendix AA.4.2 Widget PlacementTkinter has three approaches for the layout of widgets: 1. e Pack Manager is the easiest one to use, but it is somewhat limited in its capabilities. 2. e Grid Manager is more exible than the pack manager and pro-vides a versatile mechanism for widget layout. It requires more direc-tives from the programmer. You are not allowed to have the Grid Manager and the Pack Manager working together within one dialog. 3. e Place Manager is the most capable but requires more work to get everything set up just right. For simple dialogs it is implementation overkill and is not recommended.Considering the previous points, we will work with the Grid Manager. e Grid Manager considers a layout to be organized as a two-dimensional array of cells organized in rows and columns. You do not have to specify the number of rows and columns; this will be determined by the Grid Manager. A single cell is considered to be in a particular row and column. e width of a cell will be determined by the width of the column in which it lies, and the height of a cell will be determined by the height of the row in which it lies.eheight of a row will be equal to the height of the widget (in that row) with the larg-est height. e width of a column will be equal to the width of the widget (in that column) with the largest width. As discussed below, these rules are modied if the widget spans more than one column or more than one row. Specication of column span is done using the columnspan keyword.Normally, a cell will contain a single widget (e.g., a button, checkbox, scroll bar, etc.). In this appendix, we encourage the option of having a cell* contain a single frame widget instantiated by using tk.Frame or tk.LabelFrame. Each frame essentially establishes a lower level grid system with cells containing other widgets. is provides a nesting hierarchy for all the widgets and their placement in the dialog is fairly easy to reorga-nize if necessary. Figure A.2 shows the grid system that is being used for the dialog illustrated in Figure A.1. e high-level grid system is delin-eated using heavy dashed lines, while the various low-level grid systems are delineated using narrower dashed lines that are superimposed over the ridges that visually delineate a frame.* We start with one frame occupying a single cell, but as we will see later, it is possible for a frame to span two or more adjacent cells.
Appendix A ◾ 303With some careful inspection you can verify that the high-level grid system has 12 cells organized in an array with 6 rows and 2 columns. ese cells are used to organize nine “high-level” widgets as follows: 1. e Label widget referenced as topText in the script sets up a text string at the top of the dialog. e statement to do this relies on the Grid Manager and reads as follows: topText.grid(row = 0, column = 0, columnspan = 2, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W) e row and column settings indicate that the label occupies all of row 0. It starts in column 0 and spans two columns (not really nec-essary for this short string of text but advisable if the text string is made longer). ere is some vertical padding so that the text is not cramped by the edges of the cell and the “sticky” parameter species that the widget should hug the “West” side of the cell.Column 0Row 0Row 1Row 2Row 3Row 4Row 5Column 1FIGURE A.2 Grid systems for the dialog of Figure A.1.
304 ◾ Appendix A 2. e next signicant widget is a LabelFrame that contains a title label and two check buttons. In the high-level grid system, it occu-pies the cell in row 1 and column 0. 3. e third widget is a LabelFrame that contains a title label and two press buttons. In the high-level grid, it occupies the cell in row 1 and column 1. 4. e next LabelFrame contains a title label and eight radio buttons. In the high-level grid, it occupies both cells in row 2 because it starts in column 0 and spans two columns. 5. e h widget is a LabelFrame that contains a title label, a list box, and a scroll bar. In the high-level grid, it occupies the cell in row 3, column 0. 6. e sixth widget is a Frame that contains a Canvas widget. In the high-level grid, it occupies the cell in row 3, column 1. 7. e seventh widget is a LabelFrame that contains a title label, a Scale widget, and a button. High-level grid coordinates are row 4, column 0. 8. e eighth widget is a LabelFrame that contains a title label and an Entry box. High-level grid coordinates are row 4, column 1. 9. e last widget is a LabelFrame that contains a Text widget. In the high-level grid, it occupies both cells in row 5 because it starts in column 0 and spans two columns.A.4.3 Widgets in the WidgetBuffet DialogWe can now discuss the script snippets that produce the various widgets appearing in the eight frames.A.4.3.1 The Checkbutton Classe Checkbutton class can be used to generate check boxes in a GUI. Consider the following script:# Setting up two check boxes:# Checking the box will change the value# of the IntVar and will initiate the execution# of the function specified by the command option.checkBoxFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = "Title for Checkboxes",
Appendix A ◾ 305 bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)checkBoxFrame.grid(row = 1, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W)self.checkedBoxA = tk.IntVar()cbA = tk.Checkbutton(checkBoxFrame, text = "Checkbox A ", variable = self.checkedBoxA, command = self.boxAfunc, indicatoron = 1)cbA.grid(row = 0, column = 0, pady = 6, sticky = tk.E)cbA.select() # This button initialized with a check mark.self.checkedBoxB = tk.IntVar()cbB = tk.Checkbutton(checkBoxFrame, text = "Checkbox B ", variable = self.checkedBoxB, command = self.boxBfunc, indicatoron = 1)cbB.grid(row = 1, column = 0, pady = 6, sticky = tk.E)e script starts with the instantiation of the LabelFrame widget. Note that its utilization of the high-level grid will be stipulated by using the parent parameter. e border dening the visual extent of the frame is established by the option: relief = tk.RIDGE. It should be mentioned that this option has no visual eect if you leave out the option bd = 2, which species the width of this border. e subsequent statements instan-tiate the two check boxes, all within this frame. Note that they make use of the Grid Manager, but the grid system is relative to the frame (stipulated by the checkBoxFrame parameter in each instantiation). e cell coordi-nates for these two widgets are relative to this frame (see the narrow dashed lines within cell (1,0) of the high-level grid) in Figure A.2. For example, the rst Checkbutton widget is in row 0, column 0 of this lower level grid. If you were to replace the checkBoxFrame parameter with parent, then the label widget would overwrite the text at the top of the dialog.Each Checkbutton instantiation statement contains an option that species the callback function for that button. For example, a mouse click in checkbox A will initiate execution of the callback function boxAfunc:# Callback functions needed for check boxes:def boxAfunc(self): line = "Box A checked. Value = %1d" % self.checkedBoxA.get() self.textWidget.insert(tk.END, line + "\n") self.textWidget.yview(tk.END)Normally, the statements in this function would perform an action related to this check box. In this dialog, the action is simply to have a text message displayed in the text box (referenced as self.textWidget) at the bottom of the dialog. Note that the indicator value (0 or 1) associated with 
306 ◾ Appendix Athe checkbox is available to the callback function by means of the self.checkedBoxA.get() call. e g et() function is a method associated with self.checkedBoxA, which is designated as an IntVar() and used by the variable option in the Checkbutton instantiation statement. As a nal observation, we see that the statement c b A.s ele ct() initializes checkbox A to be in its selected state.A.4.3.2 Normal Buttonse script:# Setting up two buttons (one disabled for now):buttonsFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = "Title for buttons", bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)buttonsFrame.grid(row = 1, column = 1, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.E)self.btnA = tk.Button(buttonsFrame, text = " Button A name ", command = lambda: self.btnFunc("A"))self.btnA.grid(row = 0, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4)self.btnB = tk.Button(buttonsFrame, text = " Button B name ", command = lambda: self.btnFunc("B"), state = tk.DISABLED)self.btnB.grid(row = 1, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4)sets up two buttons in the frame residing in the cell at location (1, 1). If you understood the script for the checkboxes, then this script should be no trou-ble. Each button press initiates the execution of the callback function self.btnFunc. To make this script a bit more interesting, we are using the same callback function for both buttons. To identify the button that was pushed, we pass a parameter to the callback function by using a lambda expression. Using a lambda expression is necessary if this command option is to work properly aer a mouse click on the button. Note that specifying the option as: command = self.btnFunc("A") will cause the self.btnFunc callback to be called during the instantiation of the button and this is not what we want to do! We want it to be called when the button is depressed using the mouse. To add more interest to this script, the option state = tk.DISABLED has been used in the instantiation for Button B. Consequently, when the dialog is launched, this button is initially in a “grayed-out” disabled state. To give Button A “something to do,” we have included the statement self.bt n B.configure(state = tk.NORMAL) in its callback function and this will put Button B into its normal (not disabled) state.

Appendix A ◾ 307A.4.3.3 A Group of Radio Buttonse script:# Set up radio buttons as specified by a list:rButtonsFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = “Title for radio buttons”, bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)rButtonsFrame.grid(row = 2, column = 0, columnspan = 2, padx = 4, pady =4, sticky = tk.W + tk.E)# Use tk.IntVar() here for integer result values.self.rBtnResult = tk.StringVar() radioBtn_L = [("Name A", "Result A", 0, 0), (“Name B”, “Result B”, 0, 1), (“Name C”, “Result C”, 0, 2), ("Name D", "Result D", 0, 3), ("Name E", "Result E", 1, 0), ("Name F", "Result F", 1, 1), ("Name G", "Result G", 1, 2), ("Name H", "Result H", 1, 3)] self.rBtnResult.set("Result A") # This button starts off selected.for text, rslt, rw, cl in radioBtn_L: rBtn = tk.Radiobutton(rButtonsFrame, text = text, variable = self.rBtnResult, value = rslt, command = self.rBtnfunc) rBtn.grid(row = rw, column = cl, padx = 4, sticky = tk.W)sets up a Label widget and eight radio buttons within the frame referenced as rButtonsFrame. 308 ◾ Appendix AA.4.3.4 A List Box Widget with a Scroll Bar Widgete script:listBoxFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = "Title for list box", bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)listBoxFrame.grid(row = 3, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W + tk.NS) self.lstBx = tk.Listbox(listBoxFrame, height = 5, width = 18, selectmode = tk.SINGLE, bg = "white") # Use MULTIPLE for checkbox like operation. scroll = tk.Scrollbar(listBoxFrame, command = self.lstBx.yview)self.lstBx.configure(yscrollcommand = scroll.set)scroll.grid(row = 0, column = 1, padx = 5, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W +tk.N + tk.S) lstBx_L = ["Entry 0", "Entry 1", "Entry 2", "Entry 3", "Entry 4", "Entry 5"]for item in lstBx_L: self.lstBx.insert(tk.END, item)self.lstBx.grid(row = 0, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.E)self.lstBx.bind("<Double-Button-1>", self.Apply)sets up a Listbox widget and a Scrollbar widget within the frame located in the cell at row 3 and column 0 of the high-level grid. e low-level grid for this frame has a grid system with one row and two columns. Row 0 contains the list box in column 0 along with the vertical scroll bar in column 1. Note that the height of row 0 is determined by the list box vertical height, which is specied by the option height = 5, where 5 is the number of visible lines in the list box. e width of the list box, here set to be 18, is also given in terms of a character count. is dialog has the option selectmode = tk.SINGLE meaning that one can only select a single item from the list box. Using selectmode = tk.MULTIPLE would allow multiple selections.By using the function call: self.lstBx.curselection(), we can determine which list box entry was selected. For the sake of convenience, this statement has been put into the callback function for the Apply button. Note that selection of a list box line such as Entry 2 will produce a curse-lection return value that is the tuple (‘2’,). Since the selection mode is SINGLE, there is only one entry in this tuple. As you would expect, using a MULTIPLE selection mode produces tuples with multiple entries.As mentioned earlier, column 1 of row 0 contains a scrollbar widget. Notice how the script facilitates communication between 
Appendix A ◾ 309the listbox widget and the scrollbar widget. e instantia-tion of the Scrollbar widget includes the option: command = s elf.ls t B x.y v i e w , which references the listbox and sets the orienta-tion of the scrollbar to be vertical (specied as yview). e next state-ment: self.lstBx.configure(yscrollcommand = scroll.set) congures the listbox so that it knows about the scroll widget. Note how the listbox and the scrollbar can be visually associated by having the listbox move to the “East side” of its low-level cell (sticky = tk.E), while the scrollbar snuggles up to the “West side” of its cell (sticky = tk.W +tk.N + tk.S). e tk.N and tk.S specications ensure that the vertical height of the scrollbar match the height of the listbox. e nal layout has the listbox adjacent to the scrollbar with a small space between the two, as dictated by the padx settings.e listbox is populated with entries by using an insert function within a for loop that works through a list of character strings. Note that each entry is inserted at the end of the growing list in the listbox.e last statement: self.lstBx.bind("<Double-Button-1>", self.Apply) is interesting. It produces a “binding” between an event and a call-back function. More precisely, double clicking of an entry in the listbox will cause the callback function for the Apply button to be executed. is is a convenient way for the user to get immediate action on a listbox selection.A.4.3.5 A Canvas Widgete script:# Set up a canvas to the right of the list box:canvasFrame = tk.Frame(parent, bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)canvasFrame.grid(row = 3, column = 1, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.E)# Title left out (a picture is worth a thousand words…)picture = tk.Canvas(canvasFrame, width = 110, height = 107)picture.grid(row = 0, column = 0)gfImage = tk.PhotoImage(file = "canvasExample.gif")picture.create_image(0, 0, image = gfImage, anchor = tk.NW)# Store a reference to the image as an attribute of the canvas:picture.img = gfImage # Remove this and the image disappears.puts a Canvas widget into the frame that is in cell (3,1) of the high-level grid. A canvas can be used to display graphics or images. ere are sev-eral methods associated with the widget allowing the programmer to 
310 ◾ Appendix Ainsert lines, circles, polygons, and so on. ese capabilities will be ignored because they are beyond our needs. For the dialog being considered, the canvas widget simply displays the graphic canvasExample.gif. Note that the Canvas widget has a width and height that are specied as options in the Canvas instantiation.e image is accessed by opening a le having a pathname that is speci-ed by the file option in the PhotoImage function. e image is trans-ferred to the Canvas widget by means of the function create_image. e pixel coordinates (0,0) and the anchor option are used to locate the image within the two-dimensional canvas.Note that it is important for the image to have a reference that is an attribute of the canvas. is is accomplished with the statement: picture.img = gfImage. Without this statement, the image will not be retained in the dialog and the canvas will be empty.A.4.3.6 A Scale WidgetA scale is useful if the user is required to input a number that is restricted to some prespecied range of possible values. An example would be the requirement to stipulate a threshold value prior to the execution of an algorithm.e script:# Setting up a scale:scaleFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = “Title for scale”, bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)scaleFrame.grid(row = 4, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W) self.scaleVar = tk.DoubleVar()scale = tk.Scale(scaleFrame, variable = self.scaleVar, orient = tk.HORIZONTAL, length = 100, from_ = 1.0, to = 12.0, resolution = 0.1, troughcolor = “goldenrod1”)scale.grid(row = 0, column = 0, sticky = tk.W) acceptButton = tk.Button(scaleFrame, text = “Accept”, command = self.acceptSelection)acceptButton.grid(row = 0, column = 1, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.S + tk.W)sets up a Scale widget and a button in the frame occupying cell (4, 0) of the high-level grid. Statements dealing with the LabelFrame and Button 
Appendix A ◾ 311instantiations are similar to those covered in earlier sections. Note that the callback function for the Accept button is acceptSelection().Let us review some of the options that appear in the Scale instan-tiation statement. e option orient = tk.HORIZONTAL establishes a horizontal orientation for the scale while the option length = 100 species its length in pixels. e options from_ and to will dictate the range of values that the scale will put out. Since from is a Python keyword, Tkinter uses the underscore in the from_ option to avoid a Python error. e resolution = 0.1 option dictates that output from the scale will increment in steps of 0.1 as the scale slider is moved.e value provided by the scale is retained in the self.scaleVar. is is specied by the option variable = self.scaleVar. is variable is initialized as a DoubleVar() and it is accessed in the acceptSelection callback function where it is displayed in the text box.A.4.3.7 An Entry Box WidgetEntry boxes are useful for getting short character strings from a user, for example, a Protein Data Bank (PDB) identier.e script:# Setting up a entry box:entryFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = "Title for entry box", bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)entryFrame.grid(row = 4, column = 1, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.E + tk.NS) self.entryVar = tk.StringVar()entry = tk.Entry(entryFrame, textvariable = self.entryVar, width = 14, bg = "white")entry.grid(row = 0, column = 0, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.E)# Uncomment the next line if the entry box is to start DISABLED.#entry.config(state = tk.DISABLED)places an Entry widget into the frame within cell (4,1) of the high-level grid. If you understood all the Tkinter statements in the preceding sections, then there is nothing really new here. Just two small notes: the option width = 14 is specifying the entrybox width in terms of character length and the Entry widget can be disabled by means of the config function.

312 ◾ Appendix AA.4.3.8 A Text Box WidgetA text box can be used for entry of longer strings of text but, in this dialog, it is used to provide status output.e script:# Setting up a text box:textFrame = tk.LabelFrame(parent, text = "Title for text box", bd = 2, relief = tk.RIDGE)textFrame.grid(row = 5, column = 0, columnspan = 2, padx = 4, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W + tk.E) self.textWidget = tk.Text(textFrame, width = 40, height = 6, wrap = tk.WORD, bg = "white")self.textWidget.grid(row = 0, column = 0, padx = 4, sticky = tk.W)textScroll = tk.Scrollbar(textFrame, command = self.textWidget.yview)self.textWidget.configure(yscrollcommand = textScroll.set)textScroll.grid(row = 0, column = 1, padx = 2, pady = 4, sticky = tk.W +tk.N + tk.S)places a Text widget into the labeled frame that is in cells (5, 0) and (5, 1) of the high-level grid. Note that the LabelFrame widget spans two col-umns. Again, if you understood all the Tkinter statements in the preced-ing sections, then there is nothing really new here. e scrollbar is set up in a fashion that is similar to what we have seen for the list box. e Text widget has a width and height that is specied by a character count. Note that the textbox has a wrap option that determines how a line continues to the next line. By stipulating wrap = tk.WORD, we will not get lines ending in mid-word unless they are extremely long (longer than the width of the text box).In our implementation of the dialog, character strings are put into the text box by the callback functions. In all such cases, a character string stored in the variable line is created and then inserted at the end of the text box by using the function call: self.textWidget.insert(tk.END, line + "\n"). Note the use of “\n” so that each callback addition to the text appears as a new line. e nal statement: s e lf.t e x t Wid g et.y v ie w(t k.E N D) is used to ensure that the text scrolls up as each new line is put into the text box. is avoids the need for the user to constantly scroll the text box to see the latest new lines.
Appendix A ◾ 313A.4.4 Testing the WidgetBuffet Dialoge widgetBuffet.py script contains a very simple script to test the dialog:wb = WidgetBuffet()out = “Using the widgets should show results in this text box.\n”wb.textWidget.insert(tk.END, out) e dialog class is instantiated and this is enough to launch it. e next statement shows how a mainline script can access a widget in the dialog. To illustrate this, a character string has been sent to the text box at the bottom of the dialog.It should be noted that this dialog does not do any interactions with the Python Shell. Consequently, you could simply launch it as an application by using the File… Open… menu of the Chimera window and all further interactions would not involve the Python Shell.A.5 BUILDING A MODELESS DIALOGUnderstanding the underlying construction and functionality of the widgets in the WidgetBuffet dialog, we can construct other modeless dialogs by simply “stealing and modifying” its statements. One would go through the following steps: 1. Preamble: Copy over the preamble statements seen in Section A.4.1. You will want to rename the dialog and dene the text to be placed in the window pane and at the top of the dialog. You can also bring in var-ious standard buttons that are available, for example: “OK” and “Close.” Any other buttons that you name will be set up at the bottom of the dialog but you will be responsible for providing the callback functions. 2. Layout: Copy over the needed frames along with the widgets that they contain. Modications will likely include the following: a. Decide where the frames will appear relative to the high-level grid. b. Decide what goes into a frame (title label, number of buttons, etc.).
314 ◾ Appendix A c. Determine the layout of these widgets relative to the low-level grid within the frame. is will involve appropriate use of the “sticky” options and may require some modications of the width, height, and padding options. You will probably test the dialog several times, making several changes until the layout is both user-friendly and aesthetically pleasing. d. Be sure that the communication between the widget and the callback is properly handled (creating the appropriate IntVar(), StringVar(), or DoubleVar() variables). 3. Callback functions: Implement the callback functions. is will include statements that deal with interactions between the dialog and the mainline program. You will also need to determine whether a callback function responds to one widget or to several (as in the Button example discussed in Section A.4.3.2). 4. Interactions with the mainline: Write the code for the mainline program that will instantiate the dialog and subsequently work with the widgets that it contains.A.6 BUILDING A MODAL DIALOGMost of the script snippets that we have seen in the previous sections can be applied to modal dialogs. Let us now consider how a modal dialog diers from a modeless dialog by reviewing the statements in the le: Script A _ 02 Sample ModalDialog.py. It is a small dialog so be careful not to lose it among any other windows that may be open on your desktop.Here is the script:import chimera, Tkinter as tkfrom chimera.baseDialog import ModalDialogclass userInput(ModalDialog): title = "User Input" buttons = ('OK', 'Cancel') # Leave out next statement to get a Help button. help = False def __init__(self, requestToUser): self.requestToUser = requestToUser ModalDialog.__init__(self) 
Appendix A ◾ 315 def fillInUI(self, parent): topText = tk.Label(parent, text = self.requestToUser) topText.grid(row = 0, column = 0) # Next line is needed in case the user does a Cancel. self.result = None self.entry = tk.Entry(parent, width = 8) self.entry.grid(row = 0, column = 1) #==================================================== # CALLBACK functions: def OK(self): self.result = self.entry.get() ModalDialog.Cancel(self, value=None)#==================================================================# Mainline:md = userInput(" Type in a PDB ID: ")md.run(chimera.tkgui.app)if md.result: print md.resultNotice that inheritance is from ModalDialog and not ModelessDialog. Object initialization is done by the __init __ call whichtakes in a string parameter that will be used to dene topText, which is a Label widget at the top of the dialog. e __init__ function also does initialization of the ModalDialog base class: ModalDialog. __init__(self). Functionality of statements in the fillInUI function is essentially the same as what we saw earlier for the modeless dialog. Care must be taken to ensure that a nal result is avail-able to the mainline program when the user clicks on the OK button. In the given script, the widgets are not enclosed in one or more frames because the layout is very simple. e Label and Entry widgets are positioned using a grid with one row and two columns.e callback function for the OK button gets a string result from the Entry widget and sets it up in the self.result variable, which can be accessed by the mainline code immediately aer the dialog disappears due to the execution of: ModalDialog.Cancel(self, value=None). Note that there is no callback function for the Cancel button because this func-tionality is handled by a base class method.e mainline is fairly simple. It instantiates the dialog but then another statement is required to run it. Since it is a modal dialog, the statement 
316 ◾ Appendix Afollowing md.run(chimera.tkgui.app) will not run until the user dismisses the dialog by clicking on either the OK button or the Cancel button.REFERENCE[G05] J. E. Grayson. Python and Tkinter Programming. Manning Publications, Greenwich, CT, 2005.

317Appendix B ScenographicsB.1 MOTIVATION AND INTRODUCTIONere are many occasions when it is desirable to put more graphical content into a molecular display. We might wish to use an arrow to show a possible direction of movement for an atom or a cylinder-like object to represent the inertial axis of a helix. In various mathematical models, an amino acid side chain may be approximated by an ellipsoid with a corre-sponding visual component in the display. e Scenographics* package in the StructBio folder provides various methods that can be used to place three-dimensional (3D) objects and surfaces into the current display of a molecule. All objects can be positioned in 3D display space by speci-fying coordinates and, in some cases, the directional orientation of the object. Object size and color can also be specied.e Scenographics package has several classes that allow a program-mer to place scenographic elements into a Chimera scene. ese fall into the following six categories: 1. Solids 2. Surfaces 3. Lines* As noted in Wikipedia (http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scenography), the term scenography goes back to 1862 and was used by Antonio Caimi who excelled in Arte scenograca, which is the study and practice of design for stage settings. ese endeavors “required ingenious engineering to create movable sets, or create illusions of environments.” In our case, the ingenuity will require the appli-cation of linear algebra and graphics algorithms to create extra elements in the molecular display.
318 ◾ Appendix B 4. Parametric surfaces 5. Ellipsoids 6. Labelsese classes are not completely independent. For example, the class that generates parametric surfaces will use the classes that generate surfaces and lines. Considering these dependencies, we will cover these classes in the order just specied.B.2 USING THE Solids CLASSe Solids class has methods to draw various 3D objects including struts, tubes, spheres, cubes, boxes, disks, spindles, arrows, and the Platonic solids (tetrahedrons, hexahedrons, octahedrons, dodecahedrons, and icosahedrons). Figure B.1 shows some typical examples, whereas Figure B.2 presents a whimsical view of the ve regular convex polyhedra referred to as the Platonic solids. Figure B.1 was produced by the script Script B_01 Display SolidsTest.py and Figure B.2 was produced by the script Script B_02 Platonic Garden.py.Using the class as shown in these scripts requires the following import statements:from StructBio.Scenographics.solids import Solidsfrom StructBio.Utilities.miscFunctions import colorByNameFIGURE B.1 (See color insert.) Some examples of the available three-dimensional (3D) objects from the Solids class.
Appendix B ◾ 319e rst statement brings in the Solids class denition. e importation of the colorByName function found in the StructBio.Utilities. miscFunctions package is required if you wish to specify a color by using a character string such as “light sea green” which is one of the Chimera built-in colors.* Typical use of Solids will also require the importation of numpy so that the script can compute 3D coordinates using data representations provided by array(). Aer the import statements, the script can instantiate Solids objects, for example:solidsDisp = Solids()e default model name is provided by the Python string “Solids graphics”, but this can be changed by giving the class initialization function a character string argument, for example:solidsDisp = Solids(“Platonic solids”)as seen in Script B_02 Platonic Garden.py.Aer the object instantiation, the script can add various objects to the display, for example:solidDisp.addSphere(center, radius, rgb)* http://www.cgl.ucsf.edu/chimera/docs/UsersGuide/colortables.html.FIGURE B.2 (See color insert.) A Platonic garden.
320 ◾ Appendix Bwhere center, radius, and rgb specify the obvious attributes for the sphere. Once all the required objects have been added to the scene, we can initiate their display as follows:solidDisp.display()Here is a list of the function headers in the Solids class:def addStrut(self, p1, p2, radius, rgb):def addTube(self, p1, p2, radius, rgb):def addSphere(self, center, radius, rgb):def addCube(self, center, side, rgb):def addBox(self, center, sides_T, rgb):def addDisk(self, center, normal, thickness, radius, rgb):def addSpindle(self, p1, p2, radius, rgb):def addArrow(self, p1, p2, radius, rgb):def addTetrahedron(self, center, size, rgb, transparency = 1.0):def addHexahedron(self, center, size, rgb, transparency = 1.0):def addOctahedron(self, center, size, rgb, transparency = 1.0):def addDodecahedron(self, center, size, rgb, transparency = 1.0):def addIcosahedron(self, center, size, rgb, transparency = 1.0):def addPolygon(self, polyPoints_L, rgb, transparency = 1.0):Most of these methods have an obvious functionality. e addStrut function puts a cylinder (capped at both ends) into the display, whereas the addTube function generates a test-tube-like object (a cylinder that is capped at one end and has a half sphere at the other end). Figure B.1 shows a strut, tube, spindle, arrow, sphere, cube, box, and disk.e parameters for the function calls are fairly straightforward. e requirements are as follows:p1, p2, center are arrays that hold coordinates for 3D points.radius, side, thickness are oating point positive numbers.rgb designates a color (a 3-tuple of oating point numbers each in the range [0.0, 1.0]).transparency is a number in the range [0.0, 1.0].normal is an array that holds a three-component vector that designates a direction.sides_T is a tuple that designates the lengths of the sides of a box (three positive oating point numbers).Figure B.2 was generated by using Script B_02 Platonic Garden. e function calls are quite straightforward. ere is no setting of the parameters that will rotate these objects.
Appendix B ◾ 321B.3 USING THE Surfaces CLASSe Surfaces class has methods that generate a triangulated surface. e import statement to access the class is as follows:from StructBio.Scenographics.surfaces import SurfacesA sample script using the class can be found in Script B_03 Display Polygons test.py. As expected, generating a surface starts with a class instantiation such aspd = Surfaces()After this, you can add surface elements by doing function calls to addPolygon or addFace. The parameter sequence is the same for both function invocations. The first argument is a list of vertices (each being a numpy array) and the next two arguments define the color and transparency of the surface. After all polygonal additions are completed, the surface can be displayed by executing the display() function call.If you run the script, you will notice that rotation of the scene will cause the polygons dened by addFace to disappear when you attempt to look at them from behind the surface. Why is this useful? If the surface pieces are enclosing a 3D object, then there will be pieces on the far side of the object that cannot be seen because they are concealed by the part of the surface that is closer to the observation point. If the surface comprises pieces generated by addPolygon, then they will be handled by the graph-ics engine even though they will eventually be obscured from view. If the pieces are generated by addFace, then they are simply omitted when seen from behind. is back-face culling in the graphics pipeline provides a performance advantage because surface pieces that are not seen will not be processed for display.A cautionary note: One must be careful to distinguish the front face from the back face of a polygon. is orientation is dened by the order-ing of the vertices. e front (visible) face will have its vertices listed in counter-clockwise fashion in the addFace invocation. So if you are building a surface by using addFace, you will have to ensure that the vertices are listed in the required order. is will avoid unintended surface holes.


===== [78/99] chapter-78.html =====

322 ◾ Appendix BNote that Script B_03 Display Polygons test.py also has a user interaction via the Python Shell. is interaction shows how the function calls hide() and show() can be used to control the appearance of the surfaces in the display.In Section B.5 we will discuss classes that provide more sophisticated sur-face constructions that are dened by means of parameterized equations.B.4 USING THE Lines CLASSe Lines class has methods that generate an unbroken line that is made from piecewise linear line segments. e import statement to access the class is as follows:from StructBio.Scenographics.lines import LinesA sample script using the class can be found in Script B_04 Display Lines test.py. As expected, generating a line starts with a class instan-tiation such aspd = Lines()Aer this you can add line elements by doing function calls to addLine. e rst argument is a list of vertices (each being a numpy array) and the next argument denes the color of the line. Aer all line additions are completed, the lines can be displayed by executing the display() func-tion call. e Lines class has hide() and show() methods with the same functionality as in the Surfaces class.B.5 INTRODUCING THE ParametricSurfaces CLASSe ParametricSurfaces class is a base class that provides the data structures and methods that are to be used by other derived classes. e ParametricSurfaces class will allow you to construct surfaces that are much more complex than what we have seen so far. e class probably has more functionality than you will need but it should hopefully meet any extra future requirements that are not currently foreseen. To understand how this class is used to build a surface, we consider the following steps:• Step 1: Start with a curve in the x–y plane. is curve is dened as a sequence of points held in the list baseCurvePts _ L. Each entry in the list is a two component numpy array. e number of points is numBasePts.
Appendix B ◾ 323• Step 2: Use an algorithm that will replicate the base curve to form a cross section with some particular 3D position and orientation. is is done repeatedly to generate a list of cross sections stored in crossSections _ L.• Step 3: e succession of cross sections can be used to dene either a mesh or a surface.If you inspect the __init __ function of the ParametricSurfaces class,* you will see the declarations for these three variables. Figure B.3 gives an elementary example of a base curve with its nine points represented by small spheres. e details for Steps 2 and 3 will be covered later. For now, we describe these steps in more general terms.Figure B.4 shows the base curve replicated to generate four cross sections (as in Step 2). Again, for emphasis, each of the points in a cross section is represented by a small sphere.We can now ask for a mesh that will connect the corresponding pointsin the successive cross sections. is is Step 3 and is illustrated in Figure B.5.* See the package component StructBio.Scenographics.parametricSurfaces, available from the book’s website.FIGURE B.3 Base curve points.
324 ◾ Appendix Be surface can be dened by lling in the mesh with polygonal surface elements as in Figure B.6. Note that the surface has been given a transpar-ency of 0.8 and so the z axis can be seen beneath the surface.Our mesh and surface constructions will allow some extra variations. Setting closedBase = True causes the mesh generation or surface gen-eration methods to treat the base curve points as dening a closed curve. e successive cross sections then dene a cylinder type of surface as illus-trated in Figure B.7a. When the surface has a closed base curve we can cap the ends. If we set startCap = True, then the rst cross section denes a polygon that will be lled in with a surface as illustrated in Figure B.7b. A similar setting can be used for endCap.FIGURE B.4 Cross sections.FIGURE B.5 Generation of a mesh.

Appendix B ◾ 327showSurface(self), hideSurface(self), showMesh(self), and hideMesh(self)ese functions have the same functionality as the show and hide meth-ods found in the Surfaces class.As mentioned earlier, all these methods are related to Step 3. e functionality needed to set up the crossSections_L list (done in Step 2) is accomplished by classes that are derived from the ParametricSurfaces class. Each of these classes has its own strategy for the positioning and orienting of the cross sections. ese classes can be listed as follows:• FrenetFrameSurface• ExtrusionSurface• RevolutionSurface• RuledSurfaceB.5.1 Using the FrenetFrameSurface Classe FrenetFrameSurface class is the most complicated class to use, but we cover it rst because the ExtrusionSurface and RevolutionSurface classes are actually derived from this class and give the programmer an easier approach to working with this class.e class can be imported by usingfrom StructBio.Scenographics.parametricSurfaces \ import FrenetFrameSurfacee object instantiation is done via the __ init __ method that starts with the following header:__init__(self, baseCurvePts_L, spinePts_L, rotation3Dmats_L, xScale_L, yScale_L, modelName = "")As described earlier, we assume a base curve is dened in the x, y plane by specifying a list of two-dimensional (2D) points: baseCurvePts_L. When a FrenetFrameSurface object is instantiated, it will be given a list of points in 3D space dening a spine curve. In the __init__ function, these points are provided by spinePts _L. ree more lists 
328 ◾ Appendix Brotation3Dmats _L, xScale _L, and yScale _L dene operations for 3D rotation and scaling.We describe the functionality of this class by providing more details for Step 2 introduced at the beginning of Section B.5. Recall the purpose of Step 2: We use an algorithm that will replicate the base curve to form a cross section with some particular 3D position and orientation. This is done repeatedly to generate a list of cross sections stored in crossSections_L. We can now describe the operations required to generate the ith cross section, that is stored in crossSections_L[i]:• Step 2(a): Copy the points in the baseCurvePts_L list to crossSections _ L[i].• Step 2(b): e x coordinate for every point in crossSections _ L[i] is scaled by a factor stored in xScale_L[i].• Step 2(c): e y coordinate for every point in crossSections _ L[i] is scaled by a factor stored in yScale_L[i].• Step 2(d): Every point in crossSections_L[i] is subjected to a 3D rotation operation specied by the rotation matrix stored in rotation3Dmats_L[i].• Step 2(e): Translate the points in crossSections_L[i] by using the vector stored in spinePts_L[i].Figure B.8 gives an example demonstrating these operations. e base curve is shown on the lower le. ree arrows have been set up to show the x, y, and z axes. In this case only four cross sections have been created. No scaling was done for the x coordinate, but the y coor-dinate of each base curve point was subject to scaling using the four scaling factors [1.0, 0.8, 0.6, 0.4]. Four rotation matrices were gener-ated in ad hoc manner and used to rotate the scaled base curve prior to translation: cos120sin12sin120 cos1201 00,1,2,3() ()() ()πππ−π=iiiii
Appendix B ◾ 329e four spine points stored in spinePts_L were [[5, 1, 1], [4, 2, 2], [4, 4, 3], [3, 6, 4]]. ese points have been emphasized in Figure B.8 as small spheres.Aer instantiation of the class object, displayMesh was used to generate the mesh and displaySurface (color gray with a transparency of 0.5) was used to show the surface. e script for Figure B.8 can be found in the le Script B_05 Frenet frame surface Example 1.py. is example has only four cross sections, but it has some visual complex-ity because both the y scaling factor and the rotation matrix change as we go from one cross section to the next. Normally, a Frenet frame surface would have many more cross sections and they would be closer together. Furthermore, the rotations and scaling factors would vary in small steps to facilitate a smooth surface continuity that is lacking in Figure B.8.It should be noted that typical use of the Frenet Frame approach involves moving the base prole along the spine curve in the generation of the suc-cessive cross sections. Each cross section corresponds to the base curve being given an orientation and position that is established by rotating and translating its local frame of reference now called the Frenet frame. As depicted in Figure B.9, this is done in such a way that the z axis of the local frame moves along the spine curve so that it is tangential to the spine curve.is places a constraint on the generation of the rotation matrices. So, when using the FrenetFrameSurface class you should take care to ensure that the rotation matrices force the z axis to be parallel with a tangent to the spine curve. ere are typically two ways to do this: e rotation matrices can be formulated by taking into account the global topology of the sur-face (as in the next example) or rotation matrix rotation3Dmats_L[i] can be built so that the z axis of the local frame is parallel to a vector that FIGURE B.8 Generation of a simple Frenet frame surface.
330 ◾ Appendix Bgoes from spinePts_L[i-1] to spinePts_ L[i+1] (with special attention being given to the end points of the spine to avoid an out-of-bounds error when accessing the spinePts_L list). Note that the generation of Figure B.8 involved a somewhat arbitrary choice of rotation matrices and so this constraint was not put in place.e script in the le Script B_06 Frenet frame surface Example 2.py provides another example of Frenet surface generation with the rotation matrices carefully designed so that the z axis of the local frame is tangent to a spine that is a simple circle.Here are some relevant notes for Figure B.10:• e base curve is dened by a set of 16 points that form a square. ey have been shown in the gure as a set of small spheres at the center of the gure.• e surface involves 256 cross sections and so we have a very smooth continuity in areas that show surface curvature.Spine curvezxyFIGURE B.9 Base curve in Frenet local frame moving along the spine curve.FIGURE B.10 Base curve in Frenet local frame moving along a simple circle.
Appendix B ◾ 331• e rotation matrices have been generated so that the z axis of the moving local Frenet frame is always parallel to the x, y plane but its x, y components (relative to the global frame) slowly change to give a constant rotation that keeps this z axis tangent to a circle.• Note the parameter setting closedSpine = True. is closes the surface so that it comes back on itself, a useful feature when drawing surfaces of a periodic nature, for example, a toroid.We nish this section with a nal example of a Frenet frame surface that has some utility for the display of pie chart data. Appendix B ◾ 333A GUI (graphical user interface) version of this script has been put into the Appendix D directory. e extra code to support the GUI will make the script somewhat longer.e FrenetFrameSurface class gives you considerable power in the display of various sophisticated surfaces. For example, if you have a sud-den irresistible desire to draw a Klein bottle,* then this is the class to use! Unfortunately, these capabilities come with a cost: You will need to spend some extra eort to generate the lists used in the __init__ parameters of the class instantiation. is can be somewhat onerous especially when building the rotation matrices that are required. But it is oen the case that the surface requires a spine and set of associated rotation matrices that are easier to generate because the surface is simpler. us we intro-duce two classes that are derived from the FrenetFrameSurface class:1. e ExtrusionSurface class: e spine is a simple straight line. e rotation matrices perform 2D rotations of the base curve prior to the translation operations.2. e RevolutionSurface class: e spine is a circle that goes around the y axis. e rotation matrices are used to revolve the base curve around the y axis.By using these classes we can avoid the complications typically associ-ated with the instantiation of a FrenetFrameSurface object.B.5.2 Using the ExtrusionSurface Classe ExtrusionSurface class generates a sequence of cross sections by extruding† a base curve along a straight line. e class can be imported by usingfrom StructBio.Scenographics.parametricSurfaces \ import ExtrusionSurfacee object instantiation is done via the __init__ method that starts with the following header:__init__(self, baseCurvePts_L, zMin, zMax, numCrossSections, rotation2Dmats_L, xScale_L, yScale_L, modelName = "")* See http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klein_bottle. Note that the surface is nonorientable, so you would not be able to ll in the mesh using calls to addFace.† e term extrusion is oen used when describing the manufacturing of aluminum materials. See http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrusion.
334 ◾ Appendix BAs described earlier, we assume a base curve is dened in the x,y plane by specifying a list of 2D points: baseCurvePts_L. Initialization of the object is essentially a special case of the initialization done for the FrenetFrameSurface class. Prior to calling the __init__ function for the FrenetFrameSurface class the following lists are created:• e spinePts_L list is dened by generating numCross Sections points along the z axis ranging from zMin to zMax.• e rotation3Dmats_L list is built from the rotation2Dmats_L list.• e xScale_L and yScale_L lists are simply passed along unmodied.e rotation3Dmats_L list allows you to do a 2D rotation relative to the x, y plane while creating the successive cross sections. Bear in mind that an extrusion along the z axis does not signify a restriction because we can use the rotateAndMove function of the ParametricSurface class to reorient and reposition the cross sections prior to the generation of a mesh or surface.e script in the le Script B_08 Rotini extrusion.py provides an interesting example of an extrusion that involves a list of 2D rotation matrices (Figure B.14). Appendix B ◾ 337B.5.4 Using the RuledSurface Classe RuledSurface class generates a sequence of cross sections by moving a straight line through space to develop the surface. e ends of the line are constrained to move along two curves situated in the 3D space. So, in some sense we are using two spine curves instead of one. Consequently, we cannot consider the class to be a derivation of the FrenetFrameSurface class, but it can be treated as a derivation of the ParametricSurfaces class. e class can be imported by usingfrom StructBio.Scenographics.parametricSurfaces import RuledSurfacee object instantiation is done via the __init__ method that starts with the following header:__init__(self, firstCurvePts_L, finalCurvePts_L, numCrossSections, modelName = "")Instead of visualizing a ruled surface as a straight line moving through space with end points constrained to lie on two curves, we can con-sider the surface to be a set of cross sections. We start with a rst curve dened by points stored in the list firstCurvePts _L. ese points are shown in FigureB.17 as a set of small cubes. Next we specify a nal curve dened by points stored in the list finalCurvePts_L. ese points are shown in Figure B.17 as a set of small spheres. e number of points in each curve must be the same. Construction of the surface proceeds by running straight lines between the corresponding points in the two lists. In FigureB.17 we see that each cube is joined to a sphere by means of a straight line. Each straight line is then subjected to a linear interpolation procedure that places extra uniformly spaced points on that line. e cor-responding interpolation points are then linked to form cross sections. FIGURE B.17 Simple example of a ruled surface.
338 ◾ Appendix BIn Figure B.17, we are assuming that numCrossSections is 5. e rst curve and nal curve both count as a cross section, so we need three extra interpolation points on each straight line to derive the points needed to dene the three additional cross sections.Usually, the number of cross sections can be a small number because there is no signicant curvature for a rectangle bounded by two consecu-tive straight lines. ere can be a small amount of twist because the oppo-site sides of a rectangle are not necessarily parallel. Although the number of cross sections may be small, the number of points used on the rst and nal curves may be considerably larger so that these curves appear to be smoothly rendered. is is demonstrated in Figure B.18 that shows a ruled surface that has a circle as a rst curve and a square as a nal curve. e number of points in each curve is 512. is is somewhat high, but the sur-faces are very smooth. Figure B.18 shows the surfaces with ends capped and not capped.To demonstrate that ruled surfaces can be more complicated, we end this section with Figure B.19 that illustrates a neck ru (popular during Elizabethan times*). In this case, the rst curve is a simple circle, whereas * http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ru_(clothing).FIGURE B.18 Circle to square ruled surfaces (capped and not capped).FIGURE B.19 Rue ruled surface.
Appendix B ◾ 339the nal curve is a circular sinusoidal wave form. Small spheres have been placed on the curves to designate the end points of the straight lines and to give the illustration an interesting visual eect.B.6 USING THE Ellipsoid CLASSAs mentioned in the introduction, there are situations when you may wish to place an ellipsoid structure into a scene. For example, in the study of protein domain movements (see [BS08] and [BF92]) the authors work with the apo and holo conformations of a protein. ey use an ellip-soid to enclose a domain in both forms and use this visual aid to show the amount of shi and rotation that a domain experiences on ligand binding.e Ellipsoid class can be used for such an application. e class can be imported by usingfrom StructBio.Scenographics.ellipsoid import Ellipsoide object instantiation is done via the __init__ method that starts with the following header:__init__(self, length, width, height, center, axes_L, numCrossSections, numBasePts, ellipsoidName = "Ellipsoid")Display of an ellipsoid is oen preceded by calculations that derive a set of inertial axes for some given set of atoms, such as a protein domain, a secondary structure, or a side chain. We will assume that such a calcula-tion provides a list of orthonormal axes that are passed to the __init __ function via axes_L. e list should be ordered so that axes_L[0] is the primary axis corresponding to the largest eigenvalue, whereas axes_L[2] is the axis corresponding to the smallest eigenvalue. e inertial axes will be used to establish the orientation of the ellipsoid, whereas the center parameter species the location of the ellipsoid center in the display space. e length parameter determines where the ellipsoid intersects the pri-mary axis. In a similar fashion the width and height parameters cor-respond to a xes _ L[1] and axes _ L[2], respectively. ese parameters dene the lengths of the semiaxes. In other words, if the inertial axes were simply the standard x, y, and z axes (primary axis being the x axis, etc.) and the center was the origin, then the equation of the ellipsoid surface would be
340 ◾ Appendix B ++=xyzlength widthheight1222e Ellipsoid class is derived from the RevolutionSurface class. ere are two parameters that control the neness of the mesh and hence the smoothness of the surface. e variable numCrossSections controls the number of cross sections that are generated when the base curve is revolved around its axis. is essentially determines the num-ber of meridian lines that run in the same direction as the major axis. e variable numBasePts controls the number of points on the base curve. is essentially determines the number of latitude lines that are orthogonal to the meridian lines (similar to the parallels of latitude on a world globe).e script in the le Script B_12 Ellipsoid surface example.py contains a small test program for this class. It displays an ellipse with length = 8, width = 4, height = 2, center having coordinates [3,4, 5], and orientation given by an axes list dened viau = np.pi/8; v = np.pi/4; w = -np.pi/3cu = np.cos (u); cv = np.cos (v); cw = np.cos (w)su = np.sin (u); sv = np.sin (v); sw = np.sin (w)axes_L = [np.array([cw*cv, -sw*cu + cw*sv*su, sw*su + cw*sv*cu]), np.array([sw*cv, cw*cu + sw*sv*su, -cw*su + sw*sv*cu]), np.array([-sv, cv*su, cv*cu])]Although this denition seems a bit daunting, it is really a rotation matrix that can change the orientation of an object in 3D space (see p. 231 of [Bu09]). It gives us an easy strategy to create orthonormal vectors. Apart from this, the script is quite easy to understand. Extra code has been put in place to show the inertial axes (scaled with respect to axes lengths) within the ellipsoid. ey appear as spindles within the ellipsoid. e results are shown in Figure B.20. In the lower le corner you can see three arrows to indicate the orthonormal x, y, and z axes of the display space. ese axes have been put in place so that you can more easily assess the position and orientation of the ellipsoid. e ellipsoid has been covered with both a mesh and a surface (transparency set to 0.3 so that the ellipsoid axes are easily visible).
