345Appendix C The Graph ClassC.1 MOTIVATIONIn its simplest terms, a graph is dened by specifying a set of vertices and a set of arcs, each arc joining two vertices. As such, it provides an excellent math-ematical model to describe molecular structure. In the most recognizable application of the idea, the set of vertices acts as an abstraction for the atoms within the molecule, whereas the arcs going between vertices correspond to the bonds between the atoms. In more sophisticated models, a vertex may represent a larger part of the molecule, such as a protein side chain, whereas an arc between vertices may represent some type of relationship between two side chains, for example, an indication that they are in contact with each other (see [XX00]). Typically, residues are considered to be in contact if their distance of separation is less than some threshold (say, 10 Å). Depending on the application, separation may be calculated as the distance between alpha carbon atoms or the distance between the centroids of the side chain atoms.e motivation for the use of graphs arises from the possibility that some algorithm applied to the graph has a corresponding biological signif-icance. For example, using graphs to model residue interactions has played a signicant role in the study of allosteric interactions (see the study done by Pandini and others in [PF12]). In [PF12], the authors generated a graph network that models local correlated motions of residues and graph algo-rithms were used to discover various paths within that network.ere are several studies that use a Delaunay tessellation to dene the notion of neighboring residues. As described in [ST96], the Delaunay tessellation of a protein structure leads to a set of space-lling irregular 
346 ◾ Appendix C tetrahedrons, or Delaunay simplexes, each of the four vertices represent-ing a protein side chain. Researchers have done various statistical analyses, and it has been discovered that the residue composition of these simplexes is not random. Instead, there are nonrandom preferences in the makeup of these simplexes. is has led to several consequential studies dealing with Delaunay tessellations and their relationships to topics such as the following:• Four-body statistical potentials [CL01], [GT01], [KT03], [MS97]• Protein structure similarity [BS04], [BV03]• Protein shape, pockets, and cavities [LE98a], [LE98b], [LE98c], [XB07]• Mechanical properties [KY97]• Structure motifs [WY98]• Domain partitions [WH99]e paper by Huan and others [HB05] studies packing motifs and compares the use of contact distance with that of Delaunay tessellation. Finally, Delaunay tessellation applied to atoms, rather than residues, can be found in [KG97] and [MS02].C.2 INTRODUCING THE GraphBase CLASSFormally, we dene a graph as a ve-element tuple (,,,,)GVEW WVEV=Σ where V is a set of vertices and E is a set of undirected arcs* and EVV⊆×. Each of the next three members in the tuple is a function: 1. WV denes a weight for each vertex: V →. 2. WE denes a weight for each arc: E →. 3. V∑ is a bijection that denes a unique label for each vertex: VL→, where L is a set of labels such that VL=.We will also need the following denition for an induced subgraph: given the graph (,,,,)GVEW WVEV=Σ and the vertex set VV⊆, the subgraph * Although it is common to refer to the set E as having edges, we will reserve that term for a some-what dierent use (this will be discussed in Section C.3).
Appendix C ◾ 347induced by V is ,, ,,GVEW WVEV()=Σ where ,|,EuvEuVvV{}()=∈∈∈. Asexpected, the weight and label functions for G will be the same as those used in G (with domains restricted to the subgraph).e script graph.py in the StructBio directory contains various class denitions that should be useful when you need to generate a graph object (including some frequently used graph algorithms). We will sometimes use the phrase abstract graph to emphasize that the graph exists solely as a Python object derived from the GraphBase class and it does not produce any display elements. In Section C.3, we introduce the Graph class, which generates display elements.To generate an abstract graph, you would go through the following steps: 1. Import the required classes using the following import statement: from StructBio.Graphs.graph import Vertex, Arc, GraphBase 2. Instantiate a graph object using the GraphBase class. 3. For each vertex of the graph, instantiate a vertex object using the Vertex class. e instantiation function allows you to specify the vertex weight (a oating point number with a default value of 0.0). Follow the instantiation with a call to the addVertex function of the graph object. is accomplishes two things: the vertex object is associated with its vertex label (a character string) and the vertex is added to the graph. 4. For each arc in the graph, instantiate an arc object using the Arc class. e instantiation function allows you to specify the arc weight (a oating point number with a default value of 0.0). Follow the instantiation with a call to the addArc function of the graph object. e addArc function has three parameters: the rst two parameters specify the labels of the vertices dening the arc, and the last param-eter is the arc object.** It should be mentioned that the addVertex and addArc functions are not constrained to work with only Vertex and Arc objects. ey can add to the graph any objects that have the required attributes: vertexWeight and arcWeight, respectively. In particular, addVertex can add to the graph any object that is instantiated from a class that is derived from the Vertex class. is is why we will refer to the Vertex class as a base class. Note that both Arc and GraphBase are also considered to be base classes.
348 ◾ Appendix C If you need a list of arcs, then the function call get A r cT u ple List() will generate a list of tuples, each tuple holding the two vertex labels that dened the arc.Once the graph object is built, you can use any of the following func-tions to generate an induced subgraph:• getSubgraph_VertexWeightsWithinRange(self, minVal, maxVal)is function returns the subgraph that is induced by vertices with weights that are within the closed interval specied by the two oating point values minVal and maxVal.• getSubgraph_VertexDegreeWithinRange(self, minVal, maxVal)is function returns the subgraph induced by vertices with degrees* that are within the closed interval specied by the two oating point values minVal and maxVal.• getSubgraph_ArcsRemoved(self, arcTuples_L)is function returns a graph that is the same as the self object but with the arcs listed in arcTuples_L removed.• getShortestPath(self, startingNodeLabel, endingNodeLabel)is function considers the arc weights to be arc lengths. It returns the subgraph induced by the vertices that are on the shortest path running from the vertex designated by startingNodeLabel to the vertex designated by endingNodeLabel.• computeConnectedComponents(self, minSize)is function uses a depth rst search algorithm to return a list of subgraphs, each corresponding to a connected component of the graph. e parameter minSize species the minimum size of a subgraph. In other words, any connected component with fewer than minSize vertices will be ignored and does not produce a list entry.As an example of a simple weighted graph, consider Figure C.1.* e degree of a vertex is the number of arcs connected to the vertex (with loops counted twice—not relevant in this context).
Appendix C ◾ 349e following script* can be used to build the graph:Script C.1: Simple GraphBase Exampleimport setsfrom sets import Setfrom StructBio.Graphs.graph import Vertex, Arc, GraphBaseg = GraphBase()g.addVertex("alpha", Vertex())g.addVertex("delta", Vertex())g.addVertex("theta", Vertex())g.addVertex("kappa", Vertex())g.addVertex("sigma", Vertex())g.addVertex("omega", Vertex())g.addArc("alpha", "delta", Arc(4.0))g.addArc("delta", "omega", Arc(0.5))g.addArc("alpha", "theta", Arc(0.5))g.addArc("theta", "kappa", Arc(1.5))g.addArc("kappa", "omega", Arc(1.5))g.addArc("alpha", "sigma", Arc(3.0))g.addArc("sigma", "omega", Arc(2.5))sp = g.getShortestPath("alpha", "omega")print sp.orderedPathLabels_LNote the following points:• We access the needed classes by doing the following import: from StructBio.Graphs.graph import Vertex, Arc, GraphBase• e abstract graph object g is instantiated by executing g = GraphBase().* See the Appendix C directory available from the book’s website: Script C_01 Simple GraphBase example.py1.51.5αδκωθσ0.54.02.50.53.0FIGURE C.1 A simple weighted abstract graph.

350 ◾ Appendix C • A statement such as g.addVertex("alpha", Vertex()) is used to instantiate a vertex object (default weight 0.0), assign it a label, and add it to the graph g.• A statement such as g.addArc("alpha", "delta", Arc(4.0)) is used to instantiate an arc object with specied weight and subse-quently add it to the graph g. e arc will join the vertices with labels specied by the rst two parameters.• e script illustrates the use of the function getShortestPath to calculate the shortest path between the vertex labeled alpha and the vertex labeled omega. is produces a subgraph object referenced by the variable sp.• e subgraph sp has all the attributes of a graph within the graphBase class. For example, we could get the labels of the vertices in this subgraph by accessing the keys of the vertex dictionary, sp.vertices_D.keys(). Information about the arcs could be accessed by using sp.arcs_D. e graph.py script gives more information about these attributes.• Although it is possible to trace the nodes along the shortest path by working with the usual attributes of a subgraph (vertices_D and arcs_D), this is somewhat inconvenient. Consequently, when a sub-graph has been created by using the getShortestPath method, the algorithm will also dene the attribute orderedPathLabels_L, which contains a list of the labels of the nodes in the shortest path with an ordering that goes from the starting vertex to the ending vertex. is list has been accessed by the print statement printsp.orderedPathLabels_L to print the labels of the vertices in the shortest path. e printed output will be ['alpha', 't heta', 'kappa', 'o mega'].C.3 INTRODUCING THE Graph CLASSe GraphBase class is useful for various applications that need an abstract graph to be constructed with a subsequent analysis that can be accomplished by the extraction of subgraphs. Although this functionality may sometimes be useful, a more signicant role of GraphBase, Vertex, and Arc will be their utilization as base classes for derived classes that 
Appendix C ◾ 351provide extra capabilities for the display of graphs in a scene that may also contain molecular structures. Specically, we will work with the following derived classes:• Graph derived from GraphBase• Node derived from Vertex• Edge derived from ArcAll these derived classes work with the Solids class to provide display elements that are typically related to other structures in the display (e.g., a molecule). Consider a short program that produces a very simple graph: two nodes linked by a single edge (see Figure C.2):Script C.2: A Simple Graphimport numpyfrom numpy import array as arfrom StructBio.Graphs.graph import Node, Edge, Graphg = Graph("Simple graph")# Red node:n1 = Node(0., ar((1., 0., 0.)), 0.7,(1., 0., 0.))# Blue node:n2 = Node(0., ar((9., 0., 0.)), 0.7,(0., 0., 1.))e12 = Edge(0., 0.3,(0., 1., 0.))g.addNode("Node_1", n1)g.addNode("Node_2", n2)g.addEdge("Node_1", "Node_2", e12)g.solids.display()print g.vertices_D.keys()e following are some notes explaining the code:*• e script starts with importing the needed classes (Graph, Node, and Edge).* See the Appendix C directory available from the book’s website: Script C_02 A simple graph.pyFIGURE C.2 A very simple graph.
352 ◾ Appendix C • Instantiation of a Graph object includes an initialization statement that instantiates a Solids object. Since the Graph class inherits from the GraphBase class, the initialization will also create the data structures that will track vertices and arcs. Note that the graph name “Simple graph” will appear in the Model Panel viewable if you do the menu invocation Favorites… Model Panel.• Syntax for the instantiation of a Node object is Node(nodeWeight, coords, radius, color).In our script, all node weights are 0.0 and the coordinates are specied by numpy arrays. e specied radius will be the radius of the sphere that is generated in the display when the addNode function is executed. e last parameter is a 3-tuple that species the RGB components of the sphere color.• Syntax for the instantiation of an Arc object is Arc(edgeWeight, radius, color).In the script, edge weight is 0 and the radius and color are 0.3 and (0.0, 1.0, 0.0) corresponding to green.• Syntax for the addition of a Node object is addNode(nodeLabel, node).is will assign the specied node label to the node and will add the node as a vertex to the underlying abstract graph. e function will also display a sphere with the radius and color dictated by the attributes of the node object.• Syntax for the addition of an Edge object is addEdge(nodeAlabel, nodeBlabel, edge).is will add a corresponding arc to the underlying abstract graph and will display a spindle with radius and color determined by the attributes of the Edge object. In the display, the location of the edge ends will be determined by the nodes specied by the node labels.
Appendix C ◾ 353• Aer all the nodes and edges have been established, the display is made visible by using the function call g.solids.display().• e script ends with the extra statement print g.vertices_D.keys(). is generates the output ['Node_2', 'Node_1'] in the Python shell window. It was added to the end of the script as a demonstration that attributes of the underlying abstract graph can be accessed if needed.C.3.1 Reasons for Using Derived ClassesIt is worthwhile to emphasize the design aspects of the classes introduced in Sections C.2 and C.3. e base classes primarily deal with abstract graphs as dened by the mathematical denition (,,,,)GVEW WVEV=Σ. All the algorithms strictly deal with vertices, arcs, weights, and labels. ere is no dependency on display attributes or coordinates, and so the algorithms are forced to focus on abstract attributes while having the freedom to ignore attributes associated with the display.Meanwhile, users who wish to design algorithms related to molecular structure can build the display objects: the spheres and spindles related to their application. While this construction is being done, the underlying abstract graph is also created without the user having to deal with the data structures that manage vertices and arcs.e net result is a clear separation of concerns: the GraphBase, Vertex, and Arc classes relate to mathematically oriented graph algo-rithms while the Graph, Node, and Edge classes relate to the display and constructions that correspond to the biological structures within the application.C.4 METHODS WITHIN THE Graph CLASSNow that we know how a graph can be created, we can discuss the meth-ods that can be applied to these graphs. All these methods deal with the extraction of subgraphs with the exception of Delaunay tessellation, which is a function that can be used to construct the edges of a graph aer the nodes are established.C.4.1 Subgraph Extraction Determined by Node WeightsConsider the following script:Script C.3: Example for Extraction of a Subgraph

356 ◾ Appendix C C.4.2 A Function to Produce Delaunay TessellationsSection C.1 stressed the importance of Delaunay tessellations for various research studies. e graph.py module includes a function that will pro-duce a Delaunay tessellation when given the three-dimensional coordi-nates of the nodes of a graph. e algorithm that generates the tessellation must deal with node coordinates, and so this function is more naturally given as a method within the Graph class. In practice, it will determine all the necessary edges to provide the tessellation and these new edges and existing nodes will form the arcs and vertices of an underlying abstract graph that can be the input for other graph algorithms. Using the function is reasonably straightforward as exemplied by the following script:Script C.4: Using the Function for Delaunay Tessellationimport chimera, numpyfrom chimera import runCommandfrom numpy import array, random as rnfrom StructBio.Graphs.graph import Graph, Node, EdgerunCommand("set bg_color white")maxX = 32.; maxY = 16.; maxZ = 16.numNodes = 12g = Graph("Delaunay Tessellation")for ix in range(numNodes): c = array((rn.random()*maxX, rn.random()*maxY, rn.random()*maxZ)) nd = Node(0.0, c, 0.7, tuple((rn.random(), rn.random(), rn.random()))) g.addNode("V" + "%03d"%ix, nd)FIGURE C.4 Subgraphs produced by Script C.3.
Appendix C ◾ 357g.buildDelaunayTessellation(15.0)g.solids.display()e script instantiates a graph that is referenced by the variable g. e graph name is “Delaunay tessellation,” and this name will appear in the Model Panel. e script starts by creating 12 nodes randomly placed in a box with sides having lengths 32, 16, and 16 Angstroms. e nodes have randomly generated colors. e function call g.buildDelaunay Tessellation(15.0) will generate the tessellation. e parameter in the function call (15.0 in the script) sets an upper limit on the length of an edge in the tessellation. If any edge in a tetrahedron has a length greater than this threshold, then the tetrahedron is rejected and does not contribute to the graph. Figure C.5 illustrates a typical display generated by the script. Note that it is possible for a node to be excluded from the tessellation if it does not have three neighboring nodes within threshold distance.C.4.3 Subgraphs Corresponding to Connected ComponentsComputing the connected components of an undirected graph has many applications. e Graph class has a method to do this. 365Appendix D2D and 3D PlotsD.1 MOTIVATIONe informatics component of bioinformatics deals with the retrieval, man-agement, processing, and display of information. To quote from Wikipedia:*Informatics is the science of information, the practice of informa-tion processing, and the engineering of information systems. Informatics studies the structure, algorithms, behavior, and interactions of natural and articial systems that store, process, access, and communicate infor-mation. In addition, it studies human–computer interaction and how an interface can be built to maximize user eciency.In many situations, the communication of information will involve the presentation of data output in the form of a plot. We have already seen examples of this in earlier chapters.e dimensionality of a plot may be one of the following:• Two dimension (2D): e data appear in a plane. For example, a scat-ter plot with point markers appearing in a plane or a curve g(x, y) = 0 represented by a line in the x, y plane.• ree dimension (3D): e data appear in 3D space. e representa-tion may illustrate points in a 3D space (a scatter plot), a line usually dened parametrically, or in a surface; for example: z = f(x, y).* http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informatics_(academic_eld).
366 ◾ Appendix DD.2 PLOTS CONSIDERED IN THIS APPENDIXUsing Chimera, a plot can be displayed in a variety of settings:• e script can work with the matplotlib module to generate a plot that will be displayed in a separate window that is produced by rou-tines within matplotlib.• e script can work with the matplotlib module to generate a plot that will be displayed within a canvas widget that is part of a mode-less dialog.• e script can use the Solids and Surfaces classes (see AppendixB) to generate a plot that is shown within the Chimera window. is last possibility is particularly suitable for 3D plots because we can use the display facilities of Chimera to rotate and position a complex plot when we want to draw attention to some specic features of the plot. Ticks should have labels that give the reader the ability to associate a tick with a numerical value specifying its position on the axis. To avoid visual clutter, we can provide labels for a subset of the ticks with the intention that the unlabeled tick positions can be inferred. The number of ticks typically varies from one application to the next and should be determined by the user.e function tickList(minVal, maxVal, numTicks) will com-pute a list of ticks for graphs that have plotted values in the range [minVal,


===== [90/99] chapter-90.html =====

380 ◾ Appendix DmaxVal]. e number of ticks is specied by numTicks. e function can be imported by using the statement:from StructBio.Utilities.miscFunctions import tickListe objectives of the function can be stated as follows: 1. e position of the rst tick should be less than or equal to minVal. 2. e position of the last tick should be greater than or equal to maxVal. is may require a slight increase in the number of ticks. 3. e interval length between two consecutive ticks should be speci-ed by some simple number having, for example, one or two signi-cant digits. 4. e rst tick should have a rounded precision that is the same as the interval length.e test program in the le Script D_08 TickList test.py gives a printout for various test cases that illustrate these objectives. Here is a copy of the tickList script:import numpy as np# Function to provide a list of tick values:def tickList(minVal, maxVal, numTicks): incr = (maxVal - minVal)/numTicks incrRounded = round(incr, -int(np.floor(np.log10(incr)))) # The first tick is equal to minVal and is rounded with # the same precision as incr. # If this rounding causes firstTick to be more than the # minVal, then we decrease firstTick by an amount equal # to the rounded version of incr. firstTick = round(minVal, -int(np.floor(np.log10(incr)))) if firstTick > minVal: firstTick = round(minVal - incrRounded, -int(np.floor(np.log10(incr)))) t_L = [] nextTick = firstTick while nextTick < maxVal + incrRounded: # Zero if sufficiently close to0.0: if abs(nextTick) < incr * 0.00001: nextTick = 0.0 t_L.append(nextTick) nextTick += incrRounded 
Appendix D ◾ 381 # We now have the correct tick values but the internal # representation may introduce trailing 9's or a result # that is just slightly too large. # So, we use formatting to do "final roundup". # Calculate number of digits in a large number. sig = int(np.ceil(np.log10(max(abs(minVal), abs(maxVal))))) frmt = '%.3g' # Display 3 significant digits, # but if a number is larger than 999 display them all. if sig > 3: frmt = '%.' + str(sig) +'g' t_L = ['%s' % float(frmt % x) for x in t_L] # Back to floats: t_L = [float(c) for c in t_L] return t_Le rst two lines of the script calculate a rounded version of incr, which is (maxVal - minVal)/numTicks the exact value for the inter-val between consecutive ticks. Note that rounding is done using round (i n c r, -i n t(f l o o r(l o g 10(i n c r)))) so that the third point in the objec-tive list is satised. e next three statements deal with the rst point. e ensuing loop will generate the list of ticks as required, but they may not be suitable for display. One must realize that further computation using results of the round function does not necessarily produce a nal result that has an exact internal oating point representation. To appreciate this, consider the following interactions with the Python Shell:>>> round (5.616, 2)5.62>>> 1.1 + round (5.616, 2)6.720000000000001 # The result is not 6.72!>>> round (5.616, 2)5.62>>> 6.72 - round (5.616, 2)1.0999999999999996 # The result is not 1.1.To get around this small dilemma, we put the tick list through a nal processing step that involves the conversion of the tick values to output strings that provide a more concise version of the tick labels.If you run the Script D_08 TickList test.py program, you can see how it computes tick lists for various number ranges that have end points of varying magnitudes.As a nal note, you might wish to look at Script D_09 x y plot with GUI.py. Running the script will produce the dialog shown in FigureD.8. e 
382 ◾ Appendix Dscript was written to demonstrate a Tkinter plus matplotlib cooperation that is more complicated than that seen in Script D_06. e script is 161 lines long and will not be reproduced on these pages. Some points of interest:• Note the use of a CurvePlotBundle class that is used to carry the data values along with the plot color and legend entry.• e script shows a mainline test program that generates data for three curves but the script has been written to work with a larger number of curve plot bundles.• e computation of ticks for any combination of curves to be displayed is done by computing the overall maximum and mini-mum values for the data followed by a call to the tickList function.D.11 DATA STRUCTURE FOR COLORED SURFACESPython scripts that work with 3D surface plots may generate the data for these plots, which are subsequently displayed within a GUI. To facilitate the transfer of surface information between scripts that generate surface data and scripts that display surface data, it would be expedient to have a self-contained data structure capable of storing the coordinates of points on the surface and the associated color information. Storage of this data will be done using the SurfacePlotBundle class that can be imported using the statement:from StructBio.Plots.surfacePlotBundle import SurfacePlotBundleFIGURE D.8 GUI for multiple curve plots.
Appendix D ◾ 383In addition to color and transparency information, the class has attributes that track the six minimum and maximum values for the x, y, and z components of the coordinates of the points in the surface. ese are useful as input for various calculations done by plot routines. Here is the class denition, initialization, and subsequent comments:class SurfacePlotBundle(object): def __init__(self, x_A, y_A, z_A, color = np.array ((.5, .5, .5)), transparency = 1.0, surfaceName = "Surface Plot"):e rst three parameters are for three arrays each with the same shape. e entries dened by x_A[i,j], y_A[i,j], and z_A[i,j] dene a 3D point p (i, j) = (x, y, z) on the surface, where x = x_A[i,j], y= y_A[i,j], and z = z_A[i,j]. e colors and transparency parameters have default values as shown. If the default values are not used, here are the rules:• Color:• e variable color can be a three component array (this color applied to the entire surface) or it can be an array with shape (m, n, 3), where z_A.shape is (m, n).• e entry c o l o r[i,j,:] denes the rgb surface color at point p(i, j).• Transparency:• e variable transparency can be a simple oat in the interval [0.0, 1.0], or it can be an array with the same shape as z_A.Note that the x_A, y_A, and z_A arrays have the same meaning as the arrays used as input arguments for the plot_surface function in mat-plotlib. e color and transparency parameters allow a simple choice: use one value for the entire surface or use array structures that dene the color and transparency for each discrete point dening the surface.e collection of points dened by the x_A, y_A, and z_A arrays will be used to construct the surface for subsequent display. For us, the sur-face will be constructed by using the addPolygon function in the Solids class. e typical polygon has four points: p(i, j), p(i, j + 1), p(i + 1, j + 1), and p(i + 1, j).
384 ◾ Appendix DTo facilitate the transfer of a SurfacePlotBundle object from one application to the next, we make use of Python’s ability to “pickle” a class object. Technically, this is Python’s mechanism for object serialization.* Itgives us an easy strategy for saving and recovering a class object with-out the need for carefully designed I/O (input/output) formats that would otherwise be required to save all the dierent components of the surface plot bundle.e script in the le Script D_10 Saddle surface plot bun-dle build.py gives an example of code that is used to build a surface plot bundle. In the most general case, the values for the x_A, y_A, and z_A arrays are dened parametrically. Since we are dening a surface (characterized as a continuum having two degrees of freedom), the parametric denition of the surface will require two independent vari-ables, call them u and v. Consequently, to dene a surface plot bundle, we go through the following steps:• Dene m and n specifying the resolution of the surface (the higher, the smoother, but at the expense of more execution time and a larger pickle le).• Dene uMin, uMax, vMin, and vMax specifying the rectangular domain of the independent variables u and v.• Dene the surface name (this should be a character string that is reasonably short in case the string is used within a Label widget of a GUI).• Dene the default color as an RGB numpy array of shape (3,) with each array entry a oating point value in the range [0.0, 1.0] or build a more complicated array to specify color for all the points dening the surface.• Dene the transparency as a oating point number in the range [0.0, 1.0] or build an array to specify transparency for each point dening the surface.• Dene the functions x(u, v), y(u, v), and z(u, v) with values stored in the x_A, y_A, and z_A arrays. By doing this, we are really dening a mapping function that takes (u, v) in a 2D space to a point (x, y, z) * See: http://docs.python.org/3/library/pickle.html. Be sure to read the cautionary note about unpickling data from an unknown source.


===== [91/99] chapter-91.html =====

Appendix D ◾ 385in 3D space. ese points will describe a surface because the (u, v) pairs are taken from a 2D continuum. If necessary, you may get z as a function of u and v by dening z in terms of x and y aer they have been dened in terms of u and v.Note: An application using the SurfacePlotBundle class can redene the surfaceName attribute of an object.is provides all the data needed to build the surface plot bundle, which can then be passed to the plotting routines. 386 ◾ Appendix DWhy use Chimera to display surface plots?• Mouse drags give you rapid zoom and rotation operations allowing the user to position the surface in the most favorable position.• You can exercise precise control of the scene. By using the menu invocation Tools… Viewing Controls… Eects, you can launch the Viewing dialog with the Effects tab selected. is allows control of depth cueing, transparency, and graphics quality. Incidentally, the quality of graphs is greatly improved by using a high subdivi-sion value (say 18.0) and be sure to have the multisample checkbox checked to get better results with antialiasing. Viewing eects such as silhouettes and shadows are possible but of more questionable value for this application.• e Lighting tab in the Viewing dialog gives full control over the light sources for the scene including control over sharpness, reflectivity. Note the selection for Intensity and Shininess.• By using the Camera tab of the Viewing dialog, you can con-trol the projection functionality. It is likely that you will have the projection choice set to perspective. is gives you the most realistic view of the surface set in a 3D scene. Setting the projection choice to orthographic eliminates perspective but it is a useful projection when you need to line up the surface with grid lines.More information about the viewing tool can be found at http://www .cgl.ucsf.edu/chimera/docs/UsersGuide/sideview.html.Running the script in the le Script D_10 Saddle surface plot bundle build.py will demonstrate these points (see Figure D.9). e goals of the script are as follows: 1. 395Appendix EDynamic ProgrammingE.1 MOTIVATIONDynamic programming is an algorithm originally developed by Richard Bellman in the early 1950s. At that time, he was working on applied prob-lems dealing with multistage decision processes. He decided to use the term Dynamic Programming instead of multistage decision processes as a name for the algorithm that we are about to describe in this chapter. e multistage decision nature of the algorithm will eventually become clear.For bioinformatics in general, dynamic programming is undoubtedly the most useful paradigm for the design of algorithms. Most students will likely have covered this topic in an earlier year of their undergraduate pro-gram, but it is so important that we cover it once again in detail. Dynamic programming is presented at an abstract level to present the general steps involved with the strategy.E.2 INTRODUCTIONDynamic programs solve optimization problems. e overall strategy underlying the algorithm is that the given problem can be solved by rst solving smaller subproblems that have the same structure. e following two features characterize these subproblems: 1. Unlike the divide-and-conquer algorithm, which relies on subprob-lems being independent, dynamic programming handles problems that have overlapping subproblems.
396 ◾ Appendix E 2. We can apply a recursive strategy: We nd an optimal solution of each subproblem by rst nding optimal solutions for its contained subproblems.E.3 DYNAMIC PROGRAMMING EXAMPLE: THE AL GORE RHYTHM FOR GIVING TALKSWe start with a simple dynamic programming example. e bioinformat-ics content is nonexistent but it is simple enough to illustrate the main ideas underlying the strategy of dynamic programming.E.3.1 Problem StatementYou have been hired as a consultant to help Al Gore promote his campaign against global warming. He has already decided to tour various cities in the United States to lecture on this subject. e tour will take place over n consec-utive days. For each of these days, he has prescheduled each meeting site and he knows exactly how many people will show up for each site. We will assume that the ith day has a meeting with u(i) attendees. Although he would like to attend all these meetings, the demands of his campaign have weakened him to such an extent that he cannot attend meetings on two consecutive days and so some meetings must be skipped. (When he does not attend a meeting, there will be a free showing of the movie An Inconvenient Truth.)Design an algorithm that will select the days when Gore will meet the attendees with the objective that, for the entire tour, he will meet the max-imum number of these people. Rephrasing the meeting constraint: If he meets attendees on day i − 1, then he cannot meet attendees on day i. We will assume there is no meeting planned on the day before day 1.E.3.2 Terminology: Congurations and ScoresWe should always distinguish two aspects of optimization problems that are amenable to a dynamic programming solution. We ultimately want some type of conguration that provides an answer to the problem. In this problem, a conguration is simply some selection of days chosen from the available n days. e denition of a conguration will change from one problem to the next. e other aspect of the optimization problem is that we can assign a score to each conguration. In this problem, the score for a conguration will be the sum of all u(i) values such that i ranges across the selected days that make up the conguration.With this terminology in place, we can see that the solution of the optimization problem is the derivation of a conguration that provides 
Appendix E ◾ 397 the optimal score. For this problem, optimal means maximal. For other problems, we might want to nd the conguration with the minimal score.E.3.3 Analysis of Our Given ProblemAs stated earlier, we nd an optimal solution of our given problem by rst nding an optimal solution for various subproblems. We consider the fol-lowing denition of a subproblem: Find the conguration that gives the optimal score for a problem, which is limited to the rst i days of the tour. We let this optimal score be represented by the function S(i). Now we need an analysis of the situation:For day i, Gore either meets attendees or he does not.• If he does attend the meeting, then for day i the sum S(i) includes u(i), day i − 1 is skipped and moreover, the selection from the rst i − 2 days must be an optimal selection. If it was not optimal, then we could replace this nonoptimal conguration of days with a better selection having a larger sum of attendees. is sum plus the value of u(i) for day i would lead to a larger score for S(i) in contradiction of the fact that S(i) is already assumed to be optimal. So, Gore meeting attendees on day i implies S(i) = u(i) + S(i − 2), where S(i − 2) is the optimal score for the rst i − 2 days.• If Gore does not meet attendees on day i then, nothing has changed since the previous day. In other words: u(i) is not part of S(i), and S(i)= S(i − 1).Since we want to choose the scenario that yields the larger score S(i), we simply insist that S(i) is the maximum of these two possibilities, in other words: Si ui Si Si{}() () ()()=+−−max2,1 Pseudocode for the algorithm could be written as follows:S[0] : = 0; S[1] : = u[1];for i : = 2 to n do if (S[i-1] < S[i-2] + u[i]) then S[i] : = S[i-2] + u[i]; else S[i] : = S[i-1];return S[n];
398 ◾ Appendix ENote that the value of S(n) will be the best score that is achievable for the entire n days. With a bit of thought you should also realize that com-putation of the S(i) values must be completed for all i = 1, 2, …, n before we can attempt to derive the conguration that gives the solution of the prob-lem. In fact, we must inspect both S(n) and S(n − 1) before discovering whether Gore will attend the meeting on day n. Once that is established, we can then determine whether the meeting on day n − 1 is skipped or attended, then we consider S(n − 2), and so on. e pseudocode for this algorithm is as follows:i : = n;while i > 0 if (S[i-1] <> S[i]) then write(“Day” i “chosen”); i : = i – 2; else i : = i – 1;is completes the solution of the given problem. Now, let us extract from this solution some procedural steps that can be applied to all dynamic programming solutions.e rst step was the most critical because it identied the subproblems that were to be solved to get the solution of the full problem. is means that the optimal conguration for the given problem will be constructed from the optimal congurations of subproblems. ese subproblems will have optimal congurations that are in some sense smaller. e challenge of designing a dynamic programming algorithm will be to understand how these smaller optimal congurations relate to larger optimal congu-rations and eventually to the largest optimal conguration that we ulti-mately need. However, if you look back at the analysis of the problem, you will see that this relation is formulated in terms of the scores derived from the optimal congurations. is produces the recursion that is at the heart of the solution.In general, the score value in the recursion is dependent on vari-ous parameters that characterize the extent of the subproblems. In our givenproblem, S depended only on i. In more complex dynamic program-ming problems, there may be two (perhaps even three) parameters that act as independent variables for the score function. We will see that it is oen advantageous to consider the values of the scoring function to be stored in an array that has as many dimensions as there are parameters.
Appendix E ◾ 399 Using this array, we can visualize the computation of the score function recurrence by stipulating where the initial values are stored and the order of computation when lling the cells of the array. e analysis of the prob-lem will also specify the array cell containing the optimal score for the given problem. Finally, the analysis of the problem will dictate how the solution conguration is recovered. is nal step is usually referred to as the traceback or solution recovery step.Be sure to understand the signicance of the word back in the traceback step. e optimal scores for the subproblems are typically generated by having one or more parameters of the scoring function progressing through values in a direction that we characterize as the forward direction. For example, in the Al Gore problem, we have the index i going from 1 to n when the score values are evaluated. Aer all these computations are completed, we recover the conguration for the optimal solution by reversing direction and letting the parameter (or parameters) progress through values in the opposite direction. In our given problem, i progresses from n down to 1 in the traceback part of the algorithm.For students who are just learning about dynamic programming, it is crucial to stress that the formulation of a dynamic programming algorithm should deal rst with the scoring function, and then later, with the derivation of the conguration. When faced with a new and complicated dynamic programming problem, inexperienced students will sometimes attempt to derive a nal conguration much too soon—immediately trying to understand how the conguration is computed while treating the scoring function as if it is an evaluation to be done aer congurations are somehow calculated. is oen leads to confu-sion and failure.E.4 RECIPE FOR DYNAMIC PROGRAMMINGTo improve the chances of success when designing a dynamic program-ming algorithm, here is a summary of the steps that are usually taken: 1. Identify the subproblems and provide a supporting analysis that produces a recursion for the evaluation of the scoring function. We need to specify how the optimal score of a subproblem contributes to the optimal score of a larger subproblem. 2. Provide details that stipulate how the score values are evaluated.


===== [94/99] chapter-94.html =====

400 ◾ Appendix E Typically, we store the optimal scores of the subproblems in an array (possibly a big, multidimensional array). We need to a. Specify the matrix dimensions. b. Describe how the value in a cell of the array depends on the values of other cells in the array (thus specifying the order of computation). c. Specify where the nal optimal score for the full problem will be found in the array. 3. Set values for the base cases. ere will be cells that require initial values because the recursion equation alone cannot dene all the score values in the array. 4. Recover the solution conguration. a. Keep track of the congurations that provided the optimal values. b. Use a traceback strategy to determine the full conguration that gives the optimal value for the full problem.E.5 LONGEST COMMON SUBSEQUENCENow that we have some dynamic programming methodology estab-lished, we apply it to a problem with some extra complexity. e lon-gest common subsequence (LCS) problem usually arises from methods that need to compare strings of nucleotides. e solution of the problem serves to provide a measure of the amount of similarity between two nucleotide sequences. We start with some abstractions that dene the problem.Denition: A subsequence of a string X is a string that can be obtained by extracting some or all of the characters from X while still maintaining their sequence order.For example, if X = “supercalifragilisticexpialidocious”, then the characters T = “cialis” would be a subsequence. ere are approximately 16 billion subsequences that can be extracted from X.e reader should not confuse this with the notion of a substring, which is a more specialized extraction of characters: A substring is subsequence of consecutive characters that are extracted from X.
Appendix E ◾ 401 E.5.1 Problem StatementWe are given two strings: X = x1, x2, …, xm and Y = y1, y2, …, yn. e prob-lem is to nd the longest string Z that is a subsequence of both X and Y.For example, supposeX = “supercalifragilisticexpialidocious” andY = “supercalafajalistickespeealadojus”en the LCS of X and Y is Z = “supercalfalisticepaldous”.*A small issue of concern is the presentation of strings X, Y, and Z so that a reader can easily verify that Z is indeed the LCS of X and Y. One could write Y below X and then draw lines between the matching characters that produce Z. is is somewhat cumbersome and is typically not done. e usual technique is to insert dash characters into both X and Y strings so that they have the same length and written out so that matching charac-ters are in the same column. For example,X: supercalifragilistic-expi-alidociousY: supercalaf-ajalistickespeealado—j usZ: supercal f a listic e p al do usCharacters that produce the LCS have been rendered in a bold font.e LCS is not necessarily unique. For example, let X = “ATCTGAT”, Y = “TGCATA”. en both Z = “TCTA” and Z = “TGAT” are legitimate as an LCS. Naturally, both possibilities have the same optimal string length.E.5.2 PrexesWe compute the LCS using dynamic programming. Our strategy is to derive the LCS of the given strings X and Y, by rst solving various sub-problems. Each subproblem involves the computation of an LCS for some pair of prexes of X and Y.Denition: e ith prex of a string X, denoted by X(i), will be the sub-string of X made up from the rst i characters of X.* ere is some history associated with this particular Y string. It is related to a legal action brought against e Walt Disney Company. See: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercalifragi listicexpialidocious. Incidentally, the string Y is agged by Microso Word as a misspelling, but X is accepted.
402 ◾ Appendix EWe use lower case subscripted letters to denote the characters in X. Formally, we denote the full string X as: 12=…Xxxxm. en, the prex 12()=…Xi xx xi. Assuming X has m characters, we have X = X(m).Given input strings 12=…Xxxxm and 12=…Yyyyn, our subproblem will be to nd the LCS of X(i) and Y(j). Note that the subproblem is param-eterized using two indexes i and j with im≤≤1 and jn≤≤1. e score value for such a subproblem, denoted by S[i,j], is the length of the LCS of the prexes. e conguration for this subproblem is the LCS itself.Having dened this notation and the signicance of the score function, we are about half way through the rst step of our recipe for dynamic pro-gramming introduced in Section E.4. It is now necessary to provide the supporting analysis that leads to a recursion dening S[i,j]. Since prexes are essentially dened by their last position, we will be interested in the following clever observations about the last character of an LCS.E.5.3 Relations among SubproblemsSuppose we consider 12()=…Xi xx xi to be a prex of X and 12()=…Yj yy yj to be a prex of Y. Let 12()=…Zk zz zk be the LCS of X(i) and Y(j). ere are two possibilities for the last characters of X(i) and Y(j). Either xyij= or xyij≠. en the following observations can be made: 1. If xyij=, then zxyki j== and Z(k − 1) is an LCS of X(i − 1) and Y(j − 1). 2. If xyij≠, then we have the negation of (zk = xi and zk = yj). is means that zxki≠ or zykj≠. is gives us two subcases: a. zxki≠⇒ Z(k) is an LCS of X(i − 1) and Y(j). b. zykj≠⇒ Z(k) is an LCS of X(i) and Y(j − 1).Let us consider Case 1: If zxki≠, then we could simply append xi to Z(k) to obtain an LCS of X(i) and Y(j) with longer length than k, contradicting the assumption that Z(k) was the longest common subsequence. So, zk= xi=yj. What about Z(k − 1), the characters of Z(k) before zk? We know that they are a common subsequence of X(i − 1) and Y(j − 1), but can we say that Z(k − 1) is the optimal common subsequence of X(i − 1) and Y(j− 1)? In other words, has our claim that zk = xi = yj somehow compromised Z(k − 1) and it is not optimal? No. If there was an LCS W of X(i − 1) and Y(j − 1) that had k or more characters, then we would simply append xi to it to get an LCS of X(i) 
Appendix E ◾ 403 and Y(j) longer than Z(k) and this would be a contradiction. Consequently, we have the fact that Z(k − 1) is an LCS of X(i − 1) and Y(j − 1).Now consider Case 2(a): If zxki≠, then Z (k) must be a common subsequence of X(i − 1) and Y(j). Using reasoning similar to that of Case 1, we see that Z(k) must be the longest common subsequence of X(i − 1) and Y(j). If there was a longer common subsequence of X(i − 1) and Y(j), (say W with length greater than that of Z(k)) then, since W is also a subsequence of both X(i) and Y(j), we would have a common subsequence of X(i) and Y(j) longer than Z, and so derive a contradiction.Reasoning for Case 2(b) is symmetric to that of Case 2(a).E.5.4 Recurrence for the Longest Common Subsequencee dierent cases just described have implications that dene the score function S[i, j]. Recall that S[i, j] is the length of the LCS of X(i) and Y(j), in other words, it is the length of Z(k). 1. Case 1: e LCS of X(i) and Y(j) is the LCS of X(i − 1) and Y(j − 1) extended by one character. is implies that S[i, j] = S[i − 1, j − 1] +1. 2. Case 2(a): e LCS of X(i − 1) and Y(j) is the same as the LCS of X(i) and Y(j). is implies that S[i, j] = S[i − 1, j]. A similar analysis for Case 2(b) gives us S[i, j] = S[i, j − 1].We want to extract the longest common sequence and this means that for Case 2 our computation for S[i, j] will be evaluated by selecting the maximum of the two options described. Consequently, the main recur-rence is ==Si,jSi–,j–+xySi–jSi,j–1 x≠y[][1 1] 1ifmax{ [1,],[ ]} ifijij (E.1)is completes Step 1 of our recipe for dynamic programming. For Step2, we describe the data structure that retains the scores and the computa-tional procedure to evaluate them. e S[i, j] scores will be stored in an n by m array. Considering the recursion we see that score S[i, j] held in cell [i, j] will depend on score values stored in cells [i − 1, j − 1], [i − 1, j], and [i, j − 1]. In other words, S[i, j] depends on score entries in a previous row or column. Consequently, it is reasonable to have an order of evaluation that lls in the matrix row by row, starting with the rst row. e very last 
404 ◾ Appendix Ecomputation will be that of S[m, n], which represents the length of the LCS for the full strings X and Y. is completes Step 2.Step 3 handles the necessary initialization needed to begin the evalu-ation of the recursion. e recursion formula claims that entries in row i=1 would require prior values to be set up in row i = 0. is can be done by supplying an extra row 0 and column 0 with all entries initialized to 0. ese values are independent of the characters in the given sequences. We are really saying that the length of LCS is 0 when one or more of the strings is null (zero length).Step 4 deals with recovery of the solution conguration. Filling the score matrix will only compute the length of the LCS. To get the actual LCS, we have to keep track of the cell that is responsible for producing the S[i, j] value. For each [i, j] cell we keep a back-pointer D[i, j] to one of [i − 1, j − 1], [i − 1, j], or [i, j − 1], depending on which cell was used to determine S[i, j]. We use D for Direction and give each direction a symbol that would be represented in the program using some type of encoding: [, ]up-leftif, 1,11up if ,1,left if ,,1[][][] [][] []==−−+=−=−Di jSijSijSijSijSijSij (E.2)is completes all four steps of the recipe.Here is a pseudocode version of the algorithm://Base casesfor i : = 0 to m do S[i,0] : = 0;for j : = 0 to n do S[0,j] : = 0;//Filling the S[] and D[] matricesfor i : = 1 to m do for j : = 1 to n do if (x[i] = = y[j]) then S[i,j] : = S[i-1, j–1] + 1; D[i,j] : = “up-left”; else S[i,j] : = S[i-1,j]; D[i,j] : = “up”; if (S[i,j-1] > S[i-1,j]) then S[i,j] : = S[i,j-1]; D[i,j] : = “left”;return S[m,n];

Appendix E ◾ 405 Figure E.1 presents an example of the score matrix with the direction matrix superimposed. e bottom rightmost cell gives the length of the LCS when the input strings are X = “LOGARITHM” and Y =“ALGORITHM”.To use the back-pointers, we simply start in the nal location con-taining the highest score and then follow the pointers. is produces a path that denes the LCS. All other back pointers are ignored. Figure E.2 shows the path extraction. e main idea behind the path extraction is to go through the optimal congurations and while doing so keep track of the occurrences when an up-le pointer is used. e origin of the pointer will be a cell that is in a row and column such that xi and yj are the same. Considering our previous analysis, this is the situation that causes a character to be appended to the LCS. e code has been written so that the LCS is generated in reverse order. It is then reversed in the nal step.e pseudocode to do this traceback would be as follows:row : = m; col : = n; lcs : = "";while (row > 0 and col > 0) do if (D[row,col] = = “upleft”) then //x[row] = y[col] lcs : = lcs.x[row]; row : = row-1; col : = col-1; else if (D[row,col] = “up”) then row : = row-1; else if (D[row,col] = “left”) then col : = col-1;reverse lcs;return lcs;is completes our introduction to dynamic programming. 406 ◾ Appendix EE.6 EXERCISES 1. Assume you have an n-by-n checkerboard. Each square of the check-erboard is labeled with a coordinate pair so that the square in the ith row and jth column is labeled with the pair (i, j). You must move a checker from the top-le corner square (1, 1) to the bottom-right corner square (n, n) using at most 2(n − 1) moves. Legal moves are as follows: a. Move of the checker down one square. b. Move of the checker right one square. c. Diagonal move of the checker down one square and to the right one square.In this problem, you will assume that on square (1, 1) you have a purse with zero dollars and this value may go up or down as you make moves on the checkerboard according to the following payo scheme that dictates changes to the sum in your purse: a. A down move that leaves square (i, j) changes your sum by dij dollars. b. A right move that leaves square (i, j) changes your sum by rij dollars. c. A diagonal move that leaves square (i, j) changes your sum by gij dollars.A0000000000000000101010101011111111111112222222222222221222333331222344441222345551222345661222345670LOGARITHMLGORITHMFIGURE E.2 Producing the traceback path.
Appendix E ◾ 407 You are given all the dij, rij, and gij values as input data. ese values may be negative, zero, or positive so it may be possible to have a nega-tive sum in your purse at any time. You want to nd a path from (1,1) to (n, n) using moves that get as much money as possible, or to end up with the least negative value in your purse, depending on the data. Provide an ecient algorithm based on dynamic programming. Your answer should follow the four steps recipe discussed in this chapter. 2. Suppose you are given a sequence of real numbers x1, x2, x3,…, xn. Use a dynamic programming strategy to nd a sequence of consecutive numbers with the maximum sum.* In other words, we want to nd i and j such that the following sum is maximized: xkkij∑= If all the numbers are positive, we simply set i = 1 and j = n. To make the problem nontrivial we assume that the sequence of real numbers contains both positive and negative numbers. e algorithm should have execution time n()Θ. Your answer should follow the four steps recipe discussed in this chapter. You should supply a short explana-tion that justies the formulation of the recursion. 3. Repeat Exercise 1 subject to the following conditions: a. As before, you are working with an n-by-n checkerboard and you are only allowed to make the legal moves described by Exercise 1. b. e input data is the same as that described by Exercise 1 and the rules for payo are the same. c. You are allowed to start in any square with a purse of zero dollars. d. You may nish in any square as long as you follow the rules for legal moves. e. You should assume that at least one of the dij, rij, and gij values is positive.As before, you want to nd a path that will give you the most benet.* In algorithm design courses, this problem is usually known as Bentley’s Problem.
